# Paykit v0.2 specification

**Status.** Draft, not frozen. The wire format and Section 18 refer to this paragraph for what `paykit-lib` implements. The reference prototype implements the spec but the rules this revision adds, which Section 18 lists ("Where the reference prototype differs"); `paykit-lib`, with `pubky-switch-core` for the Switch's rules, implements its format rules except the Private Payment List publication records (wire 5.8), the executor's durable failed-attempt evidence and reservation-release rules, ledger repair's complete usage authority, confirmation, refusals and write (Section 8.3), the outbound `paykit_contacts` requirements for extension messages, Allowance proposals and answers and recovery asks, and the remaining deleted-record rules (Section 18), whose code, checks and vectors are to come. The format of the Allowance proposal's `allower_generation` member (wire 4.15) is implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`, and the stored proposal vectors carry it. The repair merge (`merge_for_repair`, Section 8.3) is implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`, with no vector yet. Allowance usage counting from already validated attempt evidence, `ledger_handoff` signature and record-binding checks, and its grant-history and later-write checks are implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`. Their callers remain responsible for obtaining, validating, binding and persisting the evidence; these pure checks do not establish complete usage history or payment authority. Settlement marks and their `lost_to_delete` boundary formats, pure merge and later-write checks, and the selling-scope requirements of `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts` are implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`. Authenticated creation, continuity, deletion publication, cursor holds, settlement-credit enforcement and their host warnings remain runtime work. `paykit-lib`'s public vector tests on this repository's `v0.2` branch reproduce the stored values as that branch holds them, and the Switch checks run against that branch's actual dependency (Section 18 names the Paykit vector command); these results do not establish conformance to every contract at the normative baseline. The wire format (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md`) is INCOMPLETE for protected-record creation, put-back and incarnation verification (Section 6; wire Section 5); its implemented formats have test vectors; an independent implementation, shared separately and written from the wire format alone, hasn't run this revision's vectors, which those crates' public vector tests generate or check. The spec is frozen once the team has decided the open questions and the team decisions (Section 17), and an independent human crypto review and the measurements on real infrastructure are done (Section 18).

**Release requirements.** Every released Paykit v0.2 implementation must enforce this specification's record rules, including `lost_to_delete`, its least permissive states and confirmation continuity (Section 6). A release requires the corresponding format validation, runtime behavior and positive and rejection vectors, including client support that holds the service cursor acknowledgment until a protective write has applied and authenticated record-incarnation evidence that distinguishes true absence from a retained delete at publication and survives later writes; these requirements apply from the first release. The pinned Switch specifies strict zero and permanent records (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 10.3 and 10.3.1). These do not establish the history of a Paykit record for a later reader. The protected-family requirements below refer to the origin and lineage contracts of Switch revision `f0bbddd1`. They still require a mapping on the current Switch, Paykit readers and durable publication behavior. First creation, exact-copy put-back and other dependent positive paths remain withheld until those requirements and generated cases are integrated. The incomplete Paykit contract and runtime support remain release blockers.

**Built on the Pubky Switch.** Paykit v0.2 is a protocol on the Pubky Switch, an inbox and coordination service for Pubky identities specified on its own in [`spec/spec.md`](https://github.com/pubky/pubky-switch/blob/5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61/spec/spec.md) ("the Pubky Switch spec") and [`spec/wire-format.md`](https://github.com/pubky/pubky-switch/blob/5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61/spec/wire-format.md) ("the Switch wire format") of the pubky-switch repository, at commit `5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61`. The Switch gives Paykit its identities and `identity.json`, app certificates and records, receive keys, Shared Data Keys and key bundles, the sealed and signed message, admission, claims and shared records, push, export and import, and identity management. This spec defines what Paykit adds on top: its scopes and the switch permissions each needs, its message kinds, its collections and their policies, the payment flows, Allowances, recurring requests, labelled selling scopes and the retention of payment evidence. Where Paykit relies on a rule of the Switch, it cites it as "Pubky Switch spec, Section N" or "Switch wire N". Switch citations use this commit unless they explicitly name another revision. Throughout this specification and its accompanying documents, the generic protected-origin, lineage and retention contracts, including their proof formats in Switch wire 6.9, refer to Switch commit `f0bbddd15c7ecb1ec670f263f36dd4f2373dba8f`. The current Switch has no such contracts. Paykit paths that require them remain unavailable pending a mapping on the current Switch and its implementation. A later Switch change applies only once a Paykit specification revision names it here. This is the normative Switch baseline; it adopts the contracts described below without claiming that every specified operation is implemented by the service, client or Paykit. A dependency update must use this baseline and implement the applicable Paykit rules; updating the dependency alone establishes no conformance. The remaining positive-path holds in Sections 6 and 8.4.1 still apply. Stored Paykit vectors retain their stated coverage; this adoption adds no vector or executed conformance result.

**Public-payment runtime status.** The Paykit SDK on `v0.2` implements the publication, renewal and capability listing of an app's record in Section 4 and wire 3.2, and a payer's read of public endpoints, with positive and failure-path tests. The outbound-authorization rules in Sections 3.1 to 3.4 and the offline-payment recording behavior in Section 8.4 are requirements for the forthcoming SDK implementation and its positive and failure-path tests; this specification does not claim that the current SDK implements them.

**Allowance acceptance runtime status.** Allowance acceptance is unavailable in this revision (Section 8.3.1, specified with its implementation), and the Paykit SDK on `v0.2` refuses it.

**Extension runtime status.** Section 3.3's atomic extension intake and eviction rely on the adopted generic contract in Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 11: retiring stored duplicates of a completed receive, handling later duplicates and an absent processed pair without discarding an uncertain effect, and consuming an incoming entry unprocessed while atomically committing the protocol's local writes. Unprocessed consumption removes a stored input and its retry state, adds no processed pair, performs no remote operation, and preserves duties already owed. The pinned client exposes these outcomes; the corresponding Paykit implementation and failure-path tests remain required. The bounds and recovery requirements below are not relaxed by the dependency update.

**Admission-wait runtime status.** The protocol-admission wait in Sections 3.3, 8.1, 8.3 and 9 uses the adopted generic client contract in Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 11: custody of an unadmitted authenticated input, atomic admission to the existing bounded waiting pool, a distinct durable notice when that custody is dropped, and the corresponding retry, duplicate-retirement and restore rules. The pinned client exposes the admission-wait outcome and custody notices. Paykit must integrate that contract and its positive and failure-path tests before enabling the dependent runtime. Ordinary message bounds remain strict, and independently uncertain effects remain protected.

**What this pin adopts.** The normative baseline includes the following changes from the earlier Switch contract. Reference-client APIs below name the [public client source](https://github.com/pubky/pubky-switch/tree/5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61/crates/pubky-switch-client/src) at that same baseline. These requirements apply together; availability of a client method is not evidence that a Paykit runtime rule is complete.

- **Protocol ownership and lifecycle.** The reference client's `ProtocolSet` owns scope roots, message kinds, collections and local state by protocol. `App::join`, `App::load` and `App::restore` require the registered owners of every own-certificate scope root. A Paykit App may still hold another protocol's scopes. Its SDK lifecycle must accept the host's complete protocol set or an app constructed with it, retain the Paykit owner and the other owners, and apply Paykit's scope and permission checks to Paykit's authority only (Section 3.2; Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.2, 3.3 and 8.3). A Paykit-only convenience path cannot be the sole public lifecycle path for such a certificate.
- **Callbacks and shared observations.** Messages, notices and collection records follow each protocol's durable order. A shared deny-list change or gap waits for every protocol to reach it and changes the shared copy only when every owner has taken it. Failed callbacks remain owed. Claims and records are current authenticated views, not snapshots proving another protocol handled them; writes use their conditions. Current certificate authority, keys, service time and reconcile/provider-loss restrictions apply immediately (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.3). Paykit must not infer cross-protocol order or regain withdrawn authority from an older message.
- **Atomic receive outcomes.** Completion retires eligible stored copies with the processed pair and local effect. Forgetting an absent pair cannot erase an uncertain effect. Unprocessed consumption commits the local writes, pair forgets, input removal and retry-state removal together, with no remote operation or input processed pair. Protocol-admission waiting discards the callback's staged effects and keeps only the unadmitted input or its durable custody-drop notice, under the common 21-day, 64-per-sender and 128-total waiting bounds and fair retry order (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 11). Paykit's ordinary pools, eviction victims, mandatory stops, first receipts and drop marks keep their own rules (Sections 3.3, 8.1, 8.3 and 9).
- **Duties and host events.** The client exposes inspection of pending protocol calls and duty events, durable event ownership and per-protocol acknowledgment. Inspection consumes no duty. Paykit keeps the local consequence before acknowledging its duty, tolerates repeated delivery, and cannot acknowledge another protocol's work. When one event has several owners it remains owed to each until that owner has kept its consequence. An app-wide host acknowledgment is valid only after every affected owner has done so (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2, 8.3, 11 and 12.4).
- **Backups and local storage.** Layered backup and restore preserve each protocol's local state, the carried processed pairs and their kind ownership, bounded waiting custody and owed notices, and outbox holds and origins within the Switch's stated backup coverage. They do not create absent sent-message or unanswered-batch history (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 11 and 12.4). The reference store requires atomic, durable, ordered commits, exclusive namespace ownership and guarded commits across processes; an unknown commit requires resolution from durable state before further action. Paykit uses its qualified protocol namespace and commits its state with the client outcome, without direct writes that bypass ownership or a success result after storage failure.
- **Delivery control.** The client inventories every owed message, including kept-batch and restored work, and exposes per-message holds and terminal withdrawal. Each retained outgoing message has its durable original or reconstructed origin, separate from recipient acceptance, preserved through retry, re-sealing and restore. Neither origin nor a hold release grants Paykit send authority (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11). Every delivery still satisfies Section 8.1's current per-message authorization and deny checks; Section 8.4.1's publication and restore barriers remain required.
- **App-record lifetime.** The client can set a protocol member or renew a record with a lifetime measured from the exact `updated_at` it signs. The earliest protocol expiry and the identity's record-age cap apply; changing Paykit's member preserves other protocols' members and expiry policies. The policy and signed bytes persist before delivery, and an exact retry extends no lifetime. A clock rollback requires verified service time before a new signing (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2; Switch wire 3.2). Section 4 and Paykit wire 3.2 still govern when Paykit publishes, renews or removes its endpoints.
- **Service keys, responses and account lifetimes.** One service-wide key signs responses, with a rollback mark for each account creation. Responses bind the exact received request, including signed-header presence and values, and clients judge authenticated generation as well as key; unsigned public-read headers supply no authority (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 6.1, 6.5 and 12.6; Switch wire 2.1, 7.1, 7.3 and 7.6). Signer requests bind the selected account creation under `spec/account-binding.md`; a signed recovery notice or an unreachable service never authorizes recovery automatically. Applying these contracts does not establish complete account-association or recovery implementation coverage.
- **Record versions and permanent records.** A write naming version 0 holds only where the service holds nothing at the key; a write over a retained delete names that delete, and after a recovery upload a write names the record's current write (`record_current`), not a bare version (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3). A permanent policy with `generation: null` forbids deletion in every generation. A policy naming generation `g` allows creation at zero only in `g` and deletion only by an operation in a generation above `g`. A signed read or covering listing that shows a held live key absent to an authorized reader at its hosting service raises the rollback alarm, except when the newest own identity document's `recovery_from` covers the last generation in which the app saw the key live, or the account has passed the policy's named generation (Section 10.3.1). Paykit's own collections don't use permanent policies at this revision; Section 6's put-back and the positive paths of Sections 8.3.1 and 8.4.1 stay withheld.
- **Held inbox entries.** When the Switch client holds an entry or processes it ahead of its cursor, it saves the entry ID, acceptance time, verifying service key and signed page time from that first observation. It keeps them until the cursor passes the entry. A later signed page in the same sequence that shows a different entry, a different acceptance time or a different kind of item the client reads at that position raises a rollback alarm (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 10.1, and Switch wire 7.4.4).
- **Stopped record jobs.** When a delete stops a queued record write after the recipient's switch has accepted the message, the Switch client reports the delete's record version and signed operation hash. Together they identify the delete after a recovery upload. Both the protocol that owns the outgoing message kind and the protocol that owns the record's collection receive the notice. Each distinct owner acknowledges it independently. The collection owner can read the record and decide whether it still needs a write (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 10.3, 11 and 12.6).
- **Renewing a certificate.** Before the app signs its record under a renewed certificate, each protocol gives its member and its expiry ask for that certificate, and the record is signed once with what each gave, the certificate, members and asks committed together; a member that fails validation is removed and the host told (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2). Paykit gives its member without public endpoints when the new certificate lacks `paykit_request`, and without capabilities when it lacks `paykit_pay`, with no expiry ask when no public endpoints remain (Section 4; wire 3.2).
- **Deliveries of unknown outcome.** When the client gives up a delivery it cannot prove undelivered, it reports `DeliveryUnknown` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11). The message may have reached its recipient. Paykit must never queue a given-up message again, even with its original signed bytes. While the client retains the give-up mark, `queue_stored` does not queue another delivery. A backup omits sent messages and give-up marks, though it carries unacknowledged unknown-delivery duties. On restore the client can therefore accept a copy it had previously given up. Paykit must durably record the possible delivery before acknowledging the duty and reconcile it from a reply, receipt, the shared records or claims the message would change, or contact with the counterparty. Without evidence the outcome stays unknown, including across restart and restore. Paykit must not send a replacement the recipient could act on twice. A genuinely new operation requires separate user authorization that accounts for the possibly delivered effect. The Paykit SDK does not yet handle this duty. It remains with the host, unacknowledged by Paykit. Until Paykit implements these checks, a host that cannot establish which original deliveries are still pending must keep any restored work it cannot verify out of automatic delivery, including SDK calls that can flush it, and reconcile the possible effect (Section 7.2).
- **Record answers.** A record read returns the record with the newest signed head, and a write the switch shows older than the one the app holds is told apart from one an answer current before it overtook; a protocol's check of a newer write also runs when the app has seen nothing of the record yet (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.3 and 10.3). Paykit's checks of Sections 6 and 8 apply unchanged; with nothing seen there is nothing to go back on.
- **Wire references and vectors.** Switch encodings, signatures, account-bound requests, record evidence and export references use the revisions named above. Paykit's stored vector files are unchanged and remain self-contained. New or changed Paykit formats and runtime cases require their implementing library or SDK checks; additional vectors accompany the implementing code before being copied here. Referencing the newer Switch vector set does not assert that this Paykit tree generates or checks every case in it.

**Design rule for implementations.** Private Payment List publication follows the ordering and call costs of Section 8.4.1 (Reserve, then sign and queue). For other actions, each user action needs at most one call to each Pubky Switch involved (the counterparty's, for the message, and the identity's own, for claims and shared state), sent in parallel, before the user sees its result, plus local bookkeeping. Follow-up messages, such as a one-time request's acceptance (Section 7.2 step 2), go in the background. The only other calls allowed are the background and parallel Homeserver calls listed in Section 14, the reads of the deny list and the `block_floor` record that Section 8.1 requires before a payment, a dispatch or an automatic acceptance, and one re-send when the counterparty's inbox answers `stale_key` because a recipient app has just replaced its receive key or the recipient has added an app (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.6 and 8.1). A first message to a new counterparty also reads its directory, writes its conversation entry and may deliver the sender's `identity.json`, in one parallel round before the append (Section 14 and Pubky Switch spec, Section 7).

## Terms

Paykit uses the terms of the Pubky Switch spec (its Terms): identity, signer, app, `identity.json`, scope, Shared Data Key, inbox, feed, claim, shared record, operation and the rest. Paykit's own:

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Paykit App** (app) | An app of the Switch acting for an identity in Paykit: one application with its own keys, certificate and Paykit scopes, at least one. Its certificate may also hold other protocols' scopes (Section 3.2). An app may run on several devices; Paykit treats it as one app (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.1). |
| **Version** | Besides the versions of the Switch, Private Payment Lists have versions of their own (Section 5). |
| **Billing Period** | One period of a recurring request, computed as in Section 8.2. |
| **Executor** | The one app that an Allowance record names, with the app's own signed grant, to pay automatically under that Allowance (Section 8.3). |
| **Paykit Profile** | Optional public display metadata of an identity at `/pub/paykit/profile.json`, with v0.1's fields and their meaning; how it is written is in Section 4. |

## 1. Goals and product restrictions

- **Goals:**
  - Payments between Pubky identities; Pubky is the only identity layer required.
  - About one round trip per action, independent of contact count or history size.
  - Several apps per identity, isolated by scope, sharing only what their scopes allow.
  - Payment-method agnostic: the protocol never interprets rails, addresses, amounts' meaning, or proofs.
  - Feature agnostic: the switch offers generic primitives (claims and shared records). Features such as Allowances are built in apps on those primitives.
  - An identity can move to another provider without the old operator's cooperation.
- **Product restriction:**
  - v0.2 has **no unsolicited first contact by Pubky ID alone**.
  - A sender must be on the recipient's allowlist: a contact, or a conversation the recipient started or invited, for example at a checkout.
  - Strangers can't message without an invite. An identity can hand out invite codes, for example a shop's QR code, and, where the user allows it, strangers can ask to connect (Pubky Switch spec, Section 7). Messaging without either is deferred.

## 2. Components

Paykit runs on the components of the Pubky Switch (Pubky Switch spec, Section 2): the signer, the identity's apps, the Homeserver, the Pubky Switch and the push relay.

- **A Paykit App** keeps its own keys, scopes, local data, outbox and cursor, as every app does, and its payment journal.
- **The Homeserver** also holds the records of Paykit apps with public payment endpoints, and the Paykit Profile (Section 4).

## 3. Scopes and authorization

### 3.1 Scopes

Paykit's scopes are names under the Switch's scope rule (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.3): a name, or a name and a sub-scope. Each Paykit App's certificate lists the Paykit scopes it holds:

| Scope | Allows | Requires |
|---|---|---|
| `paykit_pay` | Act as payer. Send acceptance, rejection, payer cancellation, proof, Private Payment List requests and resend requests. Receive requests, period details, payee cancellations, receipts, Private Payment Lists and resend requests. Read and write claims. Read and write `payer_activity`, `payment_lists` and `block_floor` | `paykit_contacts` |
| `paykit_request` | Act as payee. Send requests, period details, payee cancellations, receipts, Private Payment Lists and resend requests. Receive acceptance, rejection, payer cancellations, proofs, Private Payment List requests and resend requests. Read and write `payee_activity` | `paykit_settlements`, `paykit_contacts` |
| `paykit_request:<label>` | Act as payee under one label: what `paykit_request` allows, with every request sent under the label, the payer's replies received only for that label's requests, and only that label's `payee_activity` records read and written | `paykit_settlements`, `paykit_contacts` |
| `paykit_settlements` | Read and write `settlements`, the marks of the settlements the identity's payee apps have credited (Section 6, Settlement marks) | - |
| `paykit_allowance` | Receive `allowance_*` messages and resend requests for them; send `allowance_end`; send `executor_notice` to an own executor; with `paykit_contacts`, also send proposals, acceptances, rejections and resend requests (Outbound authorization, below); read and write `allowances`; read `allowance_ledgers` and claims | - |
| `paykit_allowance_execute` | Act as an Allowance executor; receive `executor_notice`; write `allowance_ledgers` | `paykit_allowance`, `paykit_pay`, `paykit_contacts` |
| `paykit_contacts` | Read the contacts, conversation entries and deny list at the switch | - |
| `paykit_manage_contacts` | Change the contacts and the deny list | `paykit_contacts` |
| `paykit_ext:<prefix>` | Receive the extension kinds `<prefix>.<name>`; with `paykit_contacts`, also send them (Section 3.3; Outbound authorization, below) | - |

- A certificate lists every scope explicitly, including required ones. The signer never issues a scope without the scopes it requires. Paykit's readers, apps and payers, also refuse a certificate that lacks a scope another requires, or holds more than 8 extension scopes or a malformed one (such as `paykit_ext:paykit` or `paykit_ext:cap`, whose prefixes don't start with `paykit-`), or more than 8 labelled selling scopes; they ignore other names they don't know, which a later version may add, and other protocols' scopes (Section 3.2). The signer refuses an app request naming a scope it doesn't know (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 3.1 and Switch wire 3.9).
- **`paykit_pay` and `paykit_allowance_execute` require `paykit_contacts`,** so that every app that pays reads the deny list and pauses payment to a counterparty on it (Section 8.1). In v0.1 a block held for every paying app, since the apps of an identity shared one state (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Block A Peer"); a payer app that can't read the deny list would pay a counterparty its user blocked without telling the user. The cost is that every paying app can read the identity's contacts, conversation entries and deny list, as every v0.1 app could.
- **`paykit_request` and every labelled selling scope require `paykit_settlements`,** so that every payee app, a labelled one included, reads and writes the one mark of each settlement the identity credits, and a settlement one own app has credited is credited by no other, as in v0.1, where the apps of an identity shared one state ("A single payment must not satisfy multiple requests or periods", `specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"; Section 8.1 step 4). A bucket of marks has its record key derived from its policy hash (Section 6), so one key for every label needs one policy for every label, and only a scope that every payee app holds can seal it: a label key opens only its own label's records (Section 3.5). The cost is that a labelled app reads every bucket: it learns how many settlements the identity has credited, when, and by which own app, whose certificate names its label, and, for a settlement it can name, whether it was credited; never a counterparty, an amount, or a settlement it can't name (Section 12). And a faulty or compromised labelled app can drop or plant any label's marks, or make buckets unreadable, so that the other payee apps credit only by hand (Section 13).
- **`paykit_request` and every labelled selling scope require `paykit_contacts` too,** so every app that receives as payee holds the identity's lists and keeps a block as a payer app does: a sender the service has flagged, or a message accepted before a block, creates no state in it, and it pauses the Private Payment Lists it sends a blocked counterparty, as in v0.1, where blocking stopped intake from that counterparty in every app (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Block A Peer"). The cost: a selling app, a hosted one such as paykit-server included, reads the identity's contacts, conversation entries, deny list and the senders the service flags (Section 12); and a payee app that can't read the lists takes no new activity until it can, a payment without a request at a hosted app included (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1).
- **Outbound authorization:** sending an extension message, an `allowance_proposal`, `allowance_acceptance`, `allowance_rejection` or any `resend_request` requires explicit `paykit_contacts` as well as the kind's other sender scopes (Section 3.3). The sender then applies the deny-copy rules of Section 8.1: missing scope, an unknown copy and a known block each prevent signing or queueing the send. Missing scope is reported to the host as `MissingScope`, naming `paykit_contacts`, so the host can offer an explicit app-authorization request to the signer; the app never grants or requests the scope on its own. A valid certificate with only an extension or Allowance scope still receives its authorized kinds and is not rejected for lacking contacts. Its receipt permission does not authorize these sends. `lists.read` obtained through another protocol does not substitute for `paykit_contacts` (Section 3.2). The same requirement applies to queued delivery and to re-sends, including answers to recovery requests: loss of the scope holds existing queued messages, reports the missing scope and preserves their normal expiry; it does not release them without a deny check. The user's initial `allowance_end` remains authorized by `paykit_allowance` alone and follows Section 8.3's precise stop rules; an `executor_notice` between own apps likewise needs no contacts scope. An end generated to reflect a crossing response is still recorded at once, with its own-app notice, but its message waits for contacts authorization and a permitting deny copy (Section 8.3, Ending). These exceptions grant no permission for a re-send to a counterparty without contacts. Recovery marks awaiting authorization follow the ordinary bounds in Section 9.
- **Extension scopes:** one scope `paykit_ext:<prefix>`, for example `paykit_ext:paykit-cap`, covers every message kind whose name starts with that prefix. It is Paykit's own: it has no keys, and its bound is Paykit's. An app narrows it to the kinds it handles with its record's `accepts` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4), which it signs itself, so a new extension kind needs no new certificate and no prompt at the signer. A certificate holds at most 8 extension scopes. A prefix always starts with `paykit-`, as `paykit-cap` does, so its kinds are Paykit's under the Switch's rule: a kind's prefix is its protocol's name, or that name followed by `-` and more characters (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.3). `paykit_ext:paykit` and `paykit_ext:cap` are malformed (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Sections 3.1 and 4.3).
- **Labelled selling scopes:** `paykit_request:<label>`, for example `paykit_request:shop1`, lets a hosted selling app, such as paykit-server, act as payee for its own sales only. The label is 1 to 32 characters of `a-z`, `0-9` and `-`, starting with a letter or digit, the syntax of a sub-scope (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.3), and, unlike an extension scope's prefix, needs no `-`; a certificate holds at most 8. Payers and the switch see the label, so it should be opaque, like `shop1`, never a name (Pubky Switch spec, Section 15). Labelled selling scopes are one use of the scope rule above: plain `paykit_request` covers every label, so an app holding it reads and writes every label's records and gets the payers' replies about every label's requests, and the identity's own wallets show all its sales. So a certificate holds `paykit_request` or labels, never both: the signer never issues one with both, it refuses an app request naming both, and every reader refuses such a certificate. A labelled app reads and writes only its label's `payee_activity` records (Section 6), under its label's keys (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.5), and gets the payers' replies only about its label's requests (Section 3.4). It has the switch permissions of `paykit_request`.

### 3.2 Switch permissions

- **Switch permissions** that each Paykit scope needs, for the certificate's `permissions` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2). This table is Paykit's: the switch enforces the signed list and never derives it. What each permission lets an app do is the Switch's (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2).

  | Switch permission | Given with |
  |---|---|
  | `claims.read` | `paykit_pay`, `paykit_allowance` |
  | `claims.write` | `paykit_pay` |
  | `lists.read` | `paykit_contacts` |
  | `lists.manage` | `paykit_manage_contacts` |
  | `conversation.add` | `paykit_pay`, `paykit_request`, any labelled selling scope, `paykit_allowance`, any extension scope |

- **Paykit's verifiers check the list.** The signer sets `permissions` from the scopes, using the table above for Paykit's and each other protocol's table for its own (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2). Paykit's verifiers, at apps and at payers, derive again from Paykit's scopes the list they need, and require every permission of it. When the certificate holds no other protocol's scope, they also require `permissions` to be exactly that list, and refuse a certificate whose `permissions` are any other list (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 3.1). A change to that table therefore needs a new certificate version (`v`).
- **Several protocols in one certificate.** A certificate may hold the scopes of other protocols beside Paykit's, so one app, such as a wallet that also chats, takes part in each of them under one App ID (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2). A scope's root is its name, the part before any `:`, and each root belongs to one protocol: a protocol's roots are its name, or its name followed by `_` or `-` and more characters (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.3). Paykit's roots are therefore `paykit` and every root that is `paykit_` or `paykit-` followed by at least one more character, the names of Section 3.1 among them. A scope with one of these roots is Paykit's, also one Paykit gives no meaning, such as `paykit_pay:x`, a plain `paykit_ext`, a plain `paykit` or a root such as `paykit_future`, which it ignores and which needs no permission. A scope with any other root is another protocol's, such as `chat`, and so are `pay` and `request`, which Paykit gives no meaning. So:
  - Paykit's verifiers require every permission Paykit's scopes need, whatever the other protocols' scopes beside them; the list may then hold more, the other protocols' permissions.
  - Paykit's scope rules (Section 3.1) apply in full to its own scopes, and its bounds, 8 extension scopes and 8 labelled selling scopes, count only them.
  - Another protocol's scopes, and the switch permissions they bring, authorize nothing in Paykit: each Paykit message still needs the Paykit scope Section 3.3 names for it, and an app whose certificate holds no Paykit scope isn't a Paykit App and sends and receives no Paykit kind.
- **Own-app lifecycle.** Public Paykit join, load and restore paths must support a valid own certificate containing Paykit and other protocols. The host supplies all registered owners through a protocol set or a constructed Switch app; the SDK retains the Paykit owner and every other owner and validates Paykit authority without requiring foreign roots to belong to Paykit. Missing owners are refused before installing partial state. Registration does not grant a foreign protocol authority over Paykit kinds, collections, local state or duties. Several devices sharing this App ID still obey the Switch's shared-state and cursor synchronization rule (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.1, 3.2 and 8.3).

### 3.3 Message kind to scope

| Kind | Sender needs | Recipient app needs |
|---|---|---|
| `payment_request`, `payment_period_details`, `payment_receipt`, `private_payment_list` | `paykit_request` | `paykit_pay` |
| `payment_request_acceptance`, `payment_request_rejection`, `payment_proof`, `private_payment_list_request` | `paykit_pay` | `paykit_request` |
| `payment_request_cancellation` | `paykit_request` or `paykit_pay` | the other one |
| `allowance_proposal`, `allowance_acceptance`, `allowance_rejection` | `paykit_allowance` and `paykit_contacts` | `paykit_allowance` |
| `allowance_end` | `paykit_allowance` | `paykit_allowance` |
| `executor_notice` (own apps only) | `paykit_allowance` | `paykit_allowance_execute` |
| `resend_request` | `paykit_contacts`: every current Paykit App of the counterparty holding it may send the request, whatever other kinds it handles | Any Paykit App; the answer re-sends only kinds the requesting app's certificate receives, and a payer's replies only under the label rule of Section 3.4 (Section 9) |
| An extension kind `<prefix>.<name>` | `paykit_ext:<prefix>` and `paykit_contacts` | `paykit_ext:<prefix>` |

- **Labelled selling scopes** send and receive the kinds of `paykit_request`. A `payment_request` carries the signed `label` of the scope it is sent under, and recipients drop one whose sender holds neither `paykit_request` nor `paykit_request:<label>` for its label, and one without a label whose sender doesn't hold `paykit_request`. So an app holding only labelled scopes sends every request under one of its labels. The payer's acceptance, rejection, cancellation and proof copy the request's label (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.3).
- **Extension kinds** carry no Paykit payment semantics: no payment relies on one, they carry no destination, and no Paykit collection records one. An app that handles one passes it to its extension as it arrives, and durably keeps it until the extension takes it or the atomic eviction rule below drops it; an app that doesn't know it ignores it, like any kind it doesn't know (Switch wire 5.1, check 10). An extension message gives its sender no more than a Paykit message would: at an app holding `paykit_contacts`, one from a sender the user blocked is dropped as one never processed and asked for again once the sender is unblocked, as a Paykit message is (Section 8.1), one from a sender the service flags is counted and not passed on, and while the app's copy of the deny list isn't known it waits, as a message that creates state does; an app without `paykit_contacts` takes it as such an app takes any message (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1; Section 17, question 27). An app keeps a bounded number of extension messages not yet taken, per sender and in all (64 and 128 are the bounds the Paykit SDK is to use). Receiving one is a local transaction: the eviction order below includes the incoming message as well as those already kept. If a message already kept goes, forgetting its processed pair, counting its drop in the sender's mark, removing its payload and keeping the incoming message commit together. If the incoming message goes, its drop is counted in that same transaction without marking it processed or keeping its payload; its re-sent copy can be taken. Copies of a message whose local receive already committed, including copies stored later, are duplicates of that same `(sender, msg_id)` and must not prevent that transaction; this requires the generic Switch support adopted under Extension runtime status above. The eviction does not discard a message whose effect is still unknown. If the required earlier victim is protected, the incoming message is not admitted: the app requests the client admission wait of Section 8.1, Waiting for admission, before any receive effects commit. It does not choose a different ordinary victim, keep the incoming extension above either bound, or move an already kept extension into client waiting custody. A local storage failure changes none of this state: the incoming entry remains unprocessed for the client's retry, with later feed entries still handled. Thus no committed extension pool exceeds either bound, and backup and restore validate those same bounds. They are a family of Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound; for them its recovery rule and the atomic local transaction above mean: past a sender's bound the message of that sender its inbox accepted earliest is dropped, and past the bound on all the earliest of the sender holding the most, the lower identity on a tie; the dropped message counts in its sender's mark of the replies kept before their record (Section 8.1), which holds no record to wait for, so it asks as soon as sends to the sender aren't held; no extension message is a receipt, so none is kept past the bound as a counterparty's first receipt of an attempt is. No extension payload is dropped unless its mark and the pair changes needed to take a re-sent copy commit with it. Thus a bound does not hold up the app's feed, and every eviction atomically records its recovery obligation with the payload and pair changes. Recovery is best effort under Section 9: the ordinary bounded mark may expire or be evicted before an ask is delivered, including while the receiver lacks `paykit_contacts`. The app tells its host when such an undelivered obligation is retired; that notice contains no payload and cannot recover it. The evicted payload may therefore be lost permanently. Even a delivered ask does not establish recovery: the sender's retention and re-send limits still apply. Its body follows the JSON rules of every message, the integer rule included, so an extension writes a decimal such as a price as a string, outside the body's opaque `metadata` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.1; `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.3 and Switch wire 1.1).

### 3.4 Authorization rules

Paykit follows the Switch's authorization rules (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4), checks every kind's sender and recipient scopes in Section 3.3 separately, and narrows the senders and recipients of a payer's replies by label. A recipient drops a message whose sender lacks a required sender scope, even when that sender's certificate is valid for receiving the kind:

- **Senders.** A payer sends its acceptance, rejection, cancellation and proof for a labelled request only to the payee's apps holding `paykit_request` or that label, and for an unlabelled request, or a payment without one, only to those holding `paykit_request`. So a hosted app never receives the payer's replies about the identity's own requests. A payee's cancellation carries no label and goes only to the payer's apps holding `paykit_pay`: a cancellation's recipient holds the other role than its sender (Section 3.3).
- **Recipients** also drop a payer's reply that the rule for senders above wouldn't have sent them: an acceptance, rejection, cancellation or proof with a label, unless they hold `paykit_request` or that label, and one without a label unless they hold `paykit_request`. A cancellation without a label from an app holding `paykit_request` or a labelled selling scope may be the payee's, and passes at an app holding `paykit_pay` instead; `paykit_pay` to `paykit_pay` and `paykit_request` to `paykit_request` are dropped.
- **Homeserver grants.** Besides the paths every app's grant covers, a Paykit App's grant may cover the Paykit Profile:
  - `/pub/paykit/profile.json` and `/pub/paykit/blobs/`, read and write, only for an app the user allows to edit the Paykit Profile. The blobs hold the Profile's files, such as an image its `image_uri` names, as in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Paykit Profile And Contacts"). The two go together: when the user approves an app, the signer grants both or neither, and the user may approve the app and decline them (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4). An app whose grant lacks them offers no Profile editing, and asks for them again only when its user asks to edit the Profile, by an app request with the keys it is listed with (Switch wire 3.9).
- **From v0.1:** v0.1 apps hold `/pub/paykit/:rw` grants. Such a grant covers the Paykit Profile and its blobs, and the rest of `/pub/paykit/` too, but none of the paths every app's grant covers (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4). Before the signer publishes an identity's first v0.2 `identity.json`, it revokes every such grant, or replaces it with one that covers only those paths and, for an app the user allows to edit the Paykit Profile, the two above.

### 3.5 Keys of Paykit's scopes

- **Label keys.** A labelled selling scope `paykit_request:<label>` is a sub-scope of `paykit_request` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.5): its keys, the label keys, derive from the `paykit_request` key. Which scopes have keys is Paykit's: its scopes of Section 3.1 and their labels do, extension scopes don't, and nor does any other sub-scope of a Paykit scope (such as `paykit_pay:x`), which Paykit ignores (wire 3.1).
- **`paykit_settlements`** has keys of its own, as every scope of Section 3.1 does. It isn't a sub-scope of `paykit_request`, so a labelled app holds its keys beside its label keys, and never the `paykit_request` key (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.5).
- Extension scopes have no scope keys: no shared record holds an extension kind, so key bundles and the approval carry keys for the other scopes only (Switch wire 3.5 and 3.10).

## 4. App records

A Paykit App's record is the Switch's app record (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2). Its `accepts` lists Paykit's kinds, and its Paykit member, `protocols.paykit`, holds its `capabilities` and `public_endpoints` (wire 3.2). These rules are Paykit's, and the switch checks none of them:

- **Expiry:** a record with public endpoints carries a signed `expires_at`, chosen by the app, and Paykit's readers refuse one without it; at most 30 days ahead is recommended. The app publishes a renewed record before then, once less than half its lifetime remains, and the same rule applies to the `expires_at` of the Private Payment Lists it sends (Sections 5 and 8.4). Payers refuse public endpoints from an expired record. So an identity whose publishing app doesn't run for longer than its record's lifetime, about a month at the recommended expiry, can't be paid publicly (tips, a profile's pay button) until the app runs again; v0.1's endpoint files had no expiry. A record without public endpoints may set it to null.
- **Record age limit:** the app key signs `expires_at`, so a stolen app key could sign a record that never expires. Each identity therefore sets, in its `identity.json`, the longest time payers trust one of its app records after it was signed (`record_max_age`, Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3; 90 days when absent). A payer uses a record's public endpoints only until the earlier of its `expires_at` and its `updated_at` plus that limit, and not at all when its `updated_at` is more than 5 minutes ahead of the payer's clock. The signer signs `identity.json`, so a stolen app key can't extend the limit. The app renews its record within the limit too. An identity whose publishing app doesn't run for longer than the limit has no payable public endpoints until it does.
- **`capabilities.endpoint_identifiers`:** the Payment Endpoint Identifiers the app can pay to, so payees can offer matching destinations.
- **Public payments are optional.** An app may leave `public_endpoints` empty. Only an app holding `paykit_request` itself publishes public endpoints: a payment to one carries no label (Section 8.4, Unlabelled), so its proof reaches only the payee's apps holding `paykit_request`, and an app holding only labelled selling scopes would never see it. A payer reads at most 256 endpoints of an identity (below), so an app publishes no more than that. The identity's public Payment List is the `public_endpoints` of every current app, each listed under its app; payers may use any of them. An identity whose records list none has no public Payment List.
- **Public endpoints are read from the Homeserver.** They are public, non-interactive data: anyone reads them without authentication or the switch, and they stay readable while the switch is down or the identity is moving to another provider. A payer also compares the Homeserver's `identity.json` with the switch's copy, in parallel and waiting on it at most a short grace, and pays without the switch's answer only as Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3 allows.
- **Reading a payee's public endpoints:** the payer reads the record of each app current in the payee's `identity.json`, in that document's order, and keeps the usable endpoints of each. One app's failure, a record it can't read, or one it refuses or that has expired, is reported as that app's, and the other apps' usable endpoints stay available, as in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Resolve Public Payment"); an app with no record has no public endpoints. The read takes at most 256 endpoints and 4 MiB of payloads over all the apps, as in v0.1: an app whose endpoints don't fit in what is left is reported as over the bound, never cut to fit. A request with `required_app_id` reads only that app's record, so that app's failure leaves the request with no public destination (Section 8.1).
- **Payers check every destination's app** before paying (Section 7.2): the app that published a public endpoint, or sent the request, period details or Private Payment List, must be current in its identity's `identity.json` with the certificate it presented (listed, or renewed and still counting, Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2); that certificate must allow `paykit_request`, and a listed certificate must not have expired. The record or Private Payment List carrying the destination must not have expired either (Section 5). A removed app's destinations are ignored, even if its record is still on the Homeserver.
- **Withdrawing a destination:** stale copies of a record or a Private Payment List can't be recalled before they expire. A payee whose destination is compromised therefore removes it and also revokes the app, or replaces it with a new app (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.2), so that payers stop paying to it once their 10-minute copy of `identity.json` shows the app removed.
- **What is public:** an app publishes its record on the Homeserver when it has public endpoints, or when another protocol it takes part in needs public data (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2). Anyone can read a published record whole: its accepted kinds, its label, its certificate with its scopes and permissions, and its Paykit member with the app's capabilities, also when its `public_endpoints` is empty. An app that publishes no record on the Homeserver appears publicly only as an app ID and certificate hash in `identity.json`, so anyone can see how many apps an identity has, but not what they are. Admitted senders get the full records from the directory.
- **Fresh before paying:** an app fetches or revalidates its own identity's `identity.json` in parallel with each `attempt`, unless it did so in the last 60 s. It moves money only when it holds such a copy and that copy names the service whose key signed the attempt's transition proof (Section 7.2); a payment made while its own switch doesn't answer, which has no attempt yet, needs a copy naming the service the app uses (Section 8.4). If the Homeserver is unreachable for longer, the app makes no new attempts. An open event stream doesn't replace this check, because a stalled stream looks the same as a quiet one.
- **The Paykit Profile** is optional public display metadata of the identity, at `/pub/paykit/profile.json` on the Homeserver, written by the signer or an app the user authorizes (Section 3.4). Two apps may write it at once, and the later write wins, since v0.2 has no Homeserver lock (Section 15). An app reads the Profile again just before it writes and writes it whole.
- **App storage.** Besides what every app keeps (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.1), a Paykit App keeps Paykit's state: derived state such as Allowance budgets, its payment journal and the Private Payment Lists it publishes.

## 5. Message kinds

Kind strings are `paykit.` followed by the names below, or extension kinds `<prefix>.<name>` under an extension scope, which carry no Paykit payment semantics (Section 3.3). Message bodies use the field definitions of the v0.1 SDK specs at commit `53b8c8cb2735c0845232fbdf34bc83052bf88404`: `specs/payment-requests.md` for requests, recurrence, Billing Periods, acceptance, rejection, cancellation, proofs and receipts; `specs/payment-conversion.md` for conversion terms, quotes and deadlines; `specs/allowances.md` for Allowance terms and messages; and `specs/paykit-sdk.md` for Private Payment List versioning. Those files title their protocol "Payment Request Protocol v0.2"; here they are the v0.1 bodies. This spec defines transport, authorization, storage and lifecycle, and changes the bodies as listed below. The exact v0.2 bodies are in `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.

| Kind | Notes |
|---|---|
| `payment_request` | Carries its amount and `payment_reference`, and usually its destination (`payment_endpoints`), which the payer must then use; without one, the payer pays to any of the payee's current endpoints, or only those of the payee app named in `required_app_id` (Section 8.1). A request carries at most one of `payment_endpoints` and `required_app_id`. Optional: proposal expiry, `recurrence`, conversion terms, payment deadline, and `per_period_endpoints` for a recurring request (Section 8.2). |
| `payment_request_acceptance` | One-time request: sent when Section 7.2 step 2 makes it sendable; not awaited. Recurring request: sent once, when the user, or an executor under an Allowance, accepts the subscription, before any payment, and recorded first (Section 8.2). The proposal expiry bounds the acceptance. |
| `payment_request_rejection` | Recorded before it is sent (Section 8.1). |
| `payment_request_cancellation` | From either side. A payer records its cancellation before sending it (Section 8.1). |
| `payment_period_details` | New in v0.2; replaces v0.1's conversion quote. The payee's details for one Billing Period of a recurring request: its `payment_endpoints`, its conversion rates (cross-asset only, as v0.1 quotes), or both, with an `expires_at`. Its rates apply to a payment whose verified payment time is from its `created_at`, cut to whole seconds, through `expires_at`, as a v0.1 quote's do from its `valid_from`. Each stays valid until its own `expires_at`, and the proof references the one it used (Section 8.2). |
| `payment_proof` | References the request and Billing Period, or none for a payment without a request, and the period details or Private Payment List version it paid to. Carries the `attempt_id` it reports and an opaque proof. Required after paying a single-use endpoint (Section 8.4). |
| `payment_receipt` | Signed by the payee; sending it is optional, but a payee app that credits a proof stores its receipt in that record, in the batch that writes the settlement's mark (Section 8.1). References the proof it confirms, and so the attempt. May carry the payee's `metadata`, and, for a payment without a request, a `payment_reference`, as in v0.1. |
| `resend_request` | Asks the counterparty to re-send messages by `msg_id`, request, Billing Period, or a range of acceptance times. Best effort. |
| `private_payment_list` | Latest state per publishing app and recipient identity. Its version is the ordered pair of the publisher's verified identity generation and a per-recipient reservation counter (Section 8.4.1, wire 4.13). Every new publication reserves a greater pair, including after a restore. It carries a required `expires_at`, which the publishing app computes from its current time, not from the version. Payers use the list only until the earlier of `expires_at` and 30 days after their inbox accepted it, so a stolen app key can't sign a list payable for long, and refuse it after that. Endpoints are reusable unless marked `single_use` (Section 8.4). |
| `private_payment_list_request` | Asks for the current list and names the version the sender holds. Best effort. |
| `allowance_proposal`, `allowance_acceptance`, `allowance_rejection`, `allowance_end` | The acceptance names the executor app at acceptance, for information; a later handoff doesn't change it. |
| `executor_notice` | New in v0.2, between apps of one identity only. Tells an executor that an Allowance ended (`reason: ended`) or was handed to another app (`reason: handed_off`), naming the Allowance's record key. Its only effect is on the recipient's consent list (Section 8.3). |

- **Differences from the v0.1 bodies and rules:**
  - `required_app_id` is optional and only allowed without `payment_endpoints`, whose owner is always the sending app (`from.app_id`).
  - Dropped: `event_id` and `payment_request_id`, replaced by `msg_id` and `refs`; Encrypted Links, Receipt Access and Delivery Confirmations; the 1000-byte limit; and FIFO message order, which Section 8.1 replaces.
  - `per_period_endpoints`, `payment_period_details` (which replaces the conversion quote and can also carry a period's destination) and `executor_notice` are new. So is `label`: a request sent under a labelled selling scope carries it, and the payer's acceptance, rejection, cancellation and proof copy it (Section 3.3). Private Payment List endpoints may carry `single_use: true`, and a list carries `expires_at`.
  - A payee app tells whether a payment was already credited to another occurrence from the settlement's mark, which a credit writes in the batch that stores its receipt, and only while no mark is there, where v0.1's one shared state held that across an identity's apps (Sections 6, Settlement marks, and 8.1 step 4).
  - Period details may come from any payee app that holds the request's record, a labelled one included, where v0.1 took a quote only from the app that proposed the request (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"): the sending app writes them into the payee's shared period record before it sends them, so every own payee app can check a proof against them (Section 8.2).
  - Billing Periods follow one rule, the anchored period rule of `specs/allowances.md` (Section 8.2), where v0.1 left expansion open.
  - Readers ignore unknown fields in message bodies (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.1), where v0.1 rejected them.
  - A Private Payment List carries a `version` member, the publisher's generation and counter pair (Section 8.4.1), where v0.1's `version` was the format version; and an Allowance proposal carries `allower_generation`, the Allower's verified identity generation (wire 4.15).
  - A second, different acceptance or rejection under one `allowance_id` makes the Allowance conflicted (Section 8.3). v0.1 marks it invalid history instead, which blocks automatic payment until an explicit review (`specs/allowances.md`, "Transport and common rules" and "Acceptance and rejection"). v0.2 has no review call, so the Allowance stays conflicted, and the two identities set up a new one.
- **Size:** the JSON of each kind stored in activity records (Section 6) is at most 16 KiB.
- There is no refund message; a refund is a normal Payment Request in the other direction.

## 6. Paykit shared records

| Collection | One record per | Contents | `read_scope` | `write_scopes` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `payer_activity` | Request received; for a recurring request also each Billing Period; or payment made without a request | **Request record:** the request, acceptance, rejection, cancellations and the request's status, and for a recurring request the Billing Periods that may have an open or `unknown` attempt (Section 8.2). **Period record:** the period details, proof sent, receipt received and the period's status. A one-time request has only a request record, holding both | `paykit_pay` | `paykit_pay` |
| `payee_activity` | Request sent; for a recurring request also each Billing Period; or payment received without a request | The same, from the payee's side | `paykit_request`, or `paykit_request:<label>` for a request sent under that label | `paykit_request`, or `paykit_request:<label>` |
| `payment_lists` | Payee and publishing app | The latest Private Payment List received and the single-use endpoints used | `paykit_pay` | `paykit_pay` |
| `private_list_publications` | Publishing app, recipient identity and publishing identity generation | The protected current publication counter and exact message binding (Section 8.4.1; wire 5.8) | `paykit_request` | `paykit_request` |
| `block_floor` | Identity: one record | The block floor: the time before which a one-time request is paid only by hand (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties) | `paykit_pay` | `paykit_pay` |
| `settlements` | Bucket of settlements: at most 1,024 records | The marks of the settlements any own payee app has credited in the bucket, each naming the occurrence it was credited to: a request, a Billing Period or a payment without a request (Section 8.1 step 4); never removed, and the record never deleted | `paykit_settlements` | `paykit_settlements` |
| `allowances` | Allowance, on either side | Proposal, accepted terms, the executor's grant/history, handoff, response, end and conflict (Section 8.3) | `paykit_allowance` | `paykit_allowance` |
| `allowance_ledgers` | Allowance | Its ledger, entries and running totals (Section 8.3) | `paykit_allowance` | `paykit_allowance_execute` |

- **Fixed policies:** each collection's records use the policy above. A request sent under a label has its `payee_activity` records, its request record and for a recurring request its period records, under that label's policy: `read_scope = paykit_request:<label>`, `write_scopes = ["paykit_request:<label>"]`. Apps ignore any record in these collections whose policy differs, and a labelled one whose request, or payer's message, names another label or none, and a fixed one whose request names a label, so no app can plant a record readable under another scope or another label.
- **Different writes at the same activity version.** After a recovery upload, the Switch client can hold one signed write for a `payer_activity` or `payee_activity` record while its next write would build on a different signed operation at that same record version (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 12.6). While the client reports this difference, the app holds any payment, credit or record update that depends on the disputed activity, including through its related request or Billing Period. It retains its local stops and the outcome of a payment already made. It keeps incoming verified messages for retry with their original message IDs under the ordinary intake rules. Unrelated activity can continue.
  The app checks the effective write again before resuming. When the client selects the original signed operation again or accepts a successor, the ordinary activity checks apply to that write. A version alone or a reconstruction from the app's journal cannot settle which signed write is current. A reload retains the comparison. After a backup restore the client reads the records again. If it has no copy of the original write, it has no same-version comparison from that copy.
- **One message per role:** an activity record stores the messages that make up its state, one per role. A request record holds the request, the payer's response (acceptance or rejection) and a cancellation from either side; a period record holds the period details, the proof and the receipt; a one-time request's record holds the request's roles and its proof and receipt; a record of a payment without a request holds its proof and receipt. A later message for a role already held is still processed when it arrives, for example a second proof, which shows a duplicate payment, but isn't stored. A record of a payment without a request is keyed by its settlement (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3), so a second proof there names the same settlement: a replay, not a second payment, and credited once (Section 8.4). The one exception is period details: a period record keeps every details it has held for its period, at most 8 (Section 8.2). Allowance records hold their proposal, response, end and conflicting message the same way (Section 8.3). The kinds activity records store are at most 16 KiB (Section 5), and a record lists only a bounded number of `msg_id`s (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), so an activity record stays within the 256 KiB limit for a shared record value without any message count, however many messages repeat a role it holds.
- **Deleting finished activity:** an app may delete an activity record (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3) once the user no longer wants it, with these exceptions: a payer never deletes its record of a paid obligation without a deadline (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2), one that carries the proof of its payment, and a payee app never deletes a record that holds a settlement it credited, one that carries its receipt, so that no own app is shown that settlement's proof again over a delete and credits it twice (Section 8.1, Records not listed yet; Section 8.4, Credited once; Section 17, question 35); a payer deletes a record holding `manual_only` only once its request's deadline has passed, by the latest service time it has verified, and never one of a request without a deadline; and it deletes a request record only once its `attempted_periods` lists no period, dropping finished periods first as any write may (Section 8.2, Attempted periods). An own app puts back a record of these deleted all the same (Putting back a deleted record). The user may archive the payer's record, which hides it in the app, but the record stays: the claim is gone 400 days after its last entry (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), and from then on the record, not the claim, stops a second payment of a re-sent copy (Section 7.2). It stays whole, since a reader ignores a write that drops a message it has seen (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). The payer's cost is one record per payment without a deadline, and the payee's one record per credited settlement, each kept for the identity's life. A record of an obligation with a deadline may go: its claim, or its tombstone, still shows the payment as done until 30 days after the deadline, after which a later copy can't be paid anyway (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3). A period record's deadline comes from its request, so the payer keeps a recurring request's record while it keeps any of the request's period records; a period record whose request record is missing counts as one without a deadline.
- **Settlement marks:** a settlement's mark records that the identity has credited it, and names the occurrence it was credited to. Marks live in `settlements`, in at most 1,024 bucket records: a settlement's bucket and the tag its mark carries derive from its settlement ID alone, as its payment method names it, whatever the counterparty, the endpoint or the occurrence (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Sections 1.5 and 5.7). A credit adds the settlement's mark to its bucket in the batch that stores the receipt, bound at application to authenticated absence for first creation or the exact current write and its verified incarnation, so of two credits of one settlement, by any own payee apps, labelled or not, to any occurrences, only one applies, and the other fails and decides again (Section 8.1 step 4; wire 5.7, Authenticated creation and continuity); only a credit by hand over a bucket that can't take the mark stores its receipt without one (below). No app removes or changes a mark, or deletes a bucket. A zero-version write requires the service to hold nothing at the key when the batch applies. A preceding absent read alone cannot enforce that condition, and neither proves the history of a key the service has forgotten. Every put-back is bound at application to the exact current delete it answers, including a complete-copy restoration prepared before a later delete. Every reader needs authenticated creation or protective put-back and exact-predecessor continuity preserving deletion restrictions; missing evidence withholds credit and receipts even by hand, also for a fresh app, an older backup and a recovered write. Switch revision `f0bbddd1` specified generic origin and lineage evidence for later readers. That evidence is unavailable at the current pin, so these dependent settlement paths remain withheld (wire 5.7). A reader that has seen a mark keeps it, and ignores and shows with its writer a later write of its bucket that drops or changes it; it writes the bucket again at once with every mark it has seen, as many as fit in 9,000 (below), over a write that is valid and opens, and puts a deleted bucket back: its copy when the delete was built on it, matched by item hash, else every mark it has seen with the delete's item hash added to its retained deletion boundaries, merged with every boundary of the current write and its copies, and tells its user once per delete it answers. A bucket put back so, or whose current write is a delete, is taken at its least permissive state: the deleted bucket could have held a mark of any settlement in it, so a settlement of it that the app has seen no mark of is decided by independent durable evidence of three kinds alone. First, the identity's own records: a `payee_activity` record or a receipt that names the settlement shows it credited, so it is never credited again; no own record shows a settlement not credited, since a record of its credit may have been lost with a delete too, so the absence of one is no evidence. Second, the payment method's own bound: a settlement whose method proves, in a way the app verifies, the depth or time from which its inclusion is final, after the delete at the method's declared final depth, such as a block above the height the app recorded after verifying the delete's actual application, or a carrier authenticated as applied later, plus the confirmations the method requires before a credit, or a time past the delete's authenticated application time, or that of such a proved later carrier, plus the clock tolerance and the method's documented timestamp error, never a claimed, broadcast or block timestamp taken as it stands, can't have been credited before the delete but by a reorganization deeper than that depth, which Section 13 excludes, and is credited, its mark written; a method that gives no such bound gives no evidence. Third, a destination issued after the delete: a settlement paid to a single-use payload or an invoice that the app's payment method guarantees was never issued before, to any app or wallet sharing its keys, for a destination the app issued and offered after verifying that applied delete or a proved later carrier, which the app still keeps with authenticated order evidence tying its later issuance to that application, also across a backup and restore (Section 8.4, Only what it offered), a restored wallet that derives its addresses again or a restored offer without that mark giving no evidence, couldn't have been paid, so credited, before it, and is credited, its mark written. These forms of evidence first require authenticated application of the particular delete to this bucket and the causal order of every observation used. An operation can be signed and its hash published before the delete applies, so the first carrier of a hash proves no date, height or issuance order. Only a carrier proved to have applied after that delete is a conservative later bound; an older observation never becomes later merely because the delete is subsequently authenticated. Every retained and pending delete is cleared separately, including revalidation of a hash encountered in a later deletion context; evidence for one clears no other. Restore, recovery, versions and user confirmation supply no missing order. If bounded current evidence cannot establish the required relation, credit stays held, without requiring lifetime history. A known boundary holds a canonical set of at most 128 delete hashes; unions exceeding that bound become an absorbing `unknown` boundary, which permits no new credit, including later settlements. Its user's confirmation alone is never evidence, since a second credit gives value twice. A settlement none of these clears is held, never credited by any app, automatically or by hand, and the app tells its host once per settlement which it holds and why (`CreditHeldForDelete`). Every boundary read in `lost_to_delete` is retained durably and merged by union, never replaced by the current write's boundary. A current delete stays pending until this reader proves that an applied put-back contains the complete authenticated deleted predecessor and every already-retained mark and boundary; otherwise its hash becomes retained too. Merely finding every mark this reader happened to see in a later write is not that proof. A retained hash is never removed, and `unknown` can never be narrowed, even after evidence clears one settlement. Every credit write, rewrite, conflict merge, overflow fill, restore and provider-loss upload preserves this union; an older backup or recovered write does not lower it. Unreadable or known-lost deletion state is `unknown`, not absence. This rule goes before a full, divergent, unreadable or invalid bucket, a refused write and a provider loss: none of them credits by hand a settlement it withholds, except by this closed list (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Reading, Deletion boundaries, Merging boundaries, Seen marks, Put back over a delete, Restore and provider loss, and Credits after a delete). A reader with no predecessor or marks still publishes a boundary-only bucket, whose `marks` is the empty base64 string and whose boundary is nonempty `known` or `unknown`. Every current participating reader keeps a durable publication obligation and withholds the service's cursor acknowledgment past the delete until the protective shared write applies, atomically with the acknowledgment or before it; local receive progress, a local duty acknowledgment and a queued repair do not suffice. Quota refusal keeps this acknowledgment hold and can retain service data, even while later items are read. Restore and recovery preserve the obligation and publish retained restrictions with no marks too. This publication-before-acknowledgment order uses existing batch and cursor semantics and needs a durable client protocol hold. The absence and incarnation requirements need additional generic Switch support, not supplied by those cursor rules (wire 5.7). A mark outlives the activity record that credited it. A claim's tombstone goes 30 days after its deadline or 400 days after its last entry (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), since a later copy of its obligation can't be paid after that; a settlement has no deadline, and its proof can be shown again at any time, so its mark never goes. A mark takes 21 bytes, 28 in the bucket's JSON, and the buckets take at most 1,024 live record keys. The `payee_activity` record each credit keeps for the identity's life reaches the storage quota and the 100,000 live record keys long before the marks do; 100,000 marks take about 3 MB. A bucket holds at most 9,000 marks, and no write, by hand or not, holds more: a rewrite whose marks don't all fit holds those of the current write it keeps and as many of the app's other seen marks as fit, in ascending byte order, so the bucket is full; the app keeps every mark it has seen in its own state, those that didn't fit too, and tells its host once per bucket (`SettlementBucketFull`); each app goes on taking every mark it has seen as a credit, whatever the bucket shows, and no app credits a settlement of a full bucket automatically, so the apps converge however their seen marks differ (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Over capacity). A credit whose bucket is full, or whose bucket write the service refuses for the storage quota or the value limit, is made only by hand, once its user has been shown that the settlement can't be marked and so may be credited again, and stores its receipt without a mark; the app tells its host once per bucket (`SettlementBucketFull`). A credit, with its mark or by hand without one, is complete only once the batch that stores its receipt applies; when the service refuses that batch for the storage quota or the value limit, also without the bucket's write, nothing is credited and no receipt is sent: the proof stays pending under the stored-entry rules (Section 8.1, Stored entries), and the app tells its host of the refusal (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Complete). While a bucket's current write doesn't open or read, or is invalid, or while its view shows it divergent, every settlement in it may already be credited: no app credits one automatically, and one by hand only once its user has been shown that it may be, and the app tells its host once per settlement (`CreditNeedsUser`). How a bucket is written again over a delete, a divergent view or a value that is invalid or doesn't open is `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Seen marks, Put back over a delete, Repairing and Over capacity.
- **Divergent records:** after a provider loss, an upload of a record that conflicts with a write made at the new service, or two different uploaded values at one version, is kept and listed as divergent until a writer writes a newer version there, and the record's view says so (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6; Switch wire 7.4.9); what to do about a divergent record is the protocol's (Switch wire 7.4.14). An app takes the current write as any other, but the upload may hold what the current write lacks, such as a cancellation or a rejection in a request record, a receipt or period details in an activity record, an end, a rejection or a `conflicted` message in an `allowances` record, entries in an `allowance_ledgers` record, a `manual_only` mark, or a `used` entry or a newer list in a `payment_lists` record. So while the latest view of a request or period record shows it divergent, the app pays or accepts that occurrence automatically never, and by hand only after its user has seen that the payee may have cancelled it, and a payee app credits no proof against it until its user confirms (Section 8.1); a recurring request's request record whose latest view shows it divergent pauses each of its Billing Periods the same way, whatever the period record's view shows, since its upload may hold a cancellation, a `manual_only` mark or a reassociation the current write lacks (Section 8.3), so while it does, no period of the request is paid automatically, and one by hand only after its user has seen that the payee may have cancelled it; and while the latest view of an Allowance's `allowances` or `allowance_ledgers` record shows it divergent, its executor pays nothing automatically under it; and while the latest view of a `payment_lists` record shows it divergent, no app pays to any endpoint of that record's Private Payment List, single-use or reusable, automatically, and by hand only after its user has seen that the list may be stale, since the list is latest state and the upload may hold a higher version, in which the payee may have withdrawn or replaced a destination.
