A request, or a Billing Period, that no confirmation could let be paid isn't paused while its record, or its request's record, is: one past its deadline, for a Billing Period its own attempt deadline (Section 7.1), is refused as one past its deadline is; one whose destination, the request's `payment_endpoints` or, for a Billing Period paid by period details, those details' (Section 8.2), comes from an app that isn't current in the payee's `identity.json`, read fresh, is refused as the destination check refuses it (Section 7.2); and one with `required_app_id` naming an app that copy lists as removed, which is never listed again (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3), is refused for that; a read of that copy that fails refuses the payment, and none of them names the record. A request requiring an app that copy neither lists nor lists as removed still pauses. An app that has seen a record divergent keeps this pause for the occurrence, the Allowance or the list until its user confirms, also once a newer write has cleared the view. It takes a record as seen divergent whenever a view it read showed it so (Switch wire 7.4.9), also when a later write it read in the same pass of the feed cleared it. The reference client tells the app of each record that turns divergent by a duty event, delivered at least once, kept until the app acknowledges it and raised again, with a new ID, after a restore while the record is still divergent: the app sets or keeps its mark from each such event by the record's key, never by the event's ID, acknowledges it once the mark is kept, or with no mark for a record it no longer holds, on which it can pay, accept or credit nothing, and judges whether the record is divergent now from its view's flag (Switch wire 7.4.9). An app keeps a mark of each record it has seen divergent, with its collection and key, the write it showed, whether its user confirmed it and whether that confirmation showed that the record may hold a credit its current write lacks; a confirmed mark lifts the pause. A payee app keeps the mark of a counterparty's record, once its view has cleared, until its user has given such a confirmation, and until then credits no proof of that counterparty (Section 8.1 step 4). A user's confirmation is of the write the app showed: given for another write than the one the app holds then, it confirms nothing, and the app shows the record again. It holds for that write alone: a later view that shows another write divergent pauses the record again. A second divergent upload beside the same current write can't be told from the first, since the view says only whether the record is divergent (Switch wire 7.4.9), so one confirmation covers both. A confirmation of a record the app no longer holds ends its pause and drops its mark. A mark whose write is later than the write the app holds at that key, or whose collection isn't that key's, matches no write the app holds: the app keeps the record at that key paused until its user confirms the write the app then shows, lifts no other pause by it, and goes on with every other record. A backup carries the marks of divergent records: a restore installs each one unconfirmed, so the restored app pauses every record it had seen divergent until its user confirms again. A mark set after the backup is lost with what the restore loses (Section 7.2); a record still divergent pauses again at its first view after the listing (Section 8.1, Records not listed yet). While a record's view shows it divergent, an app writes over it only a write that adds a stop, a cancellation, a rejection, an end, a `conflicted` message or a `manual_only` mark, a write that puts the record back over a delete (Putting back a deleted record), or settlement marks (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7), or one its user confirmed having seen what the upload may hold; a receive-step write that adds none of these is kept (Section 8.1, Stored entries; Replies before their record) and written after that confirmation, in the order the inbox accepted the kept messages, and an app's own write of another kind, such as period details or a receipt a payee app stores, waits for that confirmation too. A receipt whose payer record's view shows it divergent, or over whose record messages kept while it was divergent wait to be written, is kept with those messages, with the claim `done` it carries evidence for, whether or not the app can read the record, under the spam rule for a payment without a request when it can't (Section 8.1, Replies before their record), and both are written only once the user has confirmed the record and the record reads; the first receipt from the record's counterparty for each attempt its journal or the claim shows is never dropped for the bound on the messages kept over the record (Section 8.1, Stored entries). A receipt kept as a reply before its record counts, once the record reads paused, as a message kept over it, and waits for that confirmation too. What records a payment already dispatched, a one-time request's acceptance not yet sent, the outcome and the proof, waits while the payer record is paused, that is until its user confirms, and goes once the user has confirmed and the messages kept over the record are written (Section 8.1, Stored entries), also while the view still shows the record divergent (Section 7.2 steps 2 to 4): the rule that the app pays or accepts nothing automatically while the latest view shows a record divergent, confirmed or not, covers what starts or repeats a payment or an acceptance, not what records a payment already made.
- **Writing over a delete:** a write over a record whose current write is a delete, including a delete the app refused, names that delete's version. If the current write came from a recovery upload, the operation also names its exact application in `record_current`. If the service has since forgotten the delete, it refuses the write with a version conflict and the app decides again under the protected-family and no-copy holds below (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 10.3; Switch wire 6.2 and 6.3). A `settlements` bucket follows wire 5.7.
- **Observing a deleted payee activity record.** When an own app learns that another own app deleted a `payee_activity` record, it retains the local evidence and credit restrictions described in wire Section 5. The payee can show an already issued receipt whose signatures and occurrence it can verify. A known credit still counts under Section 8.1 step 4, including after a later write at the same key or an in-place restore from an older backup. Observing the delete does not put the record back or authorize a new credit.
- **Putting back a deleted record:** the positive write and authorization paths described here are withheld under the incomplete-contract rule in wire Section 5 until its generic mechanism is defined and pinned; the holds and retained restrictions apply meanwhile. A record whose content withholds or bounds a payment, or the credit of one, is one no Paykit App deletes: the families are listed below, each with its least permissive state. Every older copy of a `payee_activity` or `payment_lists` record belongs to its protected family, even if that copy holds no receipt or `used` entry: the deleted write may have added one. An own app that holds such a record, by its own copy, and reads a delete of it that another app wrote never acts on the delete as an end of what the record held. It doesn't take the delete, building on it as on a refused write (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2, Writing over a refused write), and writes the record back at once, over the delete, naming its version (Writing over a delete), before or in the batch that acknowledges the delete to the service. The service-held cursor never reaches or passes that delete until the protective write has applied, including when quota or the value limit refuses the write; a queued write is insufficient. The app keeps the publication obligation durably before moving its local receive position, and may continue local reads only without advancing the service-held cursor past that hold.
  - **Publication before acknowledgment:** the service uses its per-app cursor, not the app's local receive position, to forget deletions (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3; Switch wire 9.1). An app required to put a record back never sends `cursor.set` at or beyond the delete's sequence until a shared write containing every restriction it must preserve has applied: either `record.put` followed by `cursor.set` in one atomic batch, or an applied and verified protective write first (Switch wire 6.4). A write-back by another app qualifies only when it preserves those restrictions (Two apps at once). A queued repair, a local duty acknowledgment or a local cursor move does not qualify. A quota, value-limit or other refusal keeps the service-cursor hold; the app tells its host, keeps the family's least permissive state and retries when it can publish safely. Receive acknowledgment and operations that need it may remain unavailable while publication is blocked; safety does not assume room eventually becomes available. Retry, reload, backup and restore preserve the obligation, and every device sharing the app's service cursor obeys it. An older ledger that cannot yet be written safely keeps this hold until its specified repair publishes it.
  - **What it writes:** a write-back never restores a state more permissive than the one deleted. The app writes its copy as it holds it only when that copy is the write the delete was built on, the write at the delete's signed `expected_version`, matched by item hash through authenticated feed or chain evidence, not version alone; without that proof, including an upload whose predecessor cannot be authenticated, the copy is treated as older. An app holding the exact predecessor names the deleting app (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). When its copy is older, after a gap in its feed, a `missed` item or a restore that hasn't caught up (Section 7.2, What a `missed` item loses; What a restore loses), it can't tell what was deleted: it writes the family's least permissive state, built on its copy, saying that the record's content was lost to a delete and naming that delete by its item hash (`lost_to_delete`), names no app, since it can't tell that the delete broke a rule, and tells its user.
  - **Held as deleted:** until a write over the delete lands, an app reads a record it holds as deleted as the family's least permissive state, whatever its copy.
  - **Two apps at once:** two own apps that put one record back at once both build on the same current delete, and one write lands. The other reads the write that landed and takes it when it withholds everything its own write would; otherwise it writes over it, at its version, the two merged, which withholds what either does (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5, Put back over a delete). A reader that has seen more of the record than a write-back carrying `lost_to_delete` holds merges over it the same way, naming nobody, rather than ignoring it for what it lacks. A merge only adds what withholds, so no write-back answers another more than once.
  - **Again and again:** each delete is put back. An app names a deleting app once, and its user is told once per deleting app, whatever the number of deletes, and once per delete a write-back from an older copy answers, which that write-back names. Apart from that, an app asks its user to confirm the record again whenever its confirmation ends, which a missed write ends too. A user's confirmation binds the item hash and version of the write shown, and advances only when authenticated feed or chain evidence proves that exact write was the successor's predecessor. It never covers the loss of a later delete: it ends at the first delete, missed write, unproven predecessor or other value the app reads, even if a version is reused, and at a restore (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5, Confirming a loss).
  - **Visible deletion without a copy:** an app that reads an authenticated delete but holds no copy cannot tell what the deleted value withheld. It therefore treats every deleted payer activity key (request or period), payee activity key and `payment_lists` key as potentially protected, regardless of a replay's content. Deleted `allowances`, `allowance_ledgers` and `block_floor` keys are protected as their families already require. The app durably keeps the delete's identity and the hold before advancing its local read position; listing, restart, backup and restore do not turn that known deletion into absence. It writes no replacement from a replay or new message at that key, including `{0, 0}` creates, and makes no payment, credit, acceptance, executor grant or endpoint use that depends on the missing state. A deleted recurring request holds every Billing Period; a new period key cannot bypass its request's hold. An app without a copy waits for an app able to make the protective put-back; quota or value-limit refusal may leave it waiting indefinitely. It takes a later write only under the family rules and never treats a concurrent unmarked recreation as proof that no content was lost. A put-back carrying `lost_to_delete` applies its family's restrictions, including the permanent payer and payee holds; a claimed exact-copy put-back must be proved as What it writes requires. A replay or a user's confirmation supplies no such proof. A listing or read that fails to establish whether a delete is current grants nothing.
  - **Creation and later writes:** for every potentially protected family above, an app may create new usable state only with authenticated evidence, enforced when the create applies, that the key has no current value and no retained delete. A prior read showing absence is insufficient: another app can create and delete the key before the create applies. The Switch's zero-version condition enforces absence at application, including for a key the service has forgotten. A later reader still needs evidence of that creation and its successors. A create over a retained delete instead needs the protective put-back and its evidence above, with authenticated evidence enforced when the put-back applies that the current delete is the exact item it answers. A put-back prepared for one delete cannot apply over another intervening delete and must read and decide again. Every later writer preserves the restrictions and authenticated provenance of that record incarnation, through updates, recreation and protective put-back. A reader with no seen state, including a new app or an old-backup restore, may authorize payment, credit, acceptance, a grant or endpoint use from a current value only after verifying this provenance: an authenticated absence at the incarnation's creation followed by exact-predecessor continuity with every required restriction preserved, or a verified protective put-back followed by that continuity. An unmarked current value, its valid signature, a version match, a replay and a user's confirmation do not prove the absence of an earlier protected delete; a later unmarked rewrite cannot conceal such a delete and grant authority. Missing, incomplete or unverifiable provenance keeps the key held. Retry, reload, backup, restore and provider recovery preserve or reauthenticate the retained restrictions and this provenance; none resets the incarnation or turns missing evidence into authority. Switch revision `f0bbddd1` defined bounded reader evidence in its spec Section 10.3 and wire 6.9. The current pin has no corresponding proof. The mapping for these families remains INCOMPLETE (wire Section 5); this paragraph defines safety requirements, not an executable positive path. Until the mapping, generic implementation, generated cases and Paykit integration are complete and the implementing Switch revision is adopted, an implementation cannot enable first creation, protective put-back or later authority that depends on that provenance. For families without a permanent origin slot, genuine absence after the service has legitimately forgotten all evidence remains subject to the all-witnesses-removed limitation below; a still-listed witness or retained delete cannot be treated as that case.
  - **Without the write scope:** an app whose certificate holds none of the record's `write_scopes` writes nothing: it names the deleting app as above, tells its user and holds the record as deleted until an own app that holds a write scope puts it back.
  - **When a record is lost:** the service may forget a deleted value only once every app the identity's `identity.json` lists has acknowledged it with its service-held cursor (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3, Forgotten deletions). An honest app required to put it back withholds that acknowledgment until the protective write applies, including while quota blocks publication, so its local progress cannot let the key be forgotten. An app that does not acknowledge delays the forgetting; a removed app no longer does. So the value can be lost when every honest app that could put it back is removed before publication, the removed-app case that section names. For a family without a permanent origin slot, absence with no copy or retained evidence can then leave an app unable to tell that the record existed.
  - **The families**, each with its least permissive state, which a write-back from an older copy writes and a record held as deleted reads as. A write-back from an older copy can't tell whether the deleted record held a payment made, a credit made or an end, so each state forbids what any of them would have forbidden. Where money or a credit could then be given twice, a user's confirmation doesn't lift it, since the user can't see what was deleted either: only independent durable evidence does, as each family says.
    - an `allowances` record: ended for good, nothing paid, no grant written, no acceptance sent, and a proposal under its `allowance_id` refused: a replayed copy of its proposal changes nothing, and any other is conflicted (Section 8.3, Ending).
    - an `allowance_ledgers` record: only the Allowance's executor puts one back (Section 8.3, Who wrote it), and only from its last accepted version, when the delete was built on it, as a ledger write conditioned on the Allowance record's version, once that record isn't held as deleted. Its least permissive state is the delete itself, "usage history lost": an executor whose kept version isn't the one the delete was built on writes nothing, and a deleted ledger, put back or not, pays nothing automatically until its user confirms the Allowance again with its figures (Section 8.3, After a confirmation).
    - a payer's request or period record holding `manual_only`, while its request's deadline hasn't passed at the service time of the feed page that shows the delete, or of a request without a deadline: the mark kept, manual-only.
    - every payer's request or period record, when the copy is older: its occurrence, every Billing Period of the request for a recurring request's request record, is paid never, automatically or by hand, since the deleted record may have held its proof. No missing, present or recreated claim, whatever attempts it shows, and no user confirmation releases this hold (Payment history lost, below).
    - a payer's request record whose `attempted_periods` lists a period: every listed period kept listed, and, when the copy is older, every Billing Period of the request whose claim the app reads with an open or `unknown` attempt added, with `manual_only` (Section 8.2, Attempted periods).
    - a payer's record of a paid obligation without a deadline, one holding its proof: the proof kept, paid, and when the copy is older, paid never (above), `manual_only` too where the record takes it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3) (Deleting finished activity).
    - every payee's activity record when the copy is older, regardless of whether it holds a receipt, and an exact copy holding a settlement it credited, one holding its receipt: every receipt held is kept, and when the copy is older, no proof credited to its occurrence, automatically or by hand, since the deleted record may have held that credit and a user's confirmation can't show it didn't (Deleting finished activity).
    - every `payment_lists` record when the copy is older, regardless of whether it holds a `used` entry, and an exact copy holding a `used` entry: every `used` entry held is kept, and when the copy is older, every single-use endpoint of its list's version or an earlier one counted as used, and no endpoint of the list paid automatically, and one by hand only once its user has seen that the list may be stale, until its user confirms that put-back, a confirmation of that loss alone (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5, Confirming a loss).
    - the `block_floor` record: the exact authenticated deleted floor when held, never below any higher floor already seen; otherwise the conservative bound in `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.6, Recovering a deleted floor. That bound includes the clock tolerance an unblock adds. An undated delete, lost clock continuity or unrepresentable bound withholds numeric put-back and every automatic one-time dispatch or repeat; a recent response from a new service is not proof of the missing floor. The same rule applies while the delete remains current. This can hold automatic payments indefinitely; ordinary payment by hand remains subject to every other payment check, and does not clear the hold.
    - outside: the deny list and the allowlist are the switch's own lists, not records (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2): a removal from the deny list is an unblock, which the switch's rules govern, and what a block withheld in Paykit's records the block floor keeps; an exact copy of an activity record outside the protected families may be deleted (Deleting finished activity); and what an app keeps for itself alone, such as its marks of divergent records, is in no record.
  - **Payment history lost:** a payer's record put back from an older copy with `lost_to_delete` cannot be paid through again. The rule does not depend on the request's date, claim retention, the current claim's state, a provider generation or an account incarnation. An absent claim or one showing only failed attempts cannot show what payment evidence the deleted record held; provider-loss, restore, missed-item and divergent-record confirmations do not release the hold either. Erasure, reenrollment or a cooperative move cannot reset it in a record that carries the marker. This deliberately loses availability: even an unpaid request whose payment history was lost cannot be retried through that record. If another payment is intended, it needs a distinct new request or obligation, with its own identity and ordinary payment checks; replaying the old request or clearing or changing its loss marker does not create one. A put-back from the proved exact predecessor remains the existing path above: it preserves the known deleted content, including any existing loss marker, and does not add `lost_to_delete` merely because a delete occurred.
- Collection IDs and record keys are derived as in Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3. Values are sealed under the read scope's key, a label key for a labelled policy (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.5 and 13.4).
- **Destinations come only from the payee.** An app pays only to endpoints in a verified message from the payee's current app, or to the payee's public endpoints (Section 7.2). No own app can redirect a payment by writing shared state; at most it can hide records, replay older messages from the same counterparty to an app that never saw newer ones, or act within its own scopes.

## 7. Paying

### 7.1 Claims of payments

- **Obligation IDs.** Apps derive a request's obligation ID from the payee, the request and the Billing Period's start, blinded with the naming key, and a payment without a request's from its attempt ID (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3), so they all find the same claim; the service treats the claim key as opaque.
- **A second wallet** that writes `attempt` while another attempt is open gets `409 conflict` with the current state, and shows "being handled by your other wallet".
- **Deadlines:** an attempt on a one-time request carries `deadline: d + 1 ms`, where `d` is the earlier of its proposal expiry and its payment deadline. An attempt on a Billing Period carries it with `d` the period's start plus the payment deadline's `seconds`. A request with neither carries no `deadline`. `d` is taken at millisecond precision, the precision of every time comparison (Switch wire 1.6). The service rejects an `attempt` once its clock reaches the `deadline`, so `d` is the last instant at which it takes one. The first `attempt` on a claim pins its deadline, including none: the service refuses a later `attempt` that names another (`409 conflict`), and the claim view shows the pinned deadline, so apps can compare it with their own. The service thus bounds, on its own clock, when an attempt may start; the payee still checks v0.1's inclusive deadline against the verified payment time, read as for the rates of period details (Section 8.2). The proposal expiry of a recurring request bounds only its acceptance (Section 8.2).
  - **Not before the request:** the payee also bounds the verified payment time from below, as in v0.1 (`specs/payment-requests.md`, "Payment time and attribution"). Evidence whose verified payment time is earlier than the time the payee recorded its own request, by its own clock, less a tolerance its payment method sets (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment deadlines"), doesn't prove the request: the payee holds or flags it and doesn't mark the request, or the Billing Period, paid on it alone. The funds still count as received. An own app that didn't record the request itself takes as that time the request's signed `created_at`.
  - An app makes no attempt once the deadline is at or before the highest service time it has verified (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1). A transition proof that stamps an attempt at or after its deadline is misbehaviour: the app pays nothing, writes `failed` and makes no new attempt through that service until the user resolves it, as for a rollback (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1).
  - Each app computes the deadline itself, from the request, and keeps evidence for an obligation, a SETTLED proof or the signed answer that stands for one (Section 7.2, Kept proofs), at least until the later of the pinned deadline and its own, plus the 30-day reconciliation window during which the service keeps the tombstone, and longer for a claim pinned to "none" (Section 7.2 and Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), so it can still compare, upload and repair it. A pin earlier than the app's own deadline, or any pin where the app computes none, is an alert, and the obligation is doubtful: the tombstone may already be gone while the payment it records still counts. A later pin is a conformance error. Either way the service refuses this app's own `attempt`, which names its own deadline.
- **No automatic payment of doubtful claims:** a claim with an `unknown` or orphaned attempt, or whose next attempt would be flagged `possibly_duplicate` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2 row 1), is attempted again only by a user who has seen "may already be paid", never automatically.

### 7.2 Paying order

Every payment follows these steps, whether it pays a one-time request, a Billing Period, a request under an Allowance or nothing requested (Section 8.4). The one exception is a payment without a request to a public endpoint while the payer's own switch doesn't answer, which writes step 1's `attempt` after paying (Section 8.4, Without the switch).

1. **Write `attempt`**, conditioned on the version of the request record the app read (Section 6), so that a rejection or cancellation recorded since makes it fail, and on the deadline, if any (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2).
   - **Check the transfer first,** before any write. The payment method reports the asset, the amount and the largest fee it will transfer to the destination. The amount must equal the amount being paid, which the payer computes before paying, as in v0.1 (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Conversion terms"): to an endpoint of the requested asset that no rate prices, the request's amount, rounded up once to the endpoint's smallest unit (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.2); to an endpoint a rate prices, the request's fixed rates or the period details' rates (Section 8.2), the request's amount times that rate, the asset-rail rate if there is one, else the asset-wide one, rounded up once to the endpoint's smallest unit, which its payment method names; and, for a payment without a request, its own amount (Section 8.4). Vectors: `messages.json` `conversion_amounts`. The payee expects the same amount for the endpoint its proof names, by the same rule, with the smallest unit its own payment method names; a payment to an endpoint the terms leave unusable pays no occurrence, and its proof isn't credited and gets no receipt, though, as in v0.1, the payee may still show the funds received. The smallest unit belongs to the endpoint's Payment Endpoint Identifier: the payer's and the payee's payment methods name the same one for it, as both peers use the selected endpoint's precision in v0.1 (`specs/payment-conversion.md`), or a payment made by the rule pays no occurrence. A payer's payment method names the smallest unit of every endpoint it pays, and one whose unit it can't name is unusable for a payment of a request; a payee app whose payment method can't name the endpoint's smallest unit credits no proof to it. Under conversion terms, an endpoint of another asset that no rate prices is unusable for the payment, and so is one whose identifier isn't of the form `<asset>-<rail>-<format>`, whose asset can't be told, as v0.1 takes conversion only under that convention (`specs/payment-conversion.md`); a destination of the requested asset that no rate prices needs no confirmation of rates, also when the payment method orders a priced one first, and is paid automatically only where it otherwise may be; a request without conversion terms promises no rate, so the payer pays it to an endpoint of another asset only by hand, by its own calculation, which its user confirms, and the payee judges what it received by its own, as in v0.1; an app may decline such payments. A payment priced by a rate is made only by hand (Section 8.2): its user confirms the amount so computed, in the endpoint's asset, and the payer pays exactly that, by the rates it showed. Period details that arrive after the confirmation don't change it, and when the rates it confirmed stop being usable it pays nothing until its user confirms again, since v0.1 never switches an approved amount or quote silently. A destination without an amount, such as an address or an amount-less invoice or offer, is paid exactly that amount. A destination that fixes another amount or asset, or whose amount the method can't tell before paying, is unusable for automatic payment; a manual payment shows the amount it would transfer and pays it only on the user's explicit choice. Fees don't count against Allowance limits, which count the amount paid, as in v0.1. A fee the payee can influence through the destination it chose must stay within a cap the payer app sets; above it, the destination is unusable for automatic payment, and a manual payment needs the user's choice.
   - **Reserve a single-use endpoint** in the same batch: when the destination is a single-use endpoint of a Private Payment List, the batch also writes the `payment_lists` record marking it used, at its next version (Section 8.4), whatever obligation is paid. The list's generation is checked against the payee's fresh `identity.json` (Section 8.4, No usable list) before this batch is submitted, not in parallel with it, so a list the check refuses has nothing reserved and no version consumed. If that write fails its version check, nothing is written: the app re-reads the record, and pays again to the next usable endpoint or asks for a newer list. An executor's batch adds its ledger and activity writes (Section 8.3), a payment without a request its activity record (Section 8.4), an attempt on a Billing Period the request record doesn't list as attempted the write of the request record that adds it (Section 8.2), an attempt on a Billing Period paid without its user's confirmation the write of its period record at the version read (Section 8.2), and an attempt its user started by hand the write of `manual_only` on the occurrence's record (Section 8.3, Manual only).

   In parallel with the batch:
   - **check the destination:** the message or record carrying the destination verifies as in Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2, carries the destination, and hasn't expired: a record at its `expires_at` or its `updated_at` plus the payee identity's record age limit, a Private Payment List at its `expires_at` or 30 days after the payer's inbox accepted it (Section 5 and Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2); a record also names the app whose path it was read from. A request or period details carrying it name the obligation being paid by their signed fields: the request is the one paid, and period details name it first in `refs` and the Billing Period paid; a Private Payment List isn't tied to one obligation. The app that sent the request, period details or Private Payment List, or published the public endpoint, is current in the payee's `identity.json`, cached for at most 10 minutes (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 4.2 and 4.3), and, for a public endpoint, the higher of the Homeserver copy and the payee's switch's copy when the payer had to fetch it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3). If that lookup failed or hasn't answered, a manual payment needs the user's explicit confirmation before any write, an automatic one isn't made, and an answer that arrives before money moves is still used; a payment to a Private Payment List is refused instead, since the list's app and generation are judged on that copy (Section 8.4, No usable list);
   - revalidate the app's own `identity.json` if needed (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3).

   All three must succeed before any money moves. If any of them fails, the app writes `failed` instead of paying. An executor checks its Allowance again at this point (Section 8.3), and a payment priced by period details' rates checks that they are still usable (Section 8.2). An automatic payment isn't attempted at all while the device clock and the service's differ by more than the clock tolerance (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1, Clock bound).

   **Dispatch window.** The owner sets the attempt's `orphan_after` to at least the longest its payment method can take to complete or fail, plus the time it allows itself between the `attempt` and the dispatch (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2). It dispatches only while that allowance hasn't passed since the service accepted the `attempt` and its own certificate hasn't expired, judged with no wait between the judgment and the dispatch; past it, it submits nothing and writes `failed`. It repeats an idempotent dispatch only while the method's longest time still fits before the orphan age. The owner's own revocation inside the allowance isn't seen without a read; the user's confirmation that Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2 rows 5 and 6 require before another app resolves the attempt, and the `possibly_duplicate` flag (Section 7.3), cover it.
2. **Pay.** For a one-time request whose record holds no response yet (Section 8.1, Rejecting and cancelling), send the acceptance at the same time, without waiting for it. The acceptance becomes sendable only once the payment method's call is made, a resume finds the payment in the method's history, or the user marks as paid (`done`) a payment that may have been dispatched: never while a check before the dispatch can still stop it, and never for a payment the method reported failed, so a payee never sees an acceptance of a payment that never ran. The one exception is a payer record paused as divergent: the acceptance then waits with step 3's write (step 4).
3. **Write `done`** (basis `owner`) and update the activity record, in one batch. If the payment failed, write `failed` on the claim instead, leaving the activity record as it is.
4. **Send the proof** to the payee. While the payer record is paused as divergent, step 3's write, the claim's `done` with it, the proof and a one-time request's acceptance not yet sent wait until its user confirms, and go once the user has confirmed and the messages kept over the record are written, also while its view still shows it divergent (Section 6, Divergent records); a `failed` outcome, which writes nothing over the record, goes at once, alone. The attempt stays open meanwhile, so another own app frees it only once it is orphaned and its user confirms (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2 row 6), and then pays the obligation only by hand, the new attempt flagged `possibly_duplicate` (Sections 7.1 and 7.3), as for any open attempt.

- **Kept proofs:** before attempting, an app compares the claim with any SETTLED proof it keeps for that claim (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3). That proof is the transition proof of the entry that settled the claim, from the answer to its own write or a feed item that carried it (Switch wire 7.5). An app that got no such proof, such as one that first sees the claim once its entries are stubs, keeps instead the signed answer of its read showing the claim SETTLED (Switch wire 7.1 and 7.4.8), and keeps and compares that the same way; it uploads none, since the Switch's uploads take proofs, heads and operations and no answer of a read (Switch wire 7.4.14). A service that shows FREE, or nothing, for a claim the app holds a signed SETTLED proof for is treated as a rollback (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1): the app makes no attempt through it and alerts the user. The one exception is a tombstone the service may already have deleted (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3): from its pinned deadline plus the 30-day reconciliation window, or, for a claim pinned to "none", from 400 days after the proof's entry, the time the switch keeps that tombstone after its last entry, which is no earlier. The app keeps the proof until the later of the bounds that apply: the later of the pinned deadline and its own, where there is one, plus the window; and, for a pin to "none", the 400 days. A pinned deadline where the app computes none (its request sets no deadline) is an early pin, and the app keeps that proof for good, since it may still pay the obligation. While the service may have deleted the tombstone but the app still keeps the proof, the pin differs from the app's own deadline (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2), early or "none" beside a deadline, so the app alerts and still treats the obligation as paid. A bound has passed, and the app may drop the proof, only when it lies before the earlier of the highest verified service time and the device clock plus 24 hours, so one far-future signed time can't make the app drop its evidence. Until then the app keeps it and compares it with the claim, and uploads a proof in a move or after a provider loss (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 12.5 and 12.6), when a faulty old service may have left the tombstone out.
- **The record shows it paid:** an app never attempts an obligation whose activity record it holds already carries the proof of a payment, whatever the claim shows. A claim without a deadline is gone 400 days after its last entry (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), and then the record, not the claim, stops a second payment, for example of a re-sent copy of the request. So the payer never deletes the record of a paid obligation without a deadline; the user may only archive it (Section 6).
- The **attempt ID** is random. It is recorded in the app's payment journal before the write, and used as the payment method's idempotency key where the method supports one.
- **Only a verified answer lets money move.** If the answer to the `attempt` is lost or its service signature doesn't verify, the app doesn't pay; it re-reads the claim and continues from what a verified answer shows. The same holds for an answer whose transition proofs don't bind the batch: one proof per operation, each naming its operation by its stub's item hash, and its object (Switch wire 6.5). Before paying, the attempt's proof must also show the app's own attempt: the claim ATTEMPTING, with this app as owner and the attempt ID it wrote current and open, at the step after the one it expected. Otherwise the app pays nothing and writes `failed`. A signed answer that fails these checks is misbehaviour, treated as a rollback (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1).
- **Starting to pay** is recorded in the journal before the payment begins. After a crash, an app whose journal shows no payment started writes `failed`; otherwise it resolves the attempt from the payment method's history (Section 7.3). This crash rule holds only for the app's current journal. A journal restored from a backup may predate a payment made after the backup, so a restored app treats its open attempts as Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4 says.
- **What a restore loses.** A restore from a backup brings back the app's own state as it was at the backup (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4): what the app kept only in its own state since is gone, and so are the entries it passed since. The Switch recovers every lasting effect from claims and shared records, but a lasting effect kept only in the app's own state is in neither, such as a stop kept from a verified `payment_request_cancellation` or `allowance_end` whose record write had failed or was still pending (Sections 8.1 to 8.3). Cursor moves aren't in the chain (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.3), but the service reports with every feed page the cursor it holds for the app, and the Switch says when that report shows a restore clean: no entry after the backup's position was passed, and none the app could still need is lost (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4, The held cursor). After a clean restore the app resumes: it holds no request and no Allowance for the restore, asks its user nothing, and sends no `resend_request` for it. After any other restore from a backup, one whose held cursor is ahead of the backup's position or unknown, the app treats every open request and every Allowance as possibly stopped: it makes no automatic attempt or acceptance on such a request or under such an Allowance, and a manual attempt only after its user has seen that the payee may have cancelled it; resuming an acceptance whose original delivery is proved still pending, as below, is no new acceptance. Once authorized under Section 3.1, Outbound authorization, it sends each counterparty of an open request or Allowance, but a blocked one (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties), a `resend_request` with `accepted_from` at the `accepted_at` of the last entry its backup's cursor passed, less the clock tolerance, and keeps a cancellation or end that comes back as Sections 8.1 to 8.3 say. A blocked counterparty is asked once it is unblocked, which brings back only what that sender still keeps (Section 9). A restore that isn't clean also marks every counterparty its deny list holds at its next read of the lists as blocked, from the backup's cursor time less the clock tolerance, or the restore's bound on the times it restores where that is earlier, to that read (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1), and, in the commit that applies those marks, raises the block floor to that read's time (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties). What such a counterparty's inbox accepted since is then asked for once it is unblocked. A sender blocked and unblocked again between the backup and that read is in no mark the backup carries and not in the list the restore reads, and the inbox entries the device the backup came from read are deleted (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2): what its inbox accepted while it was blocked is asked for only by the restore's other re-sends, which ask the counterparty of an open request or Allowance, and is otherwise lost when its sender stops keeping it, 30 days after its acceptance. Such a sender's one-time request that comes back opens paid only by hand, by the floor, so none of it is paid automatically. The Paykit SDK can't rebuild who was blocked in that stretch: the feed shows the deny list's changes to an app holding `lists.read` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.3), but the client hands them to no protocol. Before any restored flush or reconstruction, the app needs current evidence that it still owes the exact original delivery, including any disposition since the backup. For example, before an in-place restore the host can establish from the uninterrupted original store that it still owes that exact delivery, provided it has exclusive custody through the restore and no other copy could have disposed of it. A signed message in a shared record proves intent. Neither that record, an outbox entry copied from a backup, a clean feed cursor nor the absence of a terminal notice establishes that no later delivery or give-up occurred. With only those sources, even within the replay window, the app holds the message, makes no automatic delivery and does not call `App::queue_stored`. It keeps the outcome as possibly delivered and tells the host to reconcile it under Deliveries of unknown outcome. If current evidence establishes that the original delivery remains pending, the app may resume it with its original signed bytes and replay deadline, only after listing the relevant records, obtaining their scope keys and applying all current send rules. An answer or later stop suppresses the send. A receipt stored without sending is never sent this way. Period details additionally require a readable period record without a receipt and a readable request record without rejection or cancellation. If a required record cannot be read, the app waits and checks again on receive until the original replay deadline. A copy reconstructed from stored plaintext may go only until 30 days after its `created_at`, less the clock tolerance (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 5.3 and 11). The hold on a request or an Allowance ends when its record shows it cancelled or ended, which stops it for good and which no user confirmation overrides, or when the user confirms it. A counterparty that never answers leaves the hold in place until the user confirms; nothing releases it automatically. A stop the backup holds still applies, and no confirmation overrides it either: the hold is for stops that may have come after the backup. The rule binds a restore the app knows of, because the app made it; a store rolled back without the app knowing, such as a device image restored underneath it, shows, to an app whose devices don't share its cursor, as a held cursor ahead of its own position, which the app handles as a `missed` item (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.3, Ahead; What a `missed` item loses, below); for an app whose devices share its cursor nothing in the Pubky Switch detects it (Section 17, question 28). The backup also carries the app's marks of divergent records, which the restore installs unconfirmed (Section 6, Divergent records). A restored publishing app whose backup holds its list or a list it sent holds the list and the counterparties of its backup: a list change or a withdrawal made since is gone. So it sends no list but a withdrawal until its host sets its list again, which the host does once it has re-applied what the user changed, its withdrawals first (Section 8.4, Withdrawing a list), and it tells its host so once. Every Private Payment List the restored outbox still carries, empty as well as nonempty, is taken out of it before the host can send it. The app durably retains the obligation to withdraw from that counterparty before removing the carried delivery, also suppressing a carried copy that a kept batch could later queue. Once its certificate, current identity and restore first-read guards permit delivery, it sends a newly signed empty version reserved under Section 8.4.1, then sends the list again when its host sets it, unless its host withdrew it since. A carried empty withdrawal cannot discharge this obligation: a later nonempty version may have reached the counterparty after the backup, even when the restore verdict is clean. If the app cannot establish or publish a valid newer version yet, the withdrawal remains owed and the carried list stays suppressed. This replaces only carried list deliveries; owed receipts, proofs and other messages remain subject to their own send rules, and every delivery is gated before the restored app can send it (Section 8.1, Messages already queued). Until then what is owed waits, and a payer whose list expires meanwhile has none from it until then.
- **What a `missed` item loses.** A `missed` item shows that the service deleted entries wrapped to the app before it read them (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), and the app can't check it, so a payee's `payment_request_cancellation` or a counterparty's `allowance_end` may have been among them. In the commit that passes the item, the app holds every open request and every Allowance as after a restore that isn't clean (What a restore loses, above): it makes no automatic attempt or acceptance on such a request or under such an Allowance, makes no repeat at a resume of a dispatch made before the item, since a repeat is an automatic payment too (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties), and makes a manual attempt only after its user has seen that the payee may have cancelled it. A confirmation its user gave before the item releases nothing; a request first seen after the item is held only if its signed `created_at` is earlier than the item's last acceptance time plus the clock tolerance, since a later request can't have had its cancellation among the missed entries. The item neither sets nor lifts a pause of a divergent record, nor undoes a confirmation of one: it loses inbox entries, not the records' views, which the listing after it reads again (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2; Section 8.1, Records not listed yet). The requests and Allowances the app holds stay held until the record shows a stop or the user confirms, as after a restore, by a confirmation given after the item: nothing releases them automatically (Section 17, question 28). The app asks each counterparty of an open request or Allowance, and of its unfinished activity records, to re-send as Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2 says, but a blocked one, which it asks once it is unblocked, bringing back only what that sender still keeps (Section 9; Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties), Paykit narrowing that duty, never for a time before its cursor's time less the clock tolerance (Section 9). In the commit that passes the item, the app also keeps the item's window, from its earliest to its last acceptance time, and marks every counterparty its deny list holds at its next read of the lists as blocked over that window, for the same ask, raising the block floor to the earlier of the item's last acceptance time and the service time of the read that passes it, in the commit that applies those marks, when it makes any (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties).
- **The restored certificate.** A Paykit App checks every certificate it runs under by Paykit's own scope and permission rules (Sections 3.1 and 3.2), which are stricter than the Switch's. At a restore it checks its backup's certificate before the restore writes anything, and doesn't restore from a backup whose certificate fails them. A certificate it takes afterwards, such as the renewed one its `identity.json` lists (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4), it checks before it acts under it, and it takes no Paykit action under a certificate that fails them: it sends nothing, pays nothing, and holds the entries it receives, unprocessed, until a certificate that passes replaces it. It still keeps its marks of divergent records meanwhile, setting one for each record it sees divergent: they are its own state, which acts on nothing, and they hold once a certificate that passes replaces it (Section 6, Divergent records). At a join, it checks the approval's certificate before it keeps anything.
- **Failed means definitively not paid.** Once a payment may have been submitted to the payment method, the owner MUST NOT write `failed` without authoritative evidence that it did not and cannot settle. A timeout, a disconnection, a crash or an ambiguous result MUST keep the attempt open: it blocks any replacement payment, keeps its Allowance reservation, and is resolved only through Section 7.3. As in the v0.1 SDK, the owner reports one of three outcomes: definitive failure, verified success, or nothing yet.
- **No second execution.** An app never carries out an attempt a second time, after a crash or otherwise, unless the payment method's idempotency on the attempt ID makes a repeat harmless or the journal shows the payment never started. When the method's history shows any record of the attempt, the app resolves the attempt from that record and never carries it out again, idempotent or not.
- **What a payment method declares:** each integration states whether it is idempotent on the attempt ID, and for how long it remembers the key; whether its history can show the outcome of a dispatch, and for how long it keeps it; and whether that history is authoritative across a restore of the paying device, because it lives with the account and not only on the device. An empty history means the payment never started only within the history's retention, counted from when the attempt was journaled, and, after a restore, only for a history that is authoritative across one. A dispatch is repeated only on an idempotent method, within its retention, and never from a restored journal. Otherwise a doubtful dispatch stays open, "may be paid", and is resolved only by a receipt or by the user. An owner that finds a payment it reported failed completed after all sends its proof, so the payee's receipt can correct the claim. The service keeps the claim for 30 days after its deadline (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3); a receipt after that can't be recorded on it, and an app that gets it and holds the failure shows the user that a payment marked failed was paid. An app holds the failure when it has it from a stable source: the attempt's owner from its own journal, or any own app, the owner included, from a view of the claim, kept or read, that shows the attempt `failed`. An owner restored from a backup that predates the failure has no journal entry for it, and uses such a view as any other own app does. This decides only the warning: how an Allowance ledger counts the attempt (Section 8.3) doesn't change. The record's `status` is informational (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3) and never decides it. An own app that never saw the failure, such as one that joined after the service deleted the claim, can't tell and shows nothing. Either way the app records the receipt in its activity record (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.11). It takes the claim as gone once the switch holds none, or once the claim's pinned deadline plus 30 days is at or before the highest service time it has verified.
- **Doubtful window:** only a crash between paying and writing `done`, an outcome the method can't report, or a restore whose payment history can't tell (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4), leaves doubt. It always shows as "may be paid".

### 7.3 Reconciliation

- **Orphaned attempts** (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2) stay open until reconciled.
- **After a provider loss** (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6), an obligation is uncertain when its request or Billing Period was payable before the declaration plus the residual window. Apps treat a request as made before the loss in either of two cases: their inbox accepted it, putting it in their local state, before the end of the residual window (`uncertain_until_ms`); or its signed `created_at` is earlier than that end plus the clock tolerance. A request created inside that window could still be attempted at the old service, which the declaration can't stop (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6, Residual risk). The obligations of such a request are uncertain, except those of Billing Periods that start after the declaration plus the residual window: no app attempts a period automatically before its start. A payee that misdates a re-sent request falls outside this (Section 13). An uncertain obligation stays in doubt at the new service, whichever own app attempted it or may have, this app included, until its claim there shows it SETTLED or its user states that it wasn't paid before the loss, a statement that holds for that loss only, so a newer loss puts the obligation in doubt again: no app pays it automatically, and a payment of it by hand first shows the user that it may already be paid and goes ahead only on the user's confirmation. An app keeps the loss's declaration time and the end of its residual window (`declared_at_ms` and `uncertain_until_ms`, Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6) for this, and a backup carries them: an app restored from a backup taken before it learned of the loss learns them again when it follows the loss (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6). An obligation it can't place before or after them, such as one whose request it can't date, is in doubt. This user confirmation does not lift the separate permanent hold of a payer record put back from an older copy with `lost_to_delete` (Section 6, Payment history lost).
- **Resolutions** (the rows are those of the claim table of Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2):

| Resolution | Who | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `done`, basis `evidence` (row 3) | Any own app with `claims.write` | The attempt the evidence names is done; claim SETTLED |
| `done`, basis `reconcile` (row 4) | Any own app with `claims.write`, user-confirmed, orphaned attempt | Attempt done; claim SETTLED |
| `unknown` (row 6) | Any own app with `claims.write`, user-confirmed, orphaned attempt | Attempt unknown; claim FREE; the next attempt is flagged `possibly_duplicate` |
| `failed` on an `unknown` attempt, or on an orphaned open attempt whose owner isn't current (row 5) | Its owner while current; otherwise any own app with `claims.write`, user-confirmed | Attempt failed; its Allowance amount stays counted, and is released only as Section 8.3 (`failed` isn't final) says; from a non-owner, the next attempt is flagged `possibly_duplicate` |
| The owner's later report (rows 2 and 5) | Owner of an `unknown` attempt, while current | Done or failed, as reported |

- **The evidence a resolution cites** (`evidence_ref`, Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2): for basis `evidence`, the hash of the payee's `payment_receipt` message JSON; for basis `owner` and `reconcile`, the hash of the payer's `payment_proof` message JSON. Before writing `done` by evidence, the app verifies the receipt: its issuer, its request and Billing Period, and that it confirms the proof of the attempt. A receipt carries no amount: the amount is the one that proof reports paid, the request's or the period's, or the proof's own for a payment without a request.
- **Only the payee's yes counts.** A receipt can settle an attempt, because the payee confirms it was paid. Nothing from the payee shows that an attempt was not paid: a payee gains from a second payment, and may not have seen the first one yet. A reminder, a re-sent request or a `resend_request` is no evidence either way.
- Features that account for payments, such as Allowance budgets (Section 8.3), derive their accounting from the outcome of each attempt.
- **An app that isn't current can write nothing,** not even outcomes. Its attempts are reconciled through receipts or by other apps.
- **Graceful removal:** the signer warns before revoking an app, or letting its certificate lapse (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.2), while it still owns open attempts, which `GET /v1/{id}/apps` lists. Apps with `paykit_allowance` warn when an Allowance's executor stops being current (Section 8.3).

## 8. Payment flows and features

### 8.1 One-time request

1. The payee sends `payment_request`, usually with its destination, admitted by the payer's allowlist, for example after an invite, and writes its `payee_activity` request record in parallel.
2. The payer's apps with `paykit_pay` receive it, with a push. The first to process it writes the `payer_activity` request record.
3. One app pays, following Section 7.2.
4. The payee verifies the proof by its own method, updates its `payee_activity` record to paid, and stores a `payment_receipt` there, which it may send. A settlement bucket must first satisfy the authenticated creation and incarnation rules of wire 5.7, also for a fresh reader, restore and provider recovery. Every credit batch binds the required absence or exact current bucket incarnation at application, including a receipt-only batch that omits the bucket write; unproved provenance or a refused condition permits no credit or receipt, even by hand. A payee app credits one payment to one occurrence at most, as in v0.1 ("A single payment must not satisfy multiple requests or periods", `specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"): it credits a proof only if no other record of the same counterparty, of a request, a Billing Period or a payment without a request (Section 8.4), holds a proof of the same settlement, as its payment method names it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5), with a receipt. A payee app that credits a proof, to a request, a Billing Period or as a payment without a request, stores its receipt in that record, also when it sends none, so the record shows the credit. A credit also writes the settlement's mark, unless it is made by hand over a bucket that can't take it (Section 6, Settlement marks): the batch that stores the receipt writes the settlement's bucket in `settlements` at the version the app read, `{0, 0}` for a bucket not yet written, with the mark added, naming the occurrence credited (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3; `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7), so of two own payee apps that credit one settlement at once, to one occurrence or two, of one counterparty or two, labelled or not, only one batch applies. The credit is complete, and shown so, only once the batch that stores its receipt applies, with the mark or, by hand, without one; when the service refuses that batch for the storage quota or the value limit (`429 quota_exceeded` or `413 too_large`), also once the bucket's write is left out, nothing is credited and no receipt is sent: the proof stays pending, in its record without a receipt or as a stored entry (Stored entries, below), the app tells its host of the refusal, and once room frees it decides the credit again (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Complete). Before it credits, the app reads the settlement's bucket at its switch, never deciding from its copy or a backup alone, and credits nothing, whatever its user confirms, while the bucket holds a mark of the settlement or the app has seen one; while the bucket doesn't open or read, is invalid or divergent, or the read fails, the settlement may already be credited: the app credits it never automatically, and by hand only once its user has been shown that it may be, and tells its host once per settlement (`CreditNeedsUser`); while the bucket's current write is a delete, or it carries `lost_to_delete`, a settlement the app has seen no mark of is credited only on the evidence Section 6, Settlement marks, lists, its payment method's verified bound or an offer it issued after every retained and pending delete, never under an `unknown` boundary, and otherwise neither automatically nor by hand, whatever its user confirms, the app telling its host once per settlement it holds, and why (`CreditHeldForDelete`); the app retains and unions every observed deletion boundary, with a pending delete resolved only by the authenticated complete-restoration rule (Section 6, Settlement marks), and this rule goes before every by-hand path above and below, a full bucket, a refused write and a provider loss included. A proof of a request or a Billing Period whose payment method gives no settlement ID (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5) has no mark, and is credited only by hand, once its user has been shown that it may already be credited, the host told (`CreditNeedsUser`); one of a payment without a request isn't credited (Section 8.4). When its batch fails on the bucket (`409 conflict`), the app takes the bucket's current write and decides again: another own app may have credited this settlement, or another one in the bucket. If the bucket now holds a mark of the settlement, the settlement is credited already: if the mark names this occurrence, another own app credited it, and the app takes that app's write; otherwise the app stores and sends no receipt for this occurrence, whose record keeps the proof without one, and shows its user that the settlement was credited to another occurrence. If not, it adds its mark to that write and tries again. After a provider loss, a settlement whose payment the app can't date after the declaration plus the residual window (Section 7.3) may have been credited at the lost service and its mark lost with it: the app credits it only once its user confirms, having been shown that it may already be credited, also when the new service holds no mark of it, the host told once per settlement (`CreditNeedsUser`); a mark the app holds from before the loss counts as there, and the app uploads its bucket (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6). One transaction is one settlement, so a payer pays two occurrences in two transactions, as `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5 says for payments without a request. So an occurrence whose record holds the proof and no receipt, beside another record holding a proof of that settlement with a receipt, isn't credited, and every own payee app that holds both records reads it so. An app that holds a record of that counterparty it can't open, or one whose view shows it divergent (Section 6, Divergent records), or whose copy of the records may be incomplete (Records not listed yet, below), credits no such proof until it can tell, or until its user confirms crediting it, having been shown that it may already be credited, and a user's confirmation of a divergent record of the counterparty counts as one only if it showed the user that the record may hold a credit of a settlement its current write lacks; for this it checks every record of its `payee_activity` collection it holds, and one it can't read as a record of a counterparty, deleted, unopened or unbound, counts as one of that counterparty's while it is paused, divergent or not confirmed for a credit; an app holding only labelled selling scopes reads only its labels' records, so it checks a settlement against those alone, and the settlement's mark covers the others. A payer whose record holds that proof pays the occurrence again only after a new request. The mark derives from the settlement alone, so a settlement credited to one counterparty's occurrence isn't credited to another counterparty's either.

- **Destination:** a request with `payment_endpoints` is paid only to those. One with `required_app_id` instead is paid only to that payee app's Private Payment List or public endpoints, and that app must be current; a request carrying both fields is invalid. One with neither, for example from an app that receives no payments itself, is paid to any of the payee's current Private Payment Lists or public endpoints, under the rules of Section 8.4, including single-use reservation and the explicit choice between private and public payment; the proof names the endpoint it used.
- **Rejecting and cancelling:** a payer app records its rejection or cancellation in the request record, in a batch conditioned on the step of the request's claim it read (`claim_step`), which it writes only while that claim shows no open, `unknown` or paid attempt, and sends it only after that batch succeeds. An attempt is conditioned on the request record (Section 7.2), so one own app can't reject a request while another pays it. A payer app sends at most one response, an acceptance or a rejection, to a request, as in v0.1 (`specs/payment-requests.md`, "State derivation"), and only while the request record holds neither a response nor a cancellation: after an acceptance it may cancel, after a rejection it neither accepts nor cancels, and after a cancellation it sends no response. It judges this from the request record, so a response another own app recorded counts, and sends none while its copy may lack that record or can't open it (Records not listed yet, below). A recurring request is answered and cancelled only as Section 8.2 says, conditioned on its Billing Periods' claims, never on a one-time obligation.
- **Blocked counterparties:** blocking pauses what is open with a counterparty and revokes nothing, as in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Block A Peer"). While the deny list holds a counterparty (Pubky Switch spec, Section 7), a payer app, which always holds `paykit_contacts` (Section 3.1), makes no automatic payment or acceptance for it, and accepts a subscription from it, pays its open requests and Billing Periods, and makes a payment without a request to it, only after its user confirms, having shown the user that the counterparty is blocked. Where v0.1 ran nothing for a blocked counterparty ("No intake, send, or private automation then runs for that counterparty", `specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Block A Peer"), a v0.2 payer app may still accept and pay for it on its user's confirmation; an app sends a blocked counterparty no list, renewal, answer, re-send request, list request or extension message, and asks it again for what it still owes to ask once it is unblocked (Sections 8.4 and 9). An answer is also a rejection of a request and the payer's cancellation of one, and each is a stop: while its copy of the deny list holds the payee, or that copy isn't known after it tried to read the lists, the app still records it in the request record, on the same conditions as otherwise, so that no own app pays or accepts the request, and holds only its message, which it sends from that record once that copy is known and doesn't hold the payee, at its first receive at which its records are listed, none waits for a scope key and the request record reads; a message still held 30 days after its `created_at`, less the clock tolerance, is never sent, and the app tells the host that it gave it up (`MessageGivenUp`) at its first receive past that time at which that copy is known and doesn't hold the payee, its records are listed, none waits for a scope key and the request record reads, the record still holding it. While the request record is paused as divergent and its user hasn't confirmed it, a delete included, the app decides nothing on it about the held message; once the record reads unpaused and holds neither that message nor any stop, the app gives the message up, telling the host (`MessageGivenUp`), so the user may answer again. An Allowance's proposal, acceptance and rejection require contacts authorization and are refused when it is missing or sends are blocked, recording and sending nothing; its end follows its separate stop rules (Sections 3.1 and 8.3, Blocked counterparties). Nor does an app holding `paykit_contacts` send a new request, or period details for a subscription, to a counterparty its copy shows blocked, or while its copy isn't known. What stops or settles a payment still goes when the app first sends it, though not as a restore's re-send (Section 7.2, What a restore loses), but for a publishing app's withdrawal of its list, which Section 8.4 sends again: a payee's cancellation of a request it sent, a publishing app's withdrawal of its list, a user's `allowance_end` (Section 8.3, Blocked counterparties), a receipt, and the acceptance and proof of a payment dispatched before the block or on the user's confirmation, which report money sent or being sent. A resume's repeat of a dispatched payment (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares) is an automatic payment too: the user's confirmation was for the first dispatch, so the app makes no repeat while the deny list holds the payee, and the attempt stays open. Unblocking ends the pause. A message from a blocked counterparty that would create state is dropped as one never processed and counted in a recovery mark, from which the app asks again once the counterparty is unblocked (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1; Section 9). Recovery is best effort within the mark's lifetime and the sender's retention; the mark's retirement and host-notice rules do not guarantee that the message returns. A one-time request that comes back this way is paid only by hand, whichever payer app reads it first, but for the case below. The re-sent request is routed to every current payer app (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.1), one newly added or holding no mark of its sender among them, so the cutoff is the identity's: the **block floor**, one `block_floor` record under `paykit_pay` (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.6), which every app that pays holds (Section 3.1). The floor only rises and never expires; a request created after it is dated above it. The floor is raised only as a blocked window closes, by each app once, or as a widening applies, never per message and never at the ask. A Paykit App holding `paykit_pay` that removes a sender from the deny list first raises the floor to the service time of its latest verified read plus the clock tolerance, building on the version it holds (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.6), and writes the unblock only once that write has succeeded; if the raise fails, it doesn't unblock, which fails closed, since a floor a little high only holds more requests for payment by hand. An app without it writes the unblock as any other app does, and each app holding `paykit_pay` raises the floor in the commit that records it. An app without `paykit_pay` raises nothing and holds nothing for the floor. For an unblock it didn't write, an app holding `paykit_pay` raises the floor in the commit in which it records the unblock, its read of the lists showing the sender gone from the deny list that held it, to the service time of its latest verified read plus the clock tolerance, before it processes any further entry of that sender and before it queues or sends any ask; and a restore that isn't clean raises it to the time of its first read of the lists, and a `missed` item to the earlier of the item's last acceptance time and the service time of the read that passes it, each in the commit that applies its widening (Section 7.2, What a restore loses and What a `missed` item loses). A clean restore marks no blocked sender and raises nothing for the restore, since no entry after the backup's position was passed, so nothing a blocked sender sent since was read and lost. The floor holds every one-time request of every sender dated before it, so it isn't raised while a window is open: then every own app that pays holds `paykit_contacts` (Section 3.1), sees the sender on its deny list and pays nothing for it automatically, and a blocked sender can't move the user's other one-time requests to payment by hand by sending more. Each unblock does move them: every one-time request of any sender still unpaid and created before it is paid only by hand, even when the blocked sender sent nothing. A copy that comes back once the window has closed, asked for or not, re-sent by its sender's own choice or answering another app's ask, finds the floor raised in every own app holding `paykit_pay` that saw the block, since a Paykit App holding `paykit_pay` raises it before it writes an unblock, and each app holding `paykit_pay` raises it at the unblock its read of the lists shows before it takes that sender's entries, and the raise is in the identity's chain before any entry the app takes after it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.3, One order per generation). What remains uncovered: an unblock that no app holding `paykit_pay` raised the floor before writing, one written outside Paykit or by an app without `paykit_pay`, leaves an own app that joins after it, before any own app holding `paykit_pay` that saw the block has raised the floor, with neither a deny list entry nor a mark of that sender, and a copy that comes back to it then opens unmarked. A block mark that goes, for the bound or its own expiry, needs no raise of its own: the unblock that closes its window, or the widening that made it, raises the floor. Until its raise is applied, an app holding `paykit_pay` holds that sender's entries unprocessed and asks it nothing, building the raise again on the floor read again (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.6); a widening whose raise isn't applied isn't applied either; the app applies both together at its next receive. A payer app opens the record of a one-time request whose signed `created_at` is earlier than its sender's block mark's latest time or the floor, plus the clock tolerance, with `manual_only` (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3), also when that record is written later. A record a read opened without it, at an entry whose read took a raise the entry's step didn't see yet, it marks once the read is done. The mark is never lifted (Section 8.3, Manual only), and every own app sees it, a restore included. Whatever a record holds, no app pays a one-time request so dated automatically: no executor pays it (Section 8.3, Executor), and a resume makes no repeat of its payment, since the repeat would be an automatic payment, nor a repeat of an automatic attempt's payment of a request whose record holds `manual_only` (Section 8.3, Manual only); the attempt stays open until the payment method's history or a receipt settles it. This is judged at the dispatch, not at the attempt's commit: immediately before each automatic dispatch of a one-time request, an executor's payment and a resume's repeat alike, the app reads the `block_floor` record again from its switch, as it reads the deny list again (How fresh the deny list is, below), and judges the request against the higher of the floor that read shows and the one it holds; it waits for no raise. A read that fails, or a record that doesn't read with none seen before, never counts as a floor that doesn't hold the request: the app makes no automatic dispatch of it on that read. So a raise applied before the attempt's commit, which the attempt's batch doesn't condition on, and one applied after that commit but before the dispatch, are both caught by that read. A raise applied after the dispatch began is too late for that payment, which was made under the floor as the read before it showed. While an app can't read the floor (its value sealed under a key the app doesn't hold, its payer records not listed yet, or a value that doesn't read with none seen before), every one-time request counts as so dated, and an unblock or a widening that must raise the floor is held until the app can read it. The floor holds a one-time request of any sender dated before it, which is the conservative side: one still unpaid when the floor passes its date is paid only by hand. A recurring request that comes back is taken as new, with no mark. A proof, receipt, list or reply that comes back is taken as any other.
