The app tells its host, once per sender and window and with no content, when a mark's window expires before an ask for it was delivered, when a bound on marks drops a mark of any family before an ask covering its latest time was delivered (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound), and, at a window's first ask, for the part of it older than its sender keeps: what the counterparty's inbox accepted in that window can no longer come back, and a payment among it shows only in the payment method.
- **Messages already queued:** the send rules for a blocked counterparty apply when each delivery or re-send is attempted, including a message queued before the block, a retry after a delivery failure, a message a kept batch will queue, and a message carried by reload or restore. Queuing or signing a message while sends were permitted does not authorize its later delivery. This rule applies wherever this specification requires the app to judge the deny list; it does not grant `lists.read` to an app whose scopes do not include it. The app uses the same deny decision, including its own pending block or unblock, as How fresh the deny list is, below. Before attempting delivery of a message governed by these counterparty holds, it refreshes a copy at least 10 minutes old or not known. A failed refresh leaves that send held; it does not turn an unknown list into an empty one or authorize delivery from a stale copy. A block the app wrote itself holds these messages at once, before another flush or resume can deliver them. A deny change or gap the app has learned takes effect before any subsequent delivery decision. When reload, restore or provider loss leaves the copy unknown, a successful current read is required before a queued message governed by these holds can go.
  - The app holds nonempty Private Payment Lists, renewals and list answers, list requests, re-send requests, new Payment Requests and Billing Period details, subscription acceptances that do not report a dispatched payment and do not carry the exact user confirmation described below, rejections and payer cancellations, Allowance proposals, acceptances and rejections, and an Allowance end the app generates when a proposer's withdrawal crosses its acceptance while sends to that counterparty are held. For that generated end, the durable end and own-app notice still apply at once (Section 8.3, Ending). The same rule applies to their already queued copies and re-sends. A message whose role or effect the app cannot establish is not treated as an exception: it waits until the app can decide under these rules. A user's confirmation to pay a blocked counterparty authorizes only the acceptance and proof reporting that payment, not unrelated queued messages.
  - A payee's cancellation of a request, an eligible `allowance_end` initiated by the user under Section 8.3, a receipt already owed, the acceptance and proof of a payment dispatched before the block or on its user's confirmation, and an empty newer Private Payment List that withdraws the list still go while the recipient is blocked or the deny copy is unknown. This includes continuing or retrying their owed delivery from a kept batch or outbox, including an outbox carried by a restore only when Section 7.2's current evidence establishes the delivery is still owed, subject to the selective replacement of every carried list delivery by the fresh withdrawal required after restore (Section 7.2). An own-app `executor_notice` is not a counterparty send. These exceptions bypass only the counterparty's send hold: the certificate, current identity, restore's first read and other ordinary delivery requirements still apply. They neither release another message to the same recipient nor authorize a new message or a copy reconstructed from shared records after restore where Section 7.2 withholds it; Section 9 still decides responses to re-send requests. The app determines the exception from the signed message and the durable state that establishes its role and effect, not its kind alone: a payer cancellation differs from a payee cancellation, a subscription acceptance from a payment's acceptance, a nonempty list from a withdrawal, and a user's Allowance end from one generated automatically for a crossing withdrawal. The last two ends may have the same message kind and reference form; the exception requires durable evidence that this end was initiated by the user. The record holding an end, its already queued status or its signature alone does not grant that exception. If the app cannot establish the origin, the counterparty message stays held without undoing the end or its own-app notice.
  - A manual subscription acceptance may also finish its original delivery to a blocked payee when the user confirmed that exact blocked subscription under Sections 8.1 and 8.2. This permission requires durable evidence of that confirmation, bound to the exact signed acceptance, payee and request and written with the accepted response and its queued message. It does not follow from the message kind, signature, shared record or queued state alone. Unlike the payment-reporting exceptions above, it requires a fresh known deny decision; a failed or unknown read keeps it held. It permits only that original acceptance and retries of its still-owed original delivery, not a later re-send or a copy reconstructed from a shared record. It never releases an unconfirmed acceptance or another message to that payee.
  - The classification and hold must govern every path that can transmit the message: direct delivery, generic outbox flushing, payment resume, re-send, kept-batch resolution, reload and restore. Before handing any such path work that can send, the app durably retains the required per-message holds or otherwise prevents the held messages from being transmitted. It does this when establishing its local block decision or learning that the deny copy is unknown, before a client call can flush queued work as part of that operation. A batch may still record a stop or another effect that is required while sends are held; its queued message remains held where required. A failed hold update, an unknown batch outcome or a crash between queueing and delivery cannot leave an unrestricted delivery path. Reload and restore apply the same rule before any delivery; a clean restore verdict does not make the backup's deny copy current or release a counterparty hold. Holding or dropping the whole outbox is not a substitute, because the exceptions above remain deliverable. The publishing app's separate duty to remove every carried list delivery, including an empty withdrawal, and retain the obligation for a safely newer withdrawal before returning from restore still applies (Sections 7.2 and 8.4). Suppression and the durable obligation must also survive a crash or a kept batch's later application; an unsuccessful update cannot expose the carried list through another delivery path.
  - Holds survive restart and backup/restore, and a later call decides them again from the current deny state and all other applicable restrictions. Once sends are permitted, the app releases only the messages still eligible to go; an unblock does not override a stop, a restore's list withdrawal, or another hold. Holding never extends the signed message's replay deadline. Past that deadline the message is given up under the Switch's normal rules, never sent or made eligible by a later unblock (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11). The app retains the message's required effect and reports an undelivered message as the applicable rule requires; it never erases a stop or an owed payment outcome merely to suppress a send.
- **How fresh the deny list is:** for a payment the user starts, with a request or without one, the app judges the deny list as it read it at most 10 minutes before, the age Section 7.2 allows its copy of the payee's `identity.json` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3). Only when its copy is older, or it holds none, does it read the lists first. For an automatic payment, a resume's repeat of a dispatched payment, an executor's payment under an Allowance (Section 8.3) or a Billing Period paid without its user's confirmation (Section 8.2), it reads the deny list again before each dispatch, and before an automatic acceptance (Section 8.2). It reads the `block_floor` record again before each such dispatch of a one-time request, with the same rule for a read that fails (Blocked counterparties, above). A block the app wrote itself counts at once, for its payments, its sends and the spam rule. An unblock it wrote counts at once for its payments and sends, while the batch that carries it may still apply, and in the spam rule only once its copy of the lists shows it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1). After a restore from a backup, the backup's copy counts as never read: the app reads the deny list again before any payment decision that needs a fresh copy (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1). A read that fails never counts as one that doesn't hold the counterparty: the app makes no payment to it on that read.
- **Labelled requests:** a hosted app holding `paykit_request:<label>` sends every request under its label and writes its records under that label's policy; the period details, receipts and cancellations of the request come from it as from any payee app. The payer's replies go to it and to the identity's apps holding `paykit_request`, which see the sale too (Section 3.1 and Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4).
- **Messages that cross:** a payee that receives a valid proof for an attempt treats it as payment, even if it cancelled the request meanwhile; it may refund with a new request in the other direction. A payer that has recorded a payee's cancellation makes no new attempt, and one already attempting finishes and sends its proof. A payer app that has verified a payee's `payment_request_cancellation` MUST keep it durably in its own state before it does anything else with it: for a message it receives, in the commit that passes the entry, with no service operation in that step. That kept copy is the stop, not the request record: from then on the app MUST make no new attempt for that request, and a restart, or a record write that failed or is still pending, never lifts it. The app records the cancellation in the request record by a later write of its own as soon as it can; until then the identity's other apps may not know of it, the same exposure as a cancellation not yet delivered. It keeps the copy at once whenever it holds the request record, also one whose value doesn't read or is sealed under a scope key it doesn't hold yet, since keeping it needs only the record's key: the stop never waits for a scope key, and giving up the entry never lifts it (Stored entries, below). It drops the copy once the request record holds a cancellation. While its copy of the payer's records may lack records and holds no record of the request, the entry is held unprocessed (Records not listed yet, below). A cancellation for a request whose record the app doesn't hold, once its copy of the records is listed, a record its copy holds as deleted counting as not held, is gated by the spam rule as a message that creates an activity record (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1): the app keeps such stops for records it doesn't hold under the bounds of a reply before its record (below), counted apart from those replies, at most 64 per sender identity and 128 in all, and may drop one 30 days after the cancellation's acceptance, judged on the service time it verified, but never while its copy may lack records or it keeps a stored message of that request to write (Stored entries, below); a stop it keeps for such a message counts outside these bounds and is never evicted. By then its sender re-sends nothing, and any own app that held the request got the cancellation too. A stop dropped earlier, for the bounds of 64 and 128, is dropped as one never processed: the client marked the cancellation processed when the app kept it, so the app forgets its pair in the commit that drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), and a later re-send of the same cancellation is taken and kept again; the stop counts in its sender's mark of dropped replies, which asks that sender at once (Replies before their record, below; Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound). If the client cannot yet forget the pair of the ordinary stop this order selects, the app retains that stop and defers admission of the incoming ordinary stop as Waiting for admission, below, specifies; no counted stop is admitted above either bound. This does not defer a verified cancellation that this paragraph requires to be kept immediately, or change the stops it explicitly protects outside those bounds.
- **Replies before their record:** a payer's acceptance, rejection, cancellation or proof can reach a payee app before it holds the record of the request the reply names, for example a request another own app sent whose record this app hasn't read yet. The app keeps such a reply in its own state, under the spam rule, as a message that creates an activity record (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1), and places it in the record by a later write of its own once it holds the record. The same holds for a payee's period details at a payer app before it holds the record of their request, and for an Allowance acceptance or rejection before the app holds the Allowance record of its proposal, for example one answering a proposal another own app sent: each is kept, counted with these replies, dropped and marked as a reply is below, and placed in its record by a later write of its own, except that in this revision an acceptance that would newly fill the record's response is kept and not placed (Section 8.3.1). A reply from the counterparty of a request whose record the app holds, or period details from the payee of a request whose record it holds, that it can't place yet because the period record, or, at a payer, the request record, doesn't open for good, read or bind, or because a proof names period details the period record doesn't hold, is no new state: the app keeps it outside the spam rule, within the same bounds, places it by a later write of its own once the record can take it, and after 30 days gives it up, telling the host (`MessageGivenUp`). A value waiting for its scope key holds the entry instead (Stored entries). A payee app that holds a request record whose value it can't open, read or bind, other than for want of a scope key, can't tell whether a reply naming it comes from its counterparty, since the record's key names only the request: it keeps the reply as a reply before its record, under the spam rule, places it once the record reads naming the reply's sender, and gives it up at its 30 days otherwise. No received message is written over a held record's value that the app can't read. A request reaching a payer app that holds the payer's record of it but can't open, read or bind it, other than for want of a scope key, is held, its entry unprocessed, until the record reads; once 30 days have passed since its acceptance the app takes it with no effect, telling the host (`MessageGivenUp`), and it stays processed. A proof of a payment without a request whose payee record, keyed by the endpoint and the settlement, the app holds but can't open, read or bind is new state, since that key doesn't bind its sender: it takes the spam rule, is kept as a reply before its record within those bounds, is written once the record reads naming its sender, and goes, with no notice, once it reads naming another; dropped for those bounds, it counts in its sender's mark and is asked for again as any reply (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound). A message naming a request from anyone but its counterparty is never placed: a proof naming a Billing Period is new state, under the spam rule, and a response or cancellation is invalid and dropped. A payee's cancellation or a counterparty's `allowance_end` naming a record the app doesn't hold is kept as a stop instead (Messages that cross, above; Section 8.3). A receipt reaching a payer app that holds the payer's record it names but can't open or read it, other than for want of a scope key, is kept as a reply before its record is, counted, dropped and marked with those replies, under the spam rule for a payment without a request, whose record alone names its counterparty, and once the record reads it is written there with the effects its first read would have had on the attempt it names (Stored entries, below). It keeps a reply at most 30 days from its acceptance, judged on the service time it verified, the time its sender keeps it (Section 9), and at most 64 such replies per sender identity, the identity its certificate names, and 128 in all, the counts by which Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2 bounds the entries of a sender whose standing couldn't be checked. Past a sender's 64, the app drops that sender's reply accepted earliest; past 128 in all, the reply accepted earliest of the sender identity that holds the most such replies, and among sender identities holding the same most, the lower sender identity. Earliest means by acceptance time and, within one millisecond, in the order the inbox accepted them, the order the app's feed gives its entries in, never by `msg_id`, which a sender chooses. The selection includes the incoming reply. Admission and any required eviction commit atomically, with no committed ordinary reply pool above either bound. If the selected earlier reply is protected, admission waits as Waiting for admission, below, specifies; the selected earlier reply stays where it is, with its pair and effect unchanged. The app places the kept replies of one record in that order, so of two different Allowance answers kept before their record the earlier is the record's response and the later conflicts with it (Section 8.3, Conflicted); where the earlier is an acceptance this revision doesn't place (Section 8.3.1), the record's response stays empty and the later still conflicts with it. A reply dropped for these bounds is dropped as one never processed: the client marked it processed when the app kept it, so the app forgets its pair in the commit that drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), and a later re-send of the same message is taken. For each sender identity whose replies it dropped for these bounds, the app keeps one mark holding two times: the earliest acceptance time among those replies, which a re-send asks from, and the latest, which the mark expires by. A re-sent copy dropped again counts with its new acceptance time; a sender re-sends only messages it first accepted in the last 30 days (Section 9), so such copies move the latest time to at most 30 days after their first acceptance, and a mark that only re-sent copies feed ends at most 60 days after the replies it covers were first accepted. The mark asks that sender to re-send what its inbox accepted from the mark's earliest time (`resend_request` with `accepted_from`, `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.12) as Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound, says: at once, whatever records the app holds, since the record a dropped message names may come at any time, from another own app or a re-send, and again at most once in any 24 hours by its own clock (the Paykit SDK's interval) while the mark lives: a sender may leave selected messages out for its own limits, and its requester may ask again later (Section 9). A payee's cancellation, or a counterparty's `allowance_end`, kept as a stop and dropped for the bounds on those stops (Messages that cross, above) counts in its sender's mark the same way; a sender whose sends are held is asked nothing until they aren't (Blocked counterparties, above). The retry and unknown-delivery rules of Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound, apply to the ask. Asking doesn't change the mark, and the re-send may leave out replies the sender no longer keeps, for which nothing is owed. A re-sent reply is taken as any reply: placed if the app now holds its record, kept or dropped again by these bounds if it doesn't, and not written again if its record already holds it (Stored entries, below), so a reply is never applied twice. A mark goes 30 days after its latest time (Section 9), or, past 128 marks, when it is, among the marks an ask covering whose latest time was delivered, the one whose latest time is earliest (on a tie, the one of the lower sender identity); with none such, the one whose latest time is earliest among those whose ask was refused or can't be sent yet, the host told (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound). A backup carries the marks with their times, so a restore neither loses nor resets the marks its backup held; a mark made after the backup goes with what the restore loses (Section 7.2), whose own requests for re-sends ask for those replies again. So a sender sees only ordinary `resend_request`s from each payee app, at most one in any 24 hours, while the app keeps dropping its replies and for 30 days after: an admitted sender can make a payee app send it no more than that.
- **Waiting for admission:** when the ordinary family and its required eviction order select an earlier message whose payload or processed pair the client still protects, the app does not admit the incoming message to that family. It requests the protocol-admission wait of Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 11, before the receive callback's effects commit. All staged remote operations, local writes and pair forgets are discarded; the incoming message gains no processed pair. Only that unadmitted authenticated input enters the client's existing waiting pool, with its original identity, acceptance evidence, sequence and generation, before its cursor passes. No Paykit payload queue is added, no already retained ordinary payload moves there, and no ordinary bound or required victim changes. If the ordinary rule instead selects the incoming message itself, its drop and recovery mark use the existing atomic unprocessed-consumption rule; it is not deferred merely to avoid that rule. A real inability to commit custody or the required drop notice is a real storage failure, not successful intake or a capacity wait with no durable obligation.
  - This decision comes after the rules that require an immediate durable stop or protect an attempt's first receipt. A known mandatory stop is kept immediately, including an end or conflict of an Allowance, as Messages that cross, above, and Section 8.3 require. A protected first receipt is kept under Stored entries, below. Neither may be postponed, discarded or relabeled as an ordinary admission wait; their existing retention and counting rules are unchanged. Waiting does not settle, release or forget the prior client's pending or uncertain effect, even if Paykit's monetary state is already settled.
  - Admission waits share the client's 21-day, 64-per-sender and 128-total waiting pool with sender-verification and key waits, on equal terms under the client's custody order, not Paykit's ordinary eviction order. The client applies those bounds atomically on custody admission, so the incoming copy may itself be selected for a custody drop. A retry keeps the same stored-input identity, original acceptance and age. It rechecks the current records, authorization, deny state and required ordinary victim through the client's bounded fair retry schedule, without same-read spinning or a global intake hold. Admission does not restart the ordinary family's retention period. Reload and backup preserve custody or an owed drop notice and the original evidence. Successful processing retires eligible stored duplicates under the generic completion rule; independent uncertainty remains protected. Admission is not guaranteed while the prior victim remains protected.
  - If the client expires or selects an admission-waiting copy for its custody bound, its durable notice distinguishes that reason and carries the established sender and message identity, original acceptance evidence, sequence and generation, without a payment body. The app counts this loss in the existing dropped-reply recovery mark of that sender (Replies before their record; Section 9), never the unverified or key-wait mark and never a new family. It acknowledges the client notice only in the local transaction that durably records the mark changes and any host notice those changes require; a repeated notice does not count a second loss or reset its times. It creates no incoming processed pair and forgets no independently protected pair, copy or effect merely because custody of this copy was lost. The notice is not a resolution or release of an uncertain effect. The mark's ordinary authorization, deny checks, bounds, ask cadence, retention and host-notice rules apply; no extra request, payment detail or contact information is sent to the sender. An ask and a notice guarantee neither delivery nor recovery after custody expiry or overflow.
- **Stored entries:** an app stores an entry locally, moves its cursor past it and tells Paykit when the service refuses its effect for good, or the effect doesn't fit one batch or the app's store, or the effect's fate couldn't be known, and when it drops an entry whose sender's standing couldn't be checked (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). An entry whose record write hits a per-kind limit or the storage quota (`429 quota_exceeded`), or would take the record over the 256 KiB limit for a shared record value (`413 too_large`), is stored too, and its record is written once room frees (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), or its message is given up as below. Paykit keeps the message of an entry whose effect was refused, whose record write waits for room, or whose fate couldn't be known, and of one whose record's view shows it divergent (Section 6, Divergent records), whatever its kind, and writes it by a later write of its own, as soon as the record can take it, into the record its kind is recorded in (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.3, Recorded): a request, period details and a receipt into the payer's record of the request, the Billing Period or the payment; a payer's acceptance, rejection, cancellation or proof into the payee's record of the request or the Billing Period; a proof of a payment without a request into the payee's record of its settlement, which the write may open (Section 8.4); an Allowance proposal, acceptance or rejection into its `allowances` record, but in this revision not an acceptance that would newly fill the record's response (Section 8.3.1); and a Private Payment List into the `payment_lists` record, held until it is written, a newer list from its publishing app replacing an older one. Over a record whose view shows it divergent, it keeps, of each role of the record, the earliest message its inbox accepted and, of each sender, the 16 latest after it, at most 34 in all, and for a `payment_lists` record the list of the highest version; past 34, the role's earliest aside, over a record that reads the earliest message of a sender other than its counterparty goes, and over one that doesn't, the earliest of the sender keeping the most, the lower sender identity among those keeping as many, receipts aside, as below; once its user confirms, it writes them in the order its inbox accepted them, each placed as the receive step places it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3): into its role when the record holds none for that role, period details among the period's details (Section 8.2), and every other one reflected by its `msg_id`. Receipts over a payer record are bounded by attempt, not by sender or role: the first receipt from the record's counterparty its inbox accepted for each attempt ID that the app's own journal holds, or that the claim of the obligation the receipt names shows, is kept whatever the bound, never dropped for it and counted outside the 34; only a further receipt for an attempt, or one naming an attempt neither shows, counts under the bound, and one it drops is asked for again (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound). A receipt is the payee's evidence that settles an attempt whose fate the payer doesn't know (Section 7.3): dropped, the first receipt of an attempt would leave it failed or unknown, and a payment without a request, which nothing but its record and its receipt shows paid, could then be paid again by hand. Every other write over that record that adds no stop, a new message the receive step takes, a stored entry, a kept reply or a held list, waits behind the messages kept over it that its inbox accepted before it, also after the confirmation and once the view has cleared, so none takes the role of one accepted earlier, and a new message the receive step takes meanwhile is kept beside them. A kept message that doesn't point to its record any more, one kept over a later write than the one the app holds at that key, or a proof of a payment without a request whose settlement, as its payment method reads it now, isn't the one the record is keyed by, isn't written, and is given up at its 30 days as any kept message; what waits for the kept messages waits only for those that still point to the record. It writes them before anything is paid, accepted, credited or marked done on that record. The bound drops the messages of a role between the earliest and the 16 latest, proofs among them, each as one never processed: the app forgets its pair in the commit that drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), so a later re-send of it is taken, and counts it in its sender's mark of dropped replies (Replies before their record), which asks for it again (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound), so a proof of a payment made twice while the record was divergent comes back while its sender keeps it. The selection includes the message the receive step is taking. If it selects that incoming message, the step commits its drop and recovery mark without keeping its payload or marking it processed. If it selects an earlier ordinary message whose pair or effect the client still protects, the app keeps that earlier message and defers the incoming admission as Waiting for admission, above, specifies; it neither chooses another victim nor commits an ordinary counted pool above its bound. The role's earliest message, protected first receipts and immediate stops retain their stated protection and counting rules. The rule holds as well for any kind recorded later. A payee's cancellation and a counterparty's `allowance_end` are kept as stops at once instead, and placed as Messages that cross, above, and Section 8.3 say. The app never writes over a record value it can't read or open, which may hold what another own app stored. It tries for 30 days from the message's acceptance, judged on the service time it verified, the time its sender keeps it (Section 9), and may then give the message up, telling the host (`MessageGivenUp`, the notice it also gives for a message the client drops while it waits for a scope key, below); either way the message stays processed. The 30 days run also for a message kept over a divergent record whose user hasn't confirmed it, since its sender keeps it no longer. A message waiting behind messages kept over its record keeps its own 30 days, and may be given up at them, never while the app's copy may lack its record; a held list stays held. What a given-up message loses depends on its kind: a request is never shown; period details are never paid by; a payer's acceptance, rejection, cancellation or proof never shows in the payee's record; a proof of a payment without a request opens no record of its settlement; a receipt never shows in the payer's record and settles no attempt; an Allowance proposal or answer never reaches its `allowances` record; and a Private Payment List is never paid to. No stop is lost, since a payee's cancellation and an Allowance end are kept as stops, not as messages to write. A message whose record the app's copy may lack (Records not listed yet, below) is neither written nor given up by the app meanwhile; only the client's drop of one waiting for a scope key, below, gives it up then. Giving a message up ends only its record write, and never lifts a stop. The same later write compensates for an effect whose fate couldn't be known, since a record that already holds the message isn't written again, and once the app has written it, it declines a released copy of the entry (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), so the effect isn't applied twice. For entries dropped unverified, it keeps a mark of their own per sender identity, with the same two times, bounds, expiry, backup and cadence as a dropped reply's (Replies before their record): it asks that sender at once to re-send what its inbox accepted from the mark's earliest time to its latest (`accepted_from` and `accepted_until`, `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.12), and again at most once in any 24 hours by its own clock while the mark lives (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound), and a message it already holds isn't applied twice. A message whose effect needs a scope key the app doesn't hold yet, a payee's cancellation and a counterparty's `allowance_end` aside, which never wait for one (Messages that cross, above, and Section 8.3), waits for that key in the client's local store, past the cursor, under the bounds that Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2 gives for stored entries: 21 days from its acceptance, judged on the service time the app verified, 64 per sender identity and 128 in all, counted together with entries whose sender's standing couldn't be checked and entries waiting for admission, above. Past them the client drops first every such entry kept past the 21 days, then, past a sender's 64, that sender's entries accepted earliest, then, past 128 in all, one at a time, the entry accepted earliest of the sender identity that holds the most such entries, the three waiting reasons counted and ordered alike (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.2 and 19, question 32). The client asks for the key by one full-bundle request for all such entries, again at most hourly while a key is still missing, and tries the message again once a key bundle brings that key (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.5 and 8.2), and only after it has opened the records the key opens, so the message never runs on a record still held unopened. Once the key comes, the message is processed, or held as Records not listed yet, below, says while the copy may lack its record. If the client drops it first, the message was never processed: the app tells the host once that it is given up (`MessageGivenUp`), even while its copy may lack the message's record, and keeps a mark of its own for such messages per sender identity, with the same two times, bounds, expiry and backup as a dropped reply's (Replies before their record), from which it asks that sender to re-send, at the same cadence, only once it holds every key those messages waited for (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound). A copy sent again is processed as a new message.
- **Undelivered messages:** when the client gives up a message this app sent that its store never saw the counterparty's inbox accept (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), and an activity record of this app holds it in one of its roles, the app tells its host once, naming the record's counterparty, the message and the record's request, and keeps the record as it is. The host may conclude only that this store never saw it accepted, not that the counterparty never got it: after a restore, the device the backup came from may have delivered it, so a new request in its place may be paid as well as the first. This host notice requires an activity record. The unknown-delivery duty above applies even without one, including to a `resend_request`, a list request or a list.
- **Records not listed yet:** an app's copy of the shared records lacks records until it has listed them: from the moment it joins, after a restore, after a `missed` item until the app has listed them again (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), and after it takes a certificate that reads collections, or members of one, that the certificate it last listed under, or any certificate it held since, didn't, until it holds that certificate's scope keys and has listed them (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.5 and 8.3). After a `missed` item, the client itself lists again, in the read that passes the item and, past its page budget, in the reads after it, before it hands the protocol any entry, a stored one included, and a read whose listing fails ends there, failed, the next read listing first (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2; Switch wire 7.4.4), so this rule covers what the app decides between the item and that listing. Until then a record's absence from the copy decides nothing about a collection not yet listed. An authenticated visible delete is not absence, also after listing and at an app with no seen state, such as a new app or one restored from an old backup. For any potentially protected key, it imposes Section 6's Visible deletion without a copy rule: an incoming request, period message, proof or list cannot open an unmarked replacement, and no claim attempt, payment or credit is authorized by replaying that message. An app with seen state writes nothing over the delete but the protective write-back Section 6 requires, and a replay supplies no new authority. A record is treated as genuinely absent only when the app has no retained evidence of it and the service shows neither a live value nor a delete; a key legitimately forgotten after all honest witnesses were removed remains the explicit loss limit (Section 6, When a record is lost). A settlement mark still forbids credit independently (Section 8.1 step 4). A reply is kept before its record (Replies before their record), and a payee's cancellation is kept as a stop for a request not held. An Allowance proposal over a deleted `allowances` record is not one of these: it is conflicted (Section 8.3, Ending). A received message of a kind that is recorded (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.3), whose effect is decided from whether the app holds a record of such a collection, is held unprocessed, as the store-retry hold holds an entry (Section 17, question 27), and is read again once the records are listed, so it is neither lost nor taken as new state; so are period details the app would take with no effect from what such a record holds. A message about a record the copy holds takes its effect from that record: its write builds on the version the app holds, or, where the switch's current write is one the app refused, on that write's version, as Section 17, question 36 says; and a newer write at the switch fails it, so the effect is built again on that write, at the latest at the next read. One whose effect needs a scope key of a certificate the app took and doesn't hold yet waits for that key as Stored entries, above, says, and once the key comes is held this way until the records are listed. Today these are a payment request, period details, a payer's acceptance, rejection or cancellation, a payee's cancellation, a proof, a receipt, a Private Payment List, and an Allowance proposal, acceptance, rejection or end, whose records are in `allowances`; the rule holds as well for any kind recorded later.

### 8.2 Recurring request

- **Billing Periods** follow one rule, the anchored period rule of `specs/allowances.md` applied to the request's `recurrence`. Boundary `k` is `anchor` plus `k * every` units: for minutes, hours, days and weeks a fixed number of seconds; for months and years a calendar step computed from the original anchor, clamped to the target month's last day when the anchor's day doesn't exist. Period `k` is `[boundary(k), boundary(k+1))`.
  - The first Billing Period is the one containing `starts_at`; the last is the one containing the instant just before `ends_at`, when set.
  - Obligation IDs name a period by its start boundary in Unix seconds (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3). Messages name it by `{starts_at, ends_at}`, which must equal a computed period; anything else is rejected.
- **Accepting:** when the user accepts the subscription, or an executor accepts it under an Allowance, the payer records the acceptance in the request record, in a batch conditioned on the version of the request record it read and, when the request has a proposal expiry, on `server_time_in(null, proposal_expires_at + 1 ms)`, and sends it after that batch succeeds. The expiry is inclusive: its own millisecond is the last at which the acceptance is recorded. A rejection is recorded the same way, so an acceptance and a rejection can't both go out. An executor accepts a subscription under an Allowance only while the conditions of Section 8.3, Executor, hold and the Allowance's terms are valid and in force at the current server time, capacity being checked only when each period is paid, as in v0.1, and the request matches those terms: the request's asset is the Allowance's, its amount is within `per_payment_amount` where set, and, when the terms list allowed identifiers, the request can be paid to at least one of them, as in v0.1 (`specs/allowances.md`, "Payment Request integration"). Its acceptance's batch also carries `record_version(Allowance record) == r`, as an executor batch does (Section 8.3), so it can't pin an Allowance another own app has just ended, and writes `allowance_id`, naming that Allowance, into the request record, so the Allowance is pinned at acceptance, as v0.1 pins it (`specs/allowances.md`, "One-time and recurring requests"). Accepting takes the guards of paying a request: while the request is held after a restore or a `missed` item (Section 7.2), no app accepts it automatically, and one accepts it by hand only after its user has seen that the payee may have cancelled it; and while the deny list holds the payee, an app accepts it only after its user confirms, having been told that the payee is blocked (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties). Each period then has its own obligation, attempts, proof, optional period details, optional receipt and period record. A period record of a request the app holds and the payer has accepted is an update of that request's activity, not a new activity record under the spam rule (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1).
- **Paying periods:** accepting a subscription pays nothing by itself, as in v0.1 (`specs/payment-requests.md`, "Recurring Payment Requests And Subscriptions"). A payer pays a Billing Period, by hand or automatically, only of a subscription its request record shows accepted, as in v0.1, where acceptance "makes the request active" and only an active one is paid (`specs/payment-requests.md`, "Recurring Payment Requests And Subscriptions"). Under the Allowance its request record names, which for a recurring request only an executor's acceptance under that Allowance writes (Accepting, above), or under the one the user moved the period to (Section 8.3, One Allowance per request), its executor pays a Billing Period automatically, and that payment's proof carries its `allowance_id` (Section 8.3). An executor, or an app paying a period by its own policy without its user's confirmation (below), pays automatically only from one Billing Period on: the request's first Billing Period when the payer's acceptance of the subscription was recorded before that period starts, else the period that contains the time that acceptance was recorded, the service time of that write (Accepting, above), and no period when that time is after the request's last Billing Period has ended; the periods keep the boundaries the request's recurrence gives them (Billing Periods, above). An app reads that recorded time as the service time of the request record's write it holds, which is the acceptance's or a later write's; both give the same periods paid automatically, since every period that ended before a later write's time has an automatic window that ended too (below); while the write it holds has no service time that a proof or a verified stub dates (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3), it pays no period automatically. It pays each of these periods automatically only within the period's automatic window, which ends at the earlier of its attempt deadline, when the request has a payment deadline (Section 7.1: the period's start plus the deadline's `seconds`), and the start of the next Billing Period, for every request, with a payment deadline or without one, judged on the highest service time it has verified. The window is judged again immediately before the attempt's first dispatch (Section 7.2 step 2): an attempt whose period's window has ended by then is written `failed`, never dispatched, and its period is left to payment by hand; a repeat that completes a payment already dispatched isn't judged, since money may already have moved. A period that ended before that acceptance, as for a subscription whose `starts_at` lies in the past, is paid only by hand, after its user's confirmation for that period, and so is any period whose automatic window ended before it was paid; paid by hand, a period still has its own payment deadline (Section 7.1). So an executor, or an app paying by its own policy, that accepts late or comes back after being offline never pays a run of earlier periods at once. For example, with monthly periods anchored at 2027-01-01, a `starts_at` of 2027-01-15 and an acceptance recorded at 2026-12-15, no period contains the acceptance, and the first period paid automatically is the request's first, `[2027-01-01, 2027-02-01)`, the one containing `starts_at`; and with daily periods and a 7-day payment deadline, each period's automatic window ends when the next period starts, so an executor offline for three days pays none of the three periods that ended meanwhile automatically, and each of them is paid by hand, within its own 7-day deadline. A period moved to another Allowance later (One Allowance per request, Section 8.3) is paid under it on the same terms. A subscription whose acceptance named no Allowance, one its user accepted by hand among them, has no Billing Period paid under an Allowance, and no period's payment names one, as in v0.1, where an Allowance is a candidate only for a request that remains proposed and a manual response excludes automatic handling (`specs/allowances.md`, "Payment Request integration"; `specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Durable Allowance payment accounting"). Without an Allowance, for a request whose record names none (Section 8.3, One Allowance per request), whether the paying app asks its user before each period is its own policy, as in v0.1. A period it pays without its user's confirmation for that period is an automatic payment for every rule of this spec that names one (Sections 6, 7.1, 7.2, 8.1 and 8.2, and Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6 step 5), though made under no Allowance. That includes the `manual_only` mark: no such payment is made of a Billing Period whose period record holds it, or whose request's record does (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3), with or without an Allowance, and its attempt's batch writes that period record at the version it read, as an executor's does (Section 8.3), so a mark written since makes the batch fail. Its proof, like that of a payment the user confirmed, carries no `allowance_id` (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.10).
- **Period details:** once the payer has accepted the subscription, as v0.1 issues quotes only then (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"), the payee may send `payment_period_details` for a Billing Period the payer stores them for (below), with that period's conversion rates, its destination, or both. Rates come only for a request whose conversion terms are `per_period`, as v0.1 takes a quote only on such a request (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"): a request's fixed rates price every period, and one without conversion terms promises none, so period details with rates are invalid against either, and the payer takes them with no effect (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.9). The payee app that sends them holds the request's record and writes them into its `payee_activity` period record first, conditioned on the version it read, and sends them only after that write succeeds, so the identity's other payee apps can check a proof that names them; it sends none once that record holds 8 for the period. Each carries `expires_at` and stays valid until then: a later one replaces none, as in v0.1. The payer may pay by any valid one its period record holds, by default the latest that carries what the payment needs, a destination when the request has `per_period_endpoints` and the period isn't the first, and rates when the payment is priced by them, ordered by `created_at` and then `msg_id`; it pays by one, never a destination from one and rates from another, and waits while none carries both when it needs both; its proof references the one it used. A period has at most 8 period details: the payee sends no more, the payer's period record holds them all (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3), and the payer pays by none its period record doesn't hold. A payer stores period details only for a request it has accepted, as v0.1 issues quotes only after acceptance, and only for a Billing Period whose record doesn't show it paid and that is, on the highest service time it has verified, the next to start, or one that has started and, with a payment deadline, whose deadline hasn't passed, or, without one, is one of the 16 latest to have started; it takes any other details, and those of a request it has rejected or that is cancelled, with no effect. Each app judges this on the service time it has itself verified, never on the other's: a payee that has verified none yet can't tell which periods have started and sends no period details, whatever the payer has verified, and a payer that has verified none yet holds the details it would store until it has verified one, whatever the payee has verified; details of a request it hasn't accepted, has rejected, that is cancelled or whose payee's cancellation it keeps, and for a period whose record shows it paid, the payer takes with no effect all the same. So a payee can make a payer hold period records only for periods it may still pay.
- **Rates and payment time:** as with v0.1's conversion quotes, the rates of period details apply to a payment whose independently verified payment time is from their start through their `expires_at`, compared at millisecond precision. Their start is their signed `created_at`, cut to whole seconds, which is v0.1's `valid_from`; the payee sets `created_at` to the time it signs them and never backdates them, as in v0.1. That time comes from the payment method's evidence (`specs/payment-conversion.md`), never from when the proof arrived. Both sides apply it. The payer pays by the rates only while its device clock and the latest service time it has verified are both at or after their start and before `expires_at`, checked again just before money moves (Section 7.2); otherwise it waits for valid period details, or pays to an endpoint of the requested asset; once its user has confirmed an amount by rates, either needs that user's confirmation again (Section 7.2). The payee credits a payment at those rates only if its verified payment time is from their start through `expires_at`, and a payee app does so only once its period record holds the details the proof names: it never credits at rates it can't read. A payment verified outside that interval doesn't pay the period at those rates. As in v0.1, late evidence isn't malformed: the payee still credits the funds received and may label them underpaid, late, or both, but they pay no occurrence and the proof isn't credited: it stores and sends no receipt for the period, whose record keeps the proof and stays unpaid, so the rule of Section 8.1 doesn't count them as a credit; Paykit adds no refunds, disputes or resolution. A payment to an endpoint of the requested asset is paid 1:1 without rates, so this bound doesn't apply to it, and the destination of period details is used while they haven't expired, as above. An Allowance counts the amount the payment transfers in the requested asset, never less than the requested amount, for its reservation, its spending and every limit, so a transfer above that amount counts whole and doesn't fit at a limit's edge where the requested amount would; it can't authorize repricing, so a payment priced by rates, the request's fixed rates or period details', is made only by hand (Section 8.3), as in v0.1 (`specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Conversion terms").
- **Destinations per period:** without `per_period_endpoints`, the request's destination serves every period. With it, the request's `payment_endpoints` serve only the first period: the payer never attempts a later period before receiving period details with its destination, and a payee credits a later period only for a payment to the destination of period details its proof names. A payee app credits a Billing Period's proof only once it can read the period's request record, against whose amount and destination it checks the proof: while it holds that record but can't open it, it credits nothing until it can, and no confirmation of its user stands in for it.
- **One period per payment:** a Billing Period's credit writes its settlement's mark as a credit does (Section 8.1 step 4), naming the request and the period, so one payment pays one Billing Period of one subscription at most, as in v0.1 ("A single payment must not satisfy multiple requests or periods", `specs/payment-conversion.md`, "Payment Conversion Quote"). Late evidence, which pays no occurrence (Rates and payment time, above), stores no receipt and writes no mark.
- **Not before its start:** no app pays a Billing Period automatically before it starts, and the service enforces it: every attempt on a period carries `server_time_in(s, null)`, so the service applies it only from `s` on its own clock, whatever the payer's clock says. `s` is the period's start. A payer pays a period before its start only by hand, after its user confirms paying that period early; that attempt carries as `s` the latest service time the app has verified when it builds the attempt, after that confirmation, so the service still never applies it before the user agreed.
- **Tombstones:** each paid period leaves a small tombstone, kept 400 days after its last entry when the request has no payment deadline (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3) and counted against the paying app's share of the claim limit. Without a payment deadline the payer also keeps the period's record for the identity's life (Section 6). So payees of frequent subscriptions should set one.
- **Attempted periods:** a payer's request record lists, in `attempted_periods`, the start of every Billing Period that may have an open or `unknown` attempt: at most 16, in ascending order, each once. An attempt on a period not listed writes the request record in the attempt's batch, adding the period, at the record's next version; the attempt is already conditioned on the version it read (Section 7.2 step 1). An attempt on a listed period doesn't write it. A write of the request record may drop a listed period whose claim has no open or `unknown` attempt, conditioned on that claim's step as the writer read it (`claim_step`), so a new attempt on that period meanwhile makes the write fail. Apps drop finished periods whenever they write the record anyway, for example when adding one. With 16 listed and none to drop, an app makes no attempt on a period not listed until one of them finishes. So every period with an open attempt is listed in the version of the request record its attempt's batch left, the one it was conditioned on or the one it wrote, and stays listed until its claim has no open attempt. A period whose claim holds an `unknown` attempt (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2) stays listed as one with an open attempt does, and refuses the cancellation the same way, until its owner reports it, a receipt settles it, or, once its owner isn't current, another own app fails it after its user confirms (Section 7.3; Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2 rows 2, 3 and 5). This covers every period an attempt may pay: the current one, one paid early by hand (Not before its start, above), and a past one whose payment deadline, which can be longer than the period, hasn't passed.
- **Ending:** either side's cancellation ends the subscription. A payer records its cancellation in the request record, in a batch that writes the record at its next version, so conditioned on the version it read, and conditioned on the claim of every listed period being at the step read with no open or `unknown` attempt (`claim_step`), as in Section 8.1, and sends it after that batch succeeds. An attempt on a period not listed writes the request record, and one on a listed period moves its claim's step, so an attempt and a cancellation can't both succeed. A listed period with an open or `unknown` attempt refuses the cancellation: the app shows that the payment is in progress or, for an `unknown` attempt, that it may already be paid, as for a one-time request. A payee's cancellation of a recurring request stops the payer app as Section 8.1 says (Messages that cross): the app MUST keep a verified `payment_request_cancellation` durably in its own state before it does anything else with it, and from then on MUST make no new attempt on any of the request's Billing Periods, whatever its request record shows; a restart, or a record write that failed or is still pending, never lifts the stop. It records the cancellation in the request record by a later write of its own as soon as it can.
- **Ended:** a recurring request whose recurrence sets `ends_at` has ended once its last Billing Period has ended, at that period's end, which may lie after `ends_at`, since the last period is whole (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.4), judged on the highest service time the app has verified, where v0.1 used the wallet's trusted local time. As in v0.1 this is a view, not a message: nobody sends a cancellation for it, and a proof of an earlier period may still arrive (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Recurring Scheduling"). v0.1 counted it from `ends_at` itself, which in v0.2 can fall inside a period still to be paid. Reaching its end closes nothing: a request that has ended may still be accepted, and then shows as ended, as in v0.1, where reaching `ends_at` "is not a lifecycle transition" (`specs/payment-requests.md`, "Recurring Payment Requests And Subscriptions").

### 8.3 Allowances

Allowances are built entirely in apps on claims and shared records. The switch has no Allowance endpoints, objects or rules.

| Allowance concept | Primitive |
|---|---|
| Proposal, terms, the executor's grant, and the response and end that give its status | Shared record in `allowances` |
| Ledger | One shared record per Allowance in `allowance_ledgers`: its initial ledger (Section 8.3.1), then an entry per attempt and running totals |
| Payment | The request's claim (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2) |

Record keys are derived from the counterparty and the `allowance_id` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3).

**Setting up.** How acceptance establishes the Allower's initial ledger is specified with its implementation (Section 8.3.1); acceptance grants no app authority to execute. The app that will pay automatically asks its user, checks the accepted terms against the signed proposal and acceptance stored in the Allowance record, and writes its **executor grant** there: its app ID and the terms hash (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3), signed with its own signing key over the identity, the counterparty, the `allowance_id` and its `confirmed_generation`, the current generation (Pubky Switch spec, Terms and Section 4.3). It must hold `paykit_allowance_execute`. The grant authorizes payment only once its executor has established a complete current anchor under it (A grant implies a ledger, below).

**Executor.** An app pays automatically under an Allowance only while all of these hold, besides the checks of Paying under an Allowance (below): the Allowance record holds its own grant for the current terms and counterparty, no end or rejection, and isn't conflicted (Conflicted, below), and its current write isn't one the executor ignores, unless its user has confirmed the Allowance since (Ending, below); its **consent list**, kept durably in its own state, holds the Allowance; the request comes from that counterparty and is priced in the Allowance's own asset, as in v0.1 (Section 16); the request record names this Allowance for the occurrence, or, for a one-time request, names none yet (One Allowance per request, below); its copy of the deny list, read again before the dispatch (Section 8.1, How fresh the deny list is), is complete and doesn't hold that counterparty (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1); the Allowance record, read again from the service immediately before the first dispatch, once the attempt is journaled and its batch has succeeded (Section 7.2 step 1, where an executor checks its Allowance again), as the deny list and the `block_floor` record are read again before it, still holds all of the above at the version the attempt's batch was conditioned on: another own app may have ended, rejected or handed off the Allowance after that batch, and the executor's copy doesn't show it yet; a read that fails, or a record that doesn't read, pays nothing, and a resume's repeat is held as Attempts already open (below) says; neither the occurrence's record nor, for a Billing Period, its request's record is marked manual-only (below), and its request, if a one-time one, isn't dated before the block floor as a read of the `block_floor` record made immediately before the dispatch shows it, nor does that read fail or show a floor the executor can't read (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties): the executor's batch doesn't condition on the floor's version, so that read, not the batch, is what a raise meets; it keeps no stop for the request (Sections 8.1 and 8.2) or for the Allowance (Ends reach the executor, below); the Allowance record's response is settled: the executor keeps no answer to the Allowance's proposal that it hasn't written into the record yet, kept before its record or stored (Section 8.1, Replies before their record; Stored entries), the reference client stores no entry from that counterparty that may be one (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), and that counterparty owes it no re-send of replies dropped for the bounds on kept replies (Section 8.1), since an earlier different answer among them takes the response, or makes the Allowance conflicted (Conflicted, below), once it is written; a kept answer or a mark it can't read pays nothing either; neither is held after a restore or a `missed` item (Section 7.2); the Allowance isn't paused after a provider loss (After provider loss, below), and none of its records, nor the occurrence's, a Billing Period's request record among them, is divergent (Section 6); its user has confirmed the Allowance again since any restore that isn't clean (Ends reach the executor, below); its ledger is one it may build on (Building on a version accepts it; A grant implies a ledger) and can take the new entry (Compaction, below); no rollback or misbehaviour of the service holds its attempts (Sections 7.1 and 7.2); and the obligation isn't doubtful (Doubtful obligations, below). It adds an Allowance to the list when it writes the grant, and removes it for good when it sees the Allowance ended, rejected, handed off or conflicted. No other app can make it pay: a grant can't be forged or moved to another counterparty, changing the terms voids it, and an end it has seen is final. **Manual only:** a payer app may mark an occurrence manual-only on its record, the request record of a one-time request or the period record of a Billing Period, and a recurring request as a whole on its request record (`manual_only`, `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3), as in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Durable Allowance payment accounting"). The mark is never lifted, and no executor pays the occurrence automatically while its record holds it: the executor's batch writes that record at the version it read (step 3, below), so a mark written since makes the batch fail. A Billing Period that has no period record yet, one not started among them, is marked by the write that creates its period record, holding the mark and no message, built on no version, so an executor's batch that creates that record meanwhile makes one of the two fail, and an app whose mark failed reads the record and marks it again. An attempt its user started by hand writes the mark too, as in v0.1, where a manual payment excludes automatic payment of the same occurrence: its batch (Section 7.2 step 1) also writes `manual_only` on the occurrence's record, the request record of a one-time request or, for a Billing Period, its period record, creating that record when the period has none, at the version it read, unless the record holds the mark already. So no attempt made by hand exists without its mark, and no executor, nor an app paying a period by its own policy (Section 8.2, Paying periods), pays that occurrence automatically again, also once the attempt has failed or the service has deleted its claim. The mark withholds automatic attempts and a resume's repeats of automatic attempts (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties); the repeat of an attempt its user started, the one that wrote the mark included, finishes the user's own payment and is judged as any repeat of a payment the user confirmed (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares). The claim checks still hold an occurrence whose claim shows an attempt its record doesn't mark (Section 7.1, No automatic payment of doubtful claims; Doubtful obligations, below). While the request record of a recurring request holds the mark, no executor accepts that request and none pays one of its Billing Periods: the executor reads that record immediately before the acceptance and before each such dispatch, and a read that fails or doesn't read withholds both. Deferring an occurrence stays in the executor's own state (Section 16).

**Ends reach the executor.** Any app with `paykit_allowance` may end an Allowance (Section 17, question 11). An app that ends an Allowance, hands it off, or records an `allowance_end` from the counterparty also sends `executor_notice` to the executor through its own identity's inbox (Pubky Switch spec, Section 7), so the executor learns of it even if the record is rewritten afterwards. An app that has verified a counterparty's `allowance_end` MUST keep it durably in its own state before it does anything else with it: for a message it receives, in the commit that passes the entry, with no service operation in that step. That kept copy is the stop, not the Allowance record: from then on the app MUST make no new attempt under that Allowance, and a restart, or a record write that failed or is still pending, never lifts it. It still sends `executor_notice`, and records the end in the Allowance record by a later write of its own as soon as it can; until then the identity's other apps may not know of it, the same exposure as an end not yet delivered. It keeps the copy at once whenever it holds the Allowance record, also one whose value doesn't read or is sealed under a scope key it doesn't hold yet: the stop never waits for a scope key, and giving up the entry never lifts it (Section 8.1, Stored entries). It drops the copy once the Allowance record holds an end or a rejection. While it can't yet check an end against the record, it keeps every distinct end of the Allowance, none in place of another, each counting under those bounds but the earliest; once the record reads, it takes them in the order the inbox accepted them, as kept replies are ordered (Section 8.1, Replies before their record): the first that names what the record holds ends the Allowance, and the others are reflected or dropped. A copy for an Allowance whose record the app doesn't hold, or whose record it reads and finds without the acceptance the end names, is kept under the bounds of a stop for a request the app doesn't hold (Section 8.1, Messages that cross), counted with those stops, and one dropped for those bounds is dropped as one never processed: the app forgets its pair in the commit that drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11) and counts it in its sender's mark of dropped replies, which asks that sender at once (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound), as for a dropped payee's cancellation (Section 8.1, Messages that cross), so the end comes back and is taken; such a copy may be dropped 30 days after the end's acceptance, judged on the service time it verified, but never while its copy of `allowances` may lack records or it keeps a stored message of that Allowance to write (Section 8.1, Stored entries); a stop it keeps for such a message counts outside these bounds and is never evicted. The strict ordinary-stop admission rule of Section 8.1, Waiting for admission, applies only where this paragraph bounds a not-yet-admitted ordinary copy. It never postpones the verified counterparty end this paragraph requires the app to keep immediately, and never removes a stop protected outside those bounds. A notice changes only the executor's consent list; ending the Allowance itself is the `allowance_end` exchanged with the counterparty. **After a restore** from backup that isn't clean (Section 7.2, What a restore loses), an executor asks the user to confirm each Allowance on its consent list again before paying under it, since its backup may predate an end. The messages an `allowances` record stores to be sent, whose original delivery a restored app may resume only with current evidence that it remains pending (Section 7.2, What a restore loses), are its own proposal while the record holds no response and no end, its own rejection while the record holds no end, never its acceptance in this revision (Section 8.3.1), and its own end always; none for a conflicted Allowance, or for one whose conflict or counterparty's end the app keeps as a stop. An `executor_notice` queued in a batch the backup kept unanswered isn't queued again after a restore, since no record stores it. Nothing is paid for that: if the batch applied, the end it went with is in the Allowance record, under which an executor consents to nothing, and an executor batch built on the record's earlier version fails (`record_version`); if it didn't, neither the end nor the notice went, and the record shows the Allowance not ended, as Recorded first says a restored app finds. This relies on the record: if a compromised own app rewrote an ended Allowance as running before the restore, the user's confirmation is the only check left. Every executor batch carries `record_version(Allowance record) == r`, so once the record changes, an executor working from the older version fails and re-reads it. Handing off means the new executor writes its own grant, with its user's consent, replacing the old one; the previous executor stops at its next batch. Paykit coordinates which app pays automatically and what it reserved. Carrying out payments, honouring grants and enforcing the Allowance's limits are the integrating app's job, and the service doesn't check them.

**Blocked counterparties.** Sending an `allowance_proposal`, `allowance_acceptance` or `allowance_rejection` requires `paykit_contacts` (Section 3.1, Outbound authorization). Without it the app refuses with `MissingScope`, signing, recording and queueing nothing; the Paykit SDK on `v0.2` refuses an acceptance before this check, as unavailable (`AllowanceAcceptanceUnavailable`, Section 8.3.1). An authorized app sends none of these messages to a counterparty its copy of the deny list holds, or to any while that copy isn't known after it tried to read the lists (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties): it refuses the user's proposal or answer, signing, recording and sending nothing, and the Allowance stays as it was. Refusing them moves no money in either role: no Allowance exists until it is accepted, and nothing is paid under a proposal, which no app accepts automatically. The user's `allowance_end` is never held or refused for the deny list: it stops payment, so it is recorded and sent at once, as a payee's cancellation is (Section 8.1). This exception applies to its eligible queued retries as well, under the ordinary delivery requirements. An end generated automatically for a proposer's withdrawal crossing the app's acceptance follows the counterparty hold in Ending, below, even if it was queued before the block or before the deny copy became unknown; the per-message decision in Section 8.1 preserves this distinction. As the allower, a refused end would let an executor pay later, or from another own app whose read of the lists works; as the allowee, the allower's executor keeps paying until the end reaches it. An `executor_notice` goes only between the identity's own apps and is never held for a counterparty's block: one that goes with an end goes when the end is recorded, also while the end's message to the counterparty is held (Ending, below).

**Valid terms.** Terms that aren't valid by `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.15 are refused when proposed, dropped when the proposal arrives, make an Allowance record that holds them invalid (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), and are checked again before each automatic payment. An executor never pays under them.

**Recorded first.** An own app records its proposal, acceptance or rejection in the Allowance record, in a write at the version it read; in this revision it records no acceptance of its own (Section 8.3.1). How an acceptance is recorded on the Allower's account with its initial ledger is specified with its implementation (Section 8.3.1). An own message is sent only after the complete required batch is verified applied, as Section 8.2 does for a recurring request's answer. A write that fails its version check sends nothing: the app reads the record again. So, while the record's history is retained (Section 6, When a record is lost), two own apps can't send different proposals under one `allowance_id` or different responses to one proposal. A restored app can recover the signed message from that record. Its delivery outcome is a separate question under Section 7.2, What a restore loses. In v0.1 the apps of one identity shared one state (Section 3.1), and the v0.1 SDK queued a response only while the Allowance was still proposed, so one identity answered once.

**Conflicted.** As in v0.1 (`specs/allowances.md`, "Proposal"), one `allowance_id` has one proposal. A second proposal under it, from either side, whose hash differs from that of the proposal the Allowance record holds, makes the Allowance conflicted, whichever came first: neither proposal binds, and nothing is paid under it. A second response does the same: an acceptance or rejection from the proposal's recipient whose hash differs from that of the response the record holds, such as a rejection after an acceptance, an acceptance after a rejection, or another acceptance. A re-sent copy, with the same hash, is the proposal or response itself. v0.1 makes a second response invalid history rather than conflicted (Section 5). A second, different end is processed but not stored (Section 6, One message per role): an end is final, as in v0.1, where the first valid end is kept and a later one changes nothing.

- **Who writes it:** an own app with `paykit_allowance`, executors included (Section 3.1), that verifies such a proposal or response, received or sent, MUST keep it durably in its own state before it does anything else with it, as it keeps a counterparty's `allowance_end` (Ends reach the executor, above); that kept copy is the stop. It writes the message into the record's `conflicted` member (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.4) by a later write of its own as soon as it can, but for a proposal over a record it holds as deleted, which it writes nowhere (Ending, below). Each identity's apps write only their own identity's record, so the counterparty records the conflict in its own.
- **Never cleared:** as in v0.1, no later event makes a conflicted Allowance usable: no message, grant or user confirmation lifts it, and a later write keeps `conflicted` (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.4, Seen roles). The record has no current grant, so no executor pays under it. A conflict the app keeps as a stop, not yet in the record, stays for good as well: a record written later that doesn't conflict with the message kept, such as one an own app brings back after a delete with the proposal that was taken as conflicted over it, lifts nothing.
- **Nothing is sent:** an app sends no acceptance, rejection or end for a conflicted Allowance and writes no grant for it, as in v0.1. The counterparty is told nothing: it sent or received each of the two conflicting messages, so it finds the conflict itself. Apps with `paykit_allowance` show the user the Allowance as conflicted.
- **Attempts already open:** a conflict cancels no payment already started, as at a handoff. An executor makes no new attempt under a conflicted Allowance, and every executor batch carries `record_version(Allowance record) == r`, so a batch built on a version before the write of `conflicted` fails. An attempt whose check before the dispatch (Section 7.2 step 1) finds the Allowance conflicted pays nothing and writes `failed`; one already dispatched finishes, sends its proof and is resolved as Section 7.3 says. An end or a rejection is different: a resume's repeat of a dispatch under the Allowance (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares), whose first call may not have reached the payment method, such as one the app crashed on during the call, is made only while a read of the Allowance record made immediately before the repeat doesn't show it ended or rejected and the executor keeps no stop for it (Ends reach the executor, above); a read that fails, or a record that doesn't read, holds it too. Held, it pays nothing more, its ledger entry stays reserved, and the attempt stays open, "may be paid", until the payment method's history or a receipt settles it, since nothing tells whether the first call was made. A handoff or a conflict doesn't hold a repeat. Its ledger entry counts as reserved until its outcome comes, and the executor that held the last grant records that outcome in the ledger, whose writes are still checked against the grant history (Who wrote it, below).

**Ledger.** The ledger record holds an entry for each attempt made under the Allowance: the obligation of the request or, for a Billing Period, of that period (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3), the `attempt_id`, value, asset and time `at`. The value is the amount the payment transfers in the requested asset, never less than the requested amount (Section 7.2 step 1; Section 8.2, Rates and payment time); fees aren't counted. It also holds running totals of spent amounts and payment counts for entries already compacted.

- **Who wrote it:** the ledger's first write is the acceptance mechanism's (Section 8.3.1). Every ordinary later ledger write is accepted only from the executor whose grant the Allowance record held at the version that write was conditioned on.
