The Allowance record keeps every grant it has held, oldest first, each with the record version that the write adding it built on (the version its writer read, Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3), which the writer knows before the service gives the new one; the last one is the current grant. Each grant's signature covers that version, so a history with a wrong version doesn't verify. Writers only append to it, and an app that has seen an entry ignores a write that changes or drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2); for an app that hasn't, a dropped entry only makes later ledger writes fail the check, which stops automatic payment rather than trusting a ledger. To check a ledger version, an app takes the last grant whose write built on a version older than the one the ledger write was conditioned on: it must verify for the identity, the counterparty and the Allowance, name the current terms hash, and belong to the writer. This works for versions the app never saw. A ledger write without that condition, or checked against a history that doesn't verify, isn't accepted either. A ledger written by any other app stops automatic payment under that Allowance until the user confirms again: the executor fails closed instead of trusting lower usage, and after the confirmation writes the ledger again (After a confirmation, below).
- **Building on a version accepts it:** a ledger write built on a ledger version accepts that version, so the executor builds on one only once it passes every check: the kept version (Last ledger version, below), no write of the Allowance record or the ledger by a removed app after its `distrust_from` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.2 step 3), Who wrote it, and the ledger rules (From one version to the next, below). This holds for every ledger write it makes: the executor batch, an outcome, an outcome copied from a claim, a compaction and a release. If the ledger fails them, the executor writes no ledger version on it, and the Allowance needs its user's confirmation again; an outcome batch then records the attempt's outcome on its claim alone.
- **A grant implies a ledger:** how acceptance establishes the Allower's initial ledger is specified with its implementation (Section 8.3.1). Before a grant can authorize payment, its executor establishes a current complete anchor: a valid handoff or successful complete-authority repair. An absent, deleted, unreadable or recovered ledger never means zero. Reacceptance, another grant and user confirmation do not initialize it again. A visible delete is judged by its writer like any other write; only absence contradicting a kept version is a rollback (Last ledger version). Otherwise the applicable missing-history hold remains.
- **From one version to the next:** the executor checks each newer ledger version against the last version it accepted (Last ledger version, below) before it builds on it, and builds on none that counts less: it pays nothing automatically under the Allowance while the ledger shown counts less (Building on a version accepts it). It checks the newer version against the terms of the `allowances` record it holds for that Allowance, whose longest period limit decides which entries may have been compacted, by the longest period at each time from the service time of the version it accepted to the newer write's, or, without one, its latest verified service time: an entry may have left if at some such time it was older than the longest period then plus 60 s, since a write between that it didn't see may have compacted it then (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.5). An app that only reads the ledger takes each newer version as the reference client hands it; it checks and flags one that counts less (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2) once the client hands the protocol's check the write's service time, which it doesn't yet (Section 17, question 37). An entry keeps its obligation, `attempt_id`, value, asset and time. Its outcome only goes from none to `done` or `failed`, or from `failed` to `done`. It leaves only by compaction, once old enough: a `done` one into the totals, and one the reader held without an outcome or `failed` into the compacted failed list, or into the totals, since it may have become `done` before it was compacted. A compacted failed attempt leaves that list only into the totals, and the totals never grow by less than what left them. New entries may come in any time.
- **Time of an entry:** each executor batch carries `server_time_in(at, at + 60 s)`, with `at` taken from the server time of the service's latest response and the interval cut short at the next boundary of any anchored limit. The entry therefore lies in exactly one period of every anchored limit. For a rolling limit of length L it counts from `at` until `at + 60 s + L`. The service can't write inside the sealed ledger, so the executor states the time and the condition makes the service vouch for it: whatever an executor's own clock says, its entries are dated within 60 s of when the service applied them, so every app and every later executor counts each payment in the same period.
- **Usage:** each app derives usage over the entries as `specs/allowances.md` defines it, for every period limit, the per-payment limit and the lifetime limit, with server time in place of the wallet's admission time and watermark. Each entry counts by the outcome of the attempt it names:

| Attempt outcome (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2) | Counts as |
|---|---|
| `open` | Reserved |
| `unknown` | Reserved, until reported paid or failed |
| `done` | Spent |
| `failed`, whoever wrote it | Spent, unless the executor's durable journal proves the attempt never dispatched (`failed` isn't final, below) |

- **`failed` isn't final:** a payment reported failed after the payment method's call may still complete (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares). A `failed` attempt counts as spent immediately, while its claim remains and after it is gone, whoever wrote the failure: its owner, another app, or the executor at the user's request. This applies to an entry's own `failed`, an entry without an outcome whose claim shows `failed`, and a compacted failed attempt. It releases only when the executor's durable journal proves that the attempt was ended before the payment method's call and can never be dispatched; an entry at the call, an ambiguous result, a user's confirmation alone and a journal restored from a backup prove none of that. A release requested by the user without that evidence is refused. A claim or ledger entry that shows `done`, or a payer record holding a proof of this attempt naming this Allowance, always spends, even beside such journal evidence. No readable payer record, a record without a proof, and a proof of another attempt or naming no Allowance or another one provide no evidence for a release. The current executor writes `done` into an entry, or moves a compacted failed attempt into the totals, when the claim shows `done` or its copy of the request's or Billing Period's payer record holds that proof, whichever executor made the attempt and whether or not the claim is still kept. The service keeps a failed attempt's claim until 30 days after its deadline (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), and the ledger keeps the entry or its compacted failed item visible to claim-based counting at least as long. A later `done` from the payee's receipt (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2 row 3) therefore confirms spending already counted; it never consumes capacity another obligation was allowed to use. After that window a receipt can't be recorded on the claim: an executor that gets it marks its entry `done`, or moves the compacted failed item into the totals, all the same. Every app that gets it and holds the failure shows the user that a payment marked failed was paid (Section 7.2). A compacted failed attempt counts by the same rule in the lifetime amount until the executor moves a later `done` into the totals, once.
- **One entry per attempt:** a retry after a failed attempt is a new attempt with its own entry, so every attempt is counted once.
- **Outcomes in the ledger:** the current executor writes each attempt's outcome into its entry as soon as it learns it, from the feed or, once the claim's entries are stubs, by reading the claim again (Switch wire 7.4.8 and 7.5), including outcomes of a previous executor's attempts, in the same batch when it records the outcome itself, so usage doesn't depend on claims that may since have been deleted (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3). It writes `done` from a claim, `failed` only when the attempt's owner wrote it or the user released the amount with the never-dispatched evidence above, and a later `done` over a `failed`, for which it reads the claims of `failed` entries again. An entry without an outcome whose claim is gone counts as spent.
- **Compaction:** when the record nears its size limit, the executor drops entries whose outcome is final and which are older than the longest period limit plus 60 s, by the latest service time it has verified. When a ledger write that adds an entry would take the record over the 256 KiB limit for a shared record value even after compaction, the executor makes no automatic payment under the Allowance and tells its user. It counts the record with an outcome in every entry that has none yet, so the outcome of every attempt it holds can still be written. It adds the paid amounts and counts of `done` ones to the running totals, and keeps each `failed` one's `attempt_id`, obligation and value in the totals, so that a later `done` still counts, once. A ledger with an entry in another asset than the Allowance's, an attempt listed twice, or a value or time that doesn't parse is invalid: usage and compaction refuse it rather than count it as less.
- **Local usage:** computed usage is only a cache, rebuilt from a complete authoritative ledger and, for entries without an outcome, the request claims. The complete ledger value, its authority and pending write described below are durable safety state, never a disposable cache. Every automatic payment requires that authority; repair, confirmation and a later own rewrite cannot bypass an incomplete-history hold.
- **Last ledger version:** the executor keeps the last ledger write it wrote or read with an authenticated version, from the transition proof it got with the write or the verified stub of the feed item (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3), and the write's item hash. A version number alone proves nothing, since the service gives it. Before an automatic payment it reads the ledger record: the write shown must be that one, by item hash, or one signed as built on that version or a later one (its `expected_version`). Any other write, or a missing ledger record, is a rollback (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1). Two cases aren't. A write whose signature verifies with its writer's certificate, that is the kept write or is signed as built on it or later, but that fails a check by policy, such as its writer's standing, Who wrote it or the ledger rules, only pauses the Allowance: the executor refuses it as untrusted (Building on a version accepts it). And a record is absent after a delete the executor saw. A write that fails the checks of Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3 never reaches the executor. Signed evidence of misbehaviour, such as an item that doesn't match its chain stub or a proof that contradicts the verified chain, is a rollback the reference client raises itself (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1). A write whose failure rests only on the service's word, such as a signature that doesn't verify with its writer's certificate, a missing date, or a version missing or not plausible, is ignored by the client, naming no app, and raises no alarm: the executor holds the write it held before and builds on nothing it wasn't shown, so its next ledger write puts back what it trusts or fails at the service, and nothing is paid on the write ignored. An executor builds only on the ledger write a handoff to it names (`ledger_handoff`, Handoff, below), or on one signed as built on it or later, or on a ledger its user confirmed by the repair (After a confirmation, below), never on a version the service shows it alone. A new executor may establish authority through a `ledger_handoff` that counts for its current grant or successful repair from complete usage authority under that grant (Successful repair, below). Until one of those succeeds it builds on no ledger except for that repair and pays nothing automatically. Merely holding a version changes nothing: this includes an observer copy, one kept when writing its grant and one from an earlier term, as when the grant history goes N, P, N. The earlier complete checkpoint may support repair only under the exact-current-value and uninterrupted-history conditions below. Once one counts, the named write is its kept version (Handoff, below, says how it meets a version it holds), so the write shown must be that one or one signed as built on it or a later one and pass every other check of Building on a version accepts it; from then on it keeps the write it built on. An executor restored from a backup without an accepted complete anchor for its current term needs repair. The backup alone supplies no such anchor; the ordinary later-ledger handoff and repair conditions are unchanged. The repair requires Complete usage authority; neither a handoff hash alone nor an older backup proves that no later write was applied. So a service that shows it an older version than the last one written can't make it build on that one: against the named write an older one is a rollback, and the repair counts every attempt the counterparty's claims and the identity's own records show. A split view that also spans the previous executor, or hides from the repair the claims it reads, isn't covered (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1, Not covered: a split view).
- **After a confirmation:** an Allowance whose ledger the executor didn't accept needs a repair with complete usage authority, which the user confirms with its figures shown. The executor first shows the user what the repair would write: the usage per period limit, amount and count, the lifetime amount, the entries reserved, spent and released, and the amounts counted for a `failed` another app wrote. The user confirms those figures, and the confirmation writes exactly that ledger; if anything it was built from changed in between, the executor refuses and shows the new figures. The repair writes the ledger again, as a merge that never counts less: the last ledger version it accepted, which it keeps with its content, as it was; what the ledger shown adds to it (entries it lacks, a `done`, higher totals); every attempt its journal holds under the Allowance; and every attempt of an app named in the grant history that the claims of the counterparty's obligations show, unless the payment's verified proof names no Allowance or another one. Complete usage authority below is required before any figures can be confirmed. Without it repair refuses, even when every input is empty. A shown unaccepted ledger and surviving attempts may only add to already complete usage; neither establishes that no attempt is missing. The version kept stays as it was: its entries and `totals.failed` items keep their values and times, every reader holding it checks the next version against it, and only a `done` another input shows is taken; own evidence showing one of its attempts at a larger value, or dated at or after the end of that entry's executor window (`at` plus 60 seconds, cut at the next boundary of any anchored limit), refuses the repair. Evidence within that window is the batch the entry already counts. Thus the rewrite counts no less than the identity's own evidence, per attempt, at the service time the attempt was made. For an attempt the version kept doesn't list, the entry takes what counts it most among the inputs: the largest value, the latest time, a time after the repair's counting as the repair's, the obligation of the journal or the claim, and a `done` any input shows. A `failed` the ledger shown writes is never taken, and its `totals.failed` item stays one only for an attempt neither the journal nor a claim shows; otherwise the attempt is an entry, counted by its claim in every limit. So a copy written by a removed app, or a write the app doesn't accept, never counts an attempt the payer's own evidence shows at less than that evidence does. A journal attempt takes the transferred-amount evidence described below and its claim's `opened_at`, or, with no claim showing it, the time it was journaled. The ledger shown adds nothing when its value doesn't read as the Allowance's ledger, or when the reference client refused its write, which it hands without content (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2): the repair then adds the copy the app trusts and builds on the refused write's version; while the value isn't open yet, its scope key not held, the repair waits. The counterparty's obligations are those of its one-time requests in the Allowance's asset, and of its recurring ones every Billing Period listed in `attempted_periods`, every one whose period record the app holds, and every one from the first to the one containing the latest service time verified whose claim the service may still hold: with a payment deadline, those whose deadline plus 30 days hasn't passed; without one, every period, as such a claim stays 400 days after its last entry. A done period leaves `attempted_periods`, and its claim may still show it. A request with more such periods than the repair reads refuses the repair. Every payment a payer or period record's proof shows made under the Allowance counts as `done`, also once its claim is gone, but its amount must be established by the transferred-amount rule below; a request proof carries no amount. An attempt is left out as made otherwise only on the payer identity's own evidence: the executor's own journal entry of it naming no Allowance or another one, or a proof of it in the payer's record naming no Allowance or another one, signed by the attempt's owner while that app isn't removed. A record holds only proofs its payer sent (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3), so the counterparty can't supply one, and a proof another own app signed shows nothing of how the owner made the attempt. A payer record or a claim that can't be read refuses the repair, as does a period record whose request record isn't held, whose amount can't be told, and a ledger divergent or seen so and not confirmed (Section 6). The version kept is checked first, whatever else the write shown is: a write that is neither that version nor built on it, a refused one whose version the client follows being before it included, is a rollback, whose alarm holds every attempt, and isn't repaired; while that alarm stands no Allowance is repaired. The repair writes the outcomes the claims show into the merge, and compacts it near the size limit as an executor batch's ledger is, keeping room for an outcome of every attempt it holds; one that doesn't fit then refuses. The merge may count an attempt twice, never less: an entry the ledger shown lists that the version kept compacted into its totals is added again, since nothing in the inputs tells it apart from one the version kept never held, and a hand attempt by an app the grant history names, with no proof or journal entry of it yet, counts under the Allowance. What it was built from is the Allowance record version, the version kept, the ledger write shown and the version the repair builds on, the journal's attempts, the payer records read and the step of every claim read; the repair's write carries `record_version(Allowance record)`, conditions every payer record read at its version, every period record absent from an obligation it walked at its absence, and every claim read at its step; it builds on that exact current authoritative ledger version and carries `server_time_in(at, end)` with an executor batch's `end`, `at + 60 s` cut at the next boundary of any anchored limit, so the figures the user confirmed are the ones in force when it lands. Only the current executor repairs, and only an Allowance it otherwise pays under: the confirmation is the ledger's alone, so after a restore that isn't clean, or over an Allowance record write the executor ignores, its user confirms the Allowance again first (Ends reach the executor; Executor).
- **Successful repair:** confirmation alone leaves the handoff hold in place. Once the repair's applied result is verified and durably incorporated, the executor keeps its full complete value, authenticated result version and item hash, bound to the exact current grant. That result is its accepted anchor and satisfies the automatic-payment gate as an alternative to a valid handoff; every other ledger, payment and pause check still applies. A pending, refused or applied-but-not-durably-incorporated repair supplies no exit. The repair also retains the exact authenticated base version and item hash on which its first repair since the handoff was conditioned, separately from the result anchor, for the late-handoff checks below. A later `ledger_handoff` is compared to that base, never substituted for the result or allowed to roll usage back. A grant change requires authority for the new term; it does not reuse an earlier term's repair permission without the handoff or complete-checkpoint repair checks. Thus A may hand a complete ledger to B, B may make no ledger write and go offline, and A may resume after the user hands back and confirms a repair of its still-exact-current complete checkpoint: the verified applied repair establishes A's current anchor without waiting for B to return.
- **Complete usage authority:** repair requires the complete usage of the Allowance, including attempts no surviving record enumerates. The executor's durable state keeps the full accepted ledger value, including cumulative `totals.spent`, `totals.count` and every `totals.failed` item, its authenticated version and item hash, the executor term that makes it authoritative, and any prepared ledger write whose result has not been accounted for. An attempt leaves the entries only by the prescribed compaction into those retained totals or failed items. Expiry of claims and activity records, and pruning a journal, never remove that usage. An older accepted ledger is insufficient without its complete accounted-for continuation.
- **Keeping that authority:** each executor instance serializes its ledger writes per Allowance. After the initial ledger (Section 8.3.1), every ordinary advance of this authority must build on the exact current complete value. A write over an unaccounted gap, even this executor's own signed rewrite, cannot manufacture complete authority. Before submitting one, it durably keeps the exact prepared batch and proposed ledger value with the preceding complete ledger. It submits no further ledger write until it has durably incorporated a verified applied result, or a verified definitive refusal proves the batch did not apply. A lost response, an unknown result or a failure without that proof keeps the prepared write pending across restart and retry. This includes executor batches, outcomes (also `failed` and `unknown`), copied outcomes, compactions, releases, repairs and put-backs. A terminal payment or expired claim does not resolve a ledger write. A crash or failed local commit before recording the result keeps that write pending; repair and handoff wait. The retained state needs one complete ledger and at most one prepared write, not lifetime history. If it cannot be stored, no further ledger write or automatic payment is submitted.
- **Concurrent instances and missing continuation:** serialization within an instance is not proof that another instance of the same executor submitted nothing. Before repair, the current ledger must be the exact authenticated complete value this instance holds, or its exact prepared write whose verified applied result it has now incorporated. Repair carries that current version as its `expected_version`, so any concurrent ledger write makes the repair fail and compute again. A higher version, the same signing app or grant, and a signed `expected_version` merely at or after the kept version do not account for an unseen continuation. The anchor and the current write must also belong to the same uninterrupted account history: after provider loss, an older matching copy cannot show that another instance had no later applied write in the lost history. A recovery upload or generation change never proves that continuation. An unaccounted remote write, a missing or deleted current value, or a put-back of an older value refuses repair; confirmation cannot bless it as complete. No current API is assumed to enumerate that missing continuation. A complete value obtained by the valid handoff below may establish the required current anchor, but an older handoff cannot account for subsequent writes by another instance. A complete checkpoint retained from an earlier executor term or in a backup can support a user-confirmed repair only if its prior complete authority is retained, its full value and authenticated anchor still exactly match the current ledger in the same uninterrupted account history, and every known prepared write is resolved. A ledger merely read as an observer has no such prior authority. The current-version CAS fences still-unapplied writes from another instance; it cannot undo a write already lost from history.
- **When complete usage is lost:** repair without this authority writes nothing and leaves the Allowance unavailable for automatic payments, including new obligations and later Billing Periods with no doubtful attempt of their own. Bounded claim and activity retention may leave an empty inventory even though compacted lifetime usage was nonzero. Surviving attempts and their individually known amounts, an unaccepted shown ledger, elapsed period windows, user confirmation, regrant, provider generation changes and older-copy put-back never replace missing inventory or totals. A retained complete value and accounted-for continuation, meeting the current-write or handoff rules above, can restore authority; if every such copy is lost, automatic use of this Allowance is unavailable indefinitely. The host reports incomplete usage history, not zero spending or renewed capacity. Payment by hand remains a separate authorization subject to every occurrence hold; it does not repair or reset this Allowance. A distinct newly authorized Allowance grants new authority under its own identity and limits and existing association rules, never reclassifies old spending. These durable-state and refusal rules are required in the repair implementation; no current repair API or vector supplies a complete-inventory proof.
- **Amounts recovered by a repair:** every included attempt needs durable evidence of the amount transferred, or the exact transfer amount bound before its possible dispatch, in the Allowance's asset, including endpoint rounding and excluding fees (Sections 7.2 and 8.2). A retained accepted ledger entry or `totals.failed` item supplies its accounted value. The executor's own durable journal supplies an amount only when it records that transfer bound for the same attempt, obligation, Allowance and asset; a journal containing only the nominal request or Billing Period amount does not. A claim's outcome and `opened_at`, a payer or period record's proof without an amount, the current endpoint's smallest unit or rates, and the user's confirmation cannot reconstruct an earlier transfer amount. The request's nominal amount is never substituted, even when the attempt is still on its claim, was reported failed, or its proof is the only remaining payment evidence. An unaccepted shown ledger may increase the repair's figures but cannot by itself establish the missing amount of an attempt found through a claim, proof or journal. The repair preserves every accepted larger value and applies the conservative merge above only after each such amount is established. Otherwise it refuses without writing a smaller ledger: the Allowance remains paused for automatic payments, possibly indefinitely if the evidence cannot be recovered. This rule applies equally to one-time and recurring obligations, claim-only, proof-only and journal reconstruction, and after restore or provider loss; it changes neither the default-spent failure rule nor its requirement for durable never-dispatched evidence.

**Paying under an Allowance:**

1. The executor reads the Allowance record, the ledger record and the request's claim.
2. It recomputes usage and checks the terms and limits at the current server time, and the transfer (Section 7.2 step 1): a destination that transfers another amount, or one the method can't tell, isn't paid automatically, nor is a payment priced by rates, the request's fixed rates or period details' (Section 8.2). It pays nothing automatically while its device clock and the service's differ by more than the clock tolerance (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1, Clock bound). Terms are checked whole against the table of `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.15; a time that doesn't parse fails closed, never as no limit.
3. It sends one batch:
   - the `attempt` on the request's claim;
   - the ledger record with the new entry, at its next version;
   - the activity record showing the payment as being handled;
   - when the activity record is the period record of a Billing Period not listed in `attempted_periods`, a write of the request record that adds it (Section 8.2); a recurring request's record names its Allowance from its acceptance on, so this write never names one;

   with the conditions `record_version(Allowance record) == r`, `server_time_in(at, at + 60 s)` and, on the attempt, the request's `deadline` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2), the version of the request record it read (Section 7.2) and, for a Billing Period, the period's start as its not-before (Section 8.2).
4. It follows Section 7.2. Proofs carry `allowance_id`.

- **Conflicts:** if any check fails, nothing is written and the executor recomputes. The ledger record's version serializes every executor batch for the Allowance; a change to the terms, the response, the end or the grant changes the Allowance record's version; a late batch fails `server_time_in`. If another wallet is already attempting the request, the attempt fails and nothing is added to the ledger.
- **Doubtful obligations:** an executor never attempts an obligation with an `unknown` or orphaned attempt, or whose next attempt would be flagged `possibly_duplicate` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.2). Nor does it attempt one whose claim holds an attempt that no entry or `totals.failed` item of the ledger of the Allowance the occurrence is paid under names, one made by hand or otherwise outside that Allowance, whatever its outcome, as in v0.1, where "Manual payments consume no Allowance capacity but exclude automatic payment of the same occurrence" (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Durable Allowance payment accounting"): once an occurrence has been attempted outside its Allowance, it is paid again only by hand.
- **One Allowance per request:** an executor's acceptance of a subscription under an Allowance (Section 8.2), or the first automatic payment of a one-time request, names its Allowance in the request record, in that batch, and every later automatic payment of the request or of its Billing Periods is made only under that one, as in v0.1, or under the one the user moves a later period to (below); a recurring request whose record names none has no period paid under an Allowance (Section 8.2, Paying periods). When more than one accepted Allowance covers a request whose record names none, which one an executor accepts or pays it under is its own choice, as in v0.1, where the wallet selects one of the candidates (`specs/allowances.md`, "Payment Request integration"): it pays under that one alone, and its batch names it in the request record, so later occurrences, but for a move the user makes (below), and every own app follow that choice. An Allowance that has ended, or expired at its terms' `expires_at`, pays nothing more of the request; the executor never picks another one for it, and no app pays it automatically without one: each occurrence is left to payment by hand, after its user's confirmation, as in v0.1. A period or lifetime limit without room for a payment blocks only the Billing Period it affects, and later periods are checked again against the capacity then, as in v0.1 (`specs/allowances.md`, "One-time and recurring requests"). A handoff changes the Allowance's executor, not the Allowance, so the new executor pays the request's later occurrences under it. Only the user moves a recurring request's later Billing Periods, of a subscription accepted under an Allowance and not cancelled, to another accepted Allowance of the request's counterparty, neither ended nor conflicted, whose terms match the request as at acceptance (Section 8.2, Accepting), as in v0.1 (`specs/allowances.md`, "Authorized recurring reassociation"); its `active_from` may be later, since the move authorizes no payment by itself and the terms' window and capacity are checked when each period is paid, as in v0.1, where "the accepted replacement need not be active yet" (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Durable Allowance payment accounting"): an app adds an entry to the request record's `reassociations` after its user confirms it, naming the Allowance and the first Billing Period it covers, one that hasn't started and is later than every period in `attempted_periods`. A period's Allowance is the one of the last entry starting at or before it, else the named one. Usage already counted stays in the earlier Allowance's ledger. An app that has seen the name or an entry ignores and flags a write that changes or drops it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2; `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3).
- **Limits:** the executor enforces the Allowance limits. Paykit coordinates the reservation (one ledger version, one terms version, server time) and never enforces spending limits at the protocol level, so a faulty or malicious executor can still exceed them.

**Handoff:** outstanding attempts keep counting as reserved until their outcomes arrive. The previous executor may still record the outcomes of its own attempts on their claims, without touching the ledger, which only the current executor writes; the new executor copies those outcomes into the ledger once it builds on a ledger version (below). Any other executor batch of the previous executor fails the Allowance record condition. A handoff doesn't cancel payments already started. When it learns of the handoff, from the `executor_notice` or the Allowance record, the previous executor names its last ledger version for the new one, which it keeps until then: only while it retains complete usage authority, the current ledger exactly matches its complete value and every ledger write it sent has a verified answer incorporated into that state, it writes `ledger_handoff` into the Allowance record, at the version it read, with the version and item hash of its last ledger version (Last ledger version, above) and the grant that handed the Allowance on, signed with its own certificate so that no later writer of the record can change it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.4). It writes none when it holds no ledger version, or only one a restore from a backup installed, which may predate its own later writes, or when its last ledger version isn't one it wrote or took as its kept version by a `ledger_handoff` since its own grant, such as one it held only as an app that reads the ledger or from an earlier term of its own, so a second handoff before the first one's `ledger_handoff` leaves the next executor the repair, or once the record holds a grant of another app after the one that handed the Allowance on from it. A grant the same app signs again, at a confirmation or after its own restore, is no handoff: that app is still the executor, under the rules of a restore (Ends reach the executor and Last ledger version, both above). A `ledger_handoff` that doesn't verify, or isn't signed by the app the grant history names just before the grant it names, is ignored and flagged (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2); one for a grant other than the one that handed the Allowance to the current executor, or whose app has since been removed, counts for nothing. Until a `ledger_handoff` counts for the grant that handed the Allowance to it, or a complete-authority repair under the current grant succeeds and durably establishes its result anchor, the new executor builds on no ledger except for that repair and pays nothing automatically. An earlier-term version alone grants no permission when the history goes N, P, N (Last ledger version, above): it may omit the previous executor's later payments. Repair is available only with Complete usage authority; it cannot reconstruct missing inventory from surviving records. If the previous executor is offline, removed or lost and no complete authority can be obtained, automatic payment remains unavailable, even with the user's confirmation. Once one counts, it builds on the write it names. Until it has read the named write itself, it holds only its version and item hash: a repair then waits: the version and hash do not supply the complete ledger value or its compacted totals. A version it holds when that `ledger_handoff` reaches it is checked against the named write, by version: before it has written a ledger version since the handoff, it takes the named write as its kept version unless the one it holds is later, and flags a named write before the one it holds, or another write at the same version (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2); once it has, including through repair before the `ledger_handoff`, the named write must be the write its first ledger write since the handoff built on, and one later than that, another write at that version, or any named write where it built on the ledger's absence is a rollback (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1), since it then built on a version older than the previous executor's last one, while one before it is flagged. When an executor stops being current, apps holding `paykit_allowance` prompt the user to choose another.

**Ending:** an app with `paykit_allowance` stores its `allowance_end` in the Allowance record's `end`, at its expected version, and sends it only after that write succeeds, as a payer records its cancellation first (Section 8.1). A proposer's withdrawal, an end naming the proposal alone, can cross the acceptance: the app whose identity accepted then holds a record that can't take it (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.16). That app ends the Allowance itself, unless it is conflicted (Conflicted, above), with an end naming the proposal and the acceptance, stored and sent as above, and the withdrawal is reflected by its `msg_id`, so the Allowance has ended on both sides, as in v0.1, where a withdrawal crossing an acceptance ends it (`specs/allowances.md`, "End"). While sends to the counterparty are held, including for missing contacts authorization (Section 3.1, Outbound authorization; Blocked counterparties, above), the app still makes that end and records it in the Allowance record at once, and queues its `executor_notice` to its own apps, so that every own app, an executor in another app included, finds the Allowance ended and a batch built on the record's earlier version fails; it holds only the end's message to the counterparty, which it sends from the record once sends to the counterparty aren't held, as a message queued again from its record and so only until 30 days after its `created_at`, less the clock tolerance (Section 7.2, What a restore loses); the end in the record stands either way. The counterparty hold applies to each attempted delivery of that generated end, including a queued retry, a kept batch's later delivery and a copy carried by reload or restore; On restore, its recorded effect and own-app notice prove neither that the delivery is still pending nor that it is a user-initiated end (Section 7.2). At the proposer, whose record holds its withdrawal, the crossing acceptance or rejection, and an end naming both, are reflected the same way. A withdrawal that crosses a rejection is reflected too, and the Allowance stays rejected, where v0.1 derives it ended: either leaves no authority. A proposal, acceptance or rejection whose Allowance record the app holds but can't read is never written over that record: while the app lacks the scope key the record's value is sealed under, its entry waits for that key in the client's store, as Stored entries (Section 8.1) says, and a message the app keeps for a later write waits for it within the 30 days Stored entries gives, since the app never writes over a value it can't open; a value that doesn't read for another reason isn't written over either: the message is kept and tried again as Stored entries (Section 8.1) says, and may be given up after 30 days. An end is kept as the stop all the same (Ends reach the executor, above). No app deletes an `allowances` record: the records stay as history. An app that reads a delete of one refuses it and puts the record back at once, as it was when its copy is the write the delete was built on, and ended otherwise (Section 6, Putting back a deleted record); until a write over the delete lands it holds the record as deleted, and under a record it holds as deleted it pays nothing, writes no grant and sends no acceptance, including when it has no previous copy and knows the key only from the visible delete (Section 6, Visible deletion without a copy). What it saw there stays seen (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), so it ignores, and shows with its writer, a later write that brings the record back without a proposal, response, end or `conflicted` message it saw, or with another in its place (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.4, Seen roles). An executor pays nothing automatically under an Allowance whose current record write is one it ignores, until its user confirms. A confirmation covers only the latest write it ignores, and only when that write's app is one its identity's `identity.json` lists now and doesn't list as removed; under any other it pays nothing automatically, whatever its user confirms. An app that holds the record as deleted takes a proposal over it as conflicted, as one `allowance_id` has one proposal (Conflicted, above): it can't tell whether the deleted record held another proposal or a response. The conflict's stop such a proposal makes is new state under the spam rule (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1): from a sender the app's copy shows blocked or flagged it is only counted, which loses nothing, since nothing is paid under a deleted record. It keeps the proposal as the stop and writes nothing at the key. An acceptance or rejection over a deleted record is kept as a reply before its record and dropped under those bounds (Section 8.1, Replies before their record), and an end is kept as the stop for an Allowance whose record the app doesn't hold (Ends reach the executor, above), a record held as deleted counting as not held, as for a payee's cancellation (Section 8.1, Messages that cross). So a faulty or compromised own app with `paykit_allowance` that deletes an Allowance record pays nothing under it while it is deleted, a denial, not a loss, and an own app puts it back before the service can forget the key. A record put back with `lost_to_delete` is ended for good, as a conflicted one is never cleared (Conflicted, above): no message, grant or confirmation makes it usable again, and a later write keeps the mark (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5, Put back over a delete). An app that joins later, or is restored from a backup taken before the record's response or end, lists the record put back and judges a later message against it.

**After provider loss** (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6): the Allowance record holds `confirmed_generation`, the generation in which its user last confirmed it. While that is lower than `recovery_from + 1` in the current `identity.json`, the executor makes no automatic payment under it. After the user confirms again, the executor writes a new grant with the current generation; no other app can end this generation pause. That confirmation establishes no complete usage authority: missing inventory or compacted totals still refuse repair and every new automatic payment under the Allowance. Earlier doubtful obligations stay blocked from automatic payment, and delayed outcomes of earlier attempts still count at their entries' times.

**Integrity:** every ledger write is a writer-signed operation, sealed and bound to its record key, so the service can't forge, alter or move amounts between Allowances. Rollback and incomplete batches are detected as in Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.1.

#### 8.3.1 Acceptance and initial ledger authority

Allowance acceptance is unavailable in this revision, and no new authority to pay under an Allowance is established through acceptance. No app writes an acceptance that would newly fill an Allowance record's `response`: an app refuses a direct acceptance, and keeps such a received acceptance unplaced, as a message its record can't take now (Section 8.1, Stored entries). An acceptance after a different recorded response still makes the Allowance conflicted (Section 8.3, Conflicted), and one crossing the proposer's withdrawal is still reflected by its `msg_id` (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.16). A restore queues no own acceptance again (Section 7.2, What a restore loses). The Paykit SDK on `v0.2` also holds every delivery of an acceptance, whatever path sends it. Rejections, ends and conflicts keep their rules. The mechanism by which acceptance establishes the Allower's initial ledger, and the executor's initial anchor, is specified with its implementation.

### 8.4 Paying without a request

- **Destinations:** the payee's latest Private Payment List from the `payment_lists` record, or its public endpoints. Private and public payment are separate modes, as in v0.1: the app or the user chooses one explicitly, and a payer never changes from one to the other on its own. The app that published the destination must be current (Section 7.2).
- **Record first:** before paying, the payer writes, in one batch, the `attempt` on the payment's own claim (its obligation is derived from the attempt ID alone, Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3, and its `deadline` is 10 minutes after the latest service time the app has seen, so its tombstone goes 30 days after that, at the end of its reconciliation window, rather than 400 days after its last entry: there is no re-sent copy for it to stop), a `payer_activity` record for the payment, keyed by the attempt ID, and, for a single-use endpoint, the `payment_lists` record marking it used, at its next version. Two own apps therefore can't both pay one single-use endpoint, and a crash after paying leaves an attempt that shows "may be paid" and is resolved like any other (Section 7.3).
- **Without the switch:** a payment without a request to a public endpoint (Section 4) may go ahead while the payer's own switch doesn't answer. Its obligation is its own, derived from the attempt ID, so no other app can attempt it, and a public endpoint has nothing to reserve. The app still checks the destination and its own `identity.json` (fresh within 60 s and naming the service the app uses, so a switch the identity moved away from is followed first, Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3), judges the deny list as Section 8.1 says, so it pays only on a copy of the lists read at most 10 minutes before and none while it holds no such copy, journals the payment, checks the transfer, the freshness of its `identity.json` and its copy of the deny list once more on what it holds, reading no network after them, and pays. Once the switch answers again, recording follows Late recording below, so the identity's other apps and the payee see the payment. A payment that the payment method reports failed is taken as having moved no money, and nothing of it is written; the method's history is still asked about it while it keeps it, and one that shows it completed after all is written then, as above. While the restored app is in reconcile mode (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4) the `attempt` waits with every other new attempt. A restored app whose journal holds such a payment, not yet written, takes an `attempt` the claim shows as written, with its step and `deadline`, and otherwise asks its payment method's history, never taking the claim's silence as not paid. The switch's first returning answer does not fix a deadline for an attempt not yet created; the recording batch uses Late recording below. Until then it is in that app's journal only: if the app is restored from a backup taken before that write, the payment is lost from the shared history (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4), though not from the payment method's. Payments of requests, Billing Periods, under an Allowance or to a single-use endpoint still need the switch, since their claim or reservation is what stops a second payment, and so does a payment to any endpoint of a Private Payment List: without its switch the app can't read the payee's `payment_lists` record as the switch holds it now, so it can't tell whether another own app has paid a single-use endpoint of the list's version, which consumes the whole version (Reusable and single-use endpoints, below), or recorded a newer list from the payee, whose latest list is the one paid to (Destinations, above). A public endpoint depends on neither.
- **Late recording:** recording an already dispatched payment never dispatches it again and always uses its original attempt ID and obligation. Once success is authoritatively known, including from later method history, and reconciliation permits a new attempt, the app accounts for every batch that could have recorded that attempt before it prepares another. An uninterrupted durable submission history that shows no batch was submitted establishes that none applied; a journal restored from a backup does not. A submitted batch whose answer is missing stays unresolved: the app retries it unchanged or obtains the authenticated evidence of its result under Switch wire 6.4 and 6.8. Neither an absent claim nor a refusal of a later request proves that an earlier batch never applied; after provider loss, expiry of retained batch evidence or claim forgetting, absence remains insufficient. The app retains the unresolved batch and its operations until it can account for them, following the Switch's restore and provider-loss rules (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 12.4 to 12.6).
  - If no recording batch ever applied, and none already submitted can still apply, the app reads the claim and service time afresh, then prepares the first batch of the original `attempt` and `payer_activity` with a `deadline` 10 minutes after that verified time. This may be later than the switch's first returning answer: late success in method history, reconciliation, quota and submission delays do not consume the recording window before it starts. If this batch is delayed past its deadline, it is not submitted as a new attempt under that expired deadline. A new batch with a newly computed deadline is permitted only once the same non-application and no-longer-pending conditions hold for every earlier batch; the attempt ID and obligation stay unchanged. The app journals each batch before submission. It records the known outcome as Section 7.2 requires, with `done` and the proof only once success is known, without a payment call.
  - If the attempt already exists, the app takes its actual step and pinned deadline, including none, and reads the activity record. It never replaces the attempt or refreshes that pin. An open or unknown attempt can be completed after its deadline; a done attempt is not completed twice. A failed attempt, or a claim that was applied and later forgotten, uses the existing payment-proof and receipt correction rules (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares), not a fresh attempt. While an earlier batch might have applied but its result cannot be established, no new attempt is created and that uncertainty is retained; it is never treated as a failed payment or permission to pay again.
- **After paying:** the payer writes `done` and updates the record, as in Section 7.2, and sends a `payment_proof` with no request reference. The proof is required after paying a single-use endpoint. Only the payee's receipt settles the claim by evidence: the payer matches a receipt to the payment through its record, whose counterparty must be the receipt's sender, since the obligation doesn't name the payee.
- **Unlabelled:** a payment without a request carries no label, so its proof reaches only the payee's apps holding `paykit_request`, which record and credit it (Section 3.4).
- **Blocked payers:** public endpoints are readable by anyone, so a counterparty the payee's deny list holds can still pay one. Once the block applies, the payee's switch refuses its proof (Pubky Switch spec, Section 7), and nothing asks for it again. A payee app holding `paykit_contacts` drops a proof its inbox accepted before the block applied as one never processed, asks for it again once the counterparty is unblocked, and then judges it as any proof (Section 8.1, step 4). Otherwise such a payment shows only in the payee's payment method.
- **Credited once:** the payee records a payment without a request by its settlement: the Payment Endpoint Identifier paid and the settlement ID its payment method defines (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13.3). Every proof of one settlement names the same record, so a second proof with the same evidence, under a new `msg_id`, is a replay and isn't credited again. Its credit writes the settlement's mark as a credit does, naming this record (Section 8.1 step 4), so a proof of that settlement shown for a request or a Billing Period isn't credited too, nor one naming another endpoint, whose record would sit at another key: one transaction is one settlement (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5). A proof whose method gives no settlement ID isn't credited. Each proof type has one spelling of its settlement ID, such as a `bitcoin-txid` proof's `txid` in lower-case hex, and a proof in another spelling isn't credited, so one settlement never names two records (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5).
- **Only what it offered:** a proof that names a Private Payment List of the payee app checking it (`payment_app_id` and `list_version`, `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.10), for a request or without one, is credited by that app only for a payload it offered that payer at that version, while it still keeps the offer. It keeps what each version offered until 30 days after its `expires_at`, the time a payer keeps a proof sent before then to re-send it (Section 9); after that the record of the offer is gone. Within that time, a proof naming a payload or a version it didn't offer that payer isn't credited and gets no receipt; the proof stays in its record, and the payer gets no receipt, its claim showing what its own outcome wrote (Section 7.3). Another own app, which doesn't know what the publishing app offered, checks such a proof by its payment method alone, as any other proof (Section 8.1 and Credited once, above), and so does the publishing app after that time: a late proof of a real payment is still credited and gets its receipt (Section 7.2, What a payment method declares).
- **Reusable and single-use endpoints.** Paying to a reusable endpoint consumes nothing, so the payer can pay again at once. Paying to a single-use endpoint consumes the whole list version, as paying any endpoint did in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Resolve Private Payment"), until the publisher sends a newer one, even if the payment fails; the payer then asks for a newer list.
- **Giving payloads back:** a publishing app gives a single-use payload back to its payment method only when no payer can pay it: the app can establish that the version carrying it was never sent, the client reports `MessageUndelivered { resend: false }` or other authenticated evidence proves the recipient never accepted that version, a newer version reached that inbox first, or the app was removed from its identity. On `DeliveryUnknown`, the publisher keeps the payload reserved until it establishes that no payer can use it, and reconciles the delivery under Deliveries of unknown outcome. A failed re-send of a previously accepted list leaves that acceptance in force. A version a restore brought back before it was seen delivered is given back only when the app is removed from its identity: otherwise it stays the payment method's to expire, since the device the backup came from may have delivered it.
- **Publishing:** a publishing app sends its current list to the counterparties it chooses, for example each new contact, and a newer version whenever its list changes, when it receives a proof for one of its single-use endpoints, or when it receives a `private_payment_list_request` naming a used version, an expired one, or none (`held_version: null`). A change is owed to every counterparty that gets the app's lists, from the commit that makes it until the message carrying it is queued, so a failed queueing or a crash sends it at a later receive. The durable publication intent of Section 8.4.1 makes queue materialization idempotent: a crash just after queueing resumes the exact signed message, without preparing another publication for that intent. A version that fails to go for another reason, such as the size bound, is tried again hourly for a day, then daily.
- **Answering a list request:** a request naming the newest version the publishing app sent that counterparty counts as naming a used one, on the payer's word, and gets a newer version: a payer that reserved a single-use endpoint and then failed to pay has consumed that version without a proof. A request naming an older version that hasn't expired, used or not, gets the newest version re-sent, and nothing newly issued. A request naming an expired version, or none, gets a newer version, as above. So that a payer can't use up its single-use payloads, the app sends a counterparty at most one newer version for each version it remembers that counterparty naming (the Paykit SDK is to remember every version it still tracks, and 16 others for 30 days after their answer), and at most a small number of newer versions in any 24 hours in answer to its requests, which it chooses (the Paykit SDK is to use 4). Past the bound of one newer version per version named, a request naming a version already answered gets the newest version sent again, with nothing newly issued, so an answer lost on the way is answered; the app sends the newest version again to a counterparty at most once an hour. Past the daily bound it issues nothing newer: the request is held, and answered with a newer version once the bound allows, unless a newer version sent meanwhile, such as a renewal or a list change, answered it. A proof of one of the single-use endpoints of the newest version it sent that counterparty is answered the same way, within the same daily bound: the publishing app doesn't verify the proof before it answers, so a counterparty sending proofs can't make it issue more fresh payloads than its requests could.
- **Blocked counterparties:** while its copy of the deny list holds a counterparty (Section 8.1), or that copy isn't known after it tried to read the lists, a publishing app holding `paykit_contacts` sends it no list, renewal or answer, as in v0.1 (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "Block A Peer"). Before it sends one, it reads the lists again once its copy is 10 minutes old or more (Section 8.1). A renewal, an answer or a change owed to a counterparty it already sends its lists to is kept, and goes at the first receive after its copy shows that counterparty not blocked and nothing else holds it (Section 7.2, What a restore loses; Publishing; Answering a list request). A list the host chooses to send a counterparty is refused instead, and the host asks again. A nonempty list already queued, including a renewal or answer, is held before delivery while that counterparty is blocked or the deny copy is unknown, through every send path and after reload or restore (Section 8.1, Messages already queued). It goes only once sends are permitted and it is still eligible; its replay deadline is unchanged. A withdrawal goes all the same, as below. Unblocking ends the pause.
- **Withdrawing a list:** when the app stops sending a counterparty its lists, it sends at once an empty newer version, held sends or not: it only stops payments to endpoints the app no longer wants paid, which a payer it blocked could otherwise still pay. The publisher may retry an original withdrawal that is still pending. Once the client gives it up, a replacement requires proof that the original was never sent or never reached the recipient, such as `MessageUndelivered { resend: false }`, and follows the schedule below. The publisher never requeues a given-up message. On `DeliveryUnknown`, it reconciles the delivery under Deliveries of unknown outcome and sends no automatic replacement. A failed re-send of an accepted withdrawal preserves its original acceptance. After a restore, Section 7.2 independently requires a newly signed empty version to supersede any list the publisher may have sent after the backup. The publisher reserves it under Section 8.4.1. Retries of that fresh withdrawal follow the same delivery rules. A clean restore does not prove that a carried withdrawal is newer than the counterparty's list. An eligible retry goes an hour after the withdrawal at the earliest, then hourly for the rest of a day from the withdrawal and daily after, while a version that counterparty may hold can still be paid; one a restore's withdrawal (Section 7.2, What a restore loses) recorded whose message the client never took goes again at once. One the counterparty's switch refused for good isn't sent again, a restored one included. After a restore, until its host sets its list again, an authenticated eligible list request from a counterparty the restored state holds nothing of also causes a withdrawal. Every newly constructed withdrawal reserves the next pair from current protected authority under Section 8.4.1; a carried empty list never supplies that authority. At an unchanged generation, the withdrawals after successive restores take successive pairs of that one counter, whatever the verified times. A genuinely greater verified generation starts its own namespace; a provider change without that advancement does not. After the withdrawal the counterparty is sent no other list until the host sends it one, or, after a restore's withdrawal, until the host sets its list again.
- **No usable list:** if the list is missing, expired or used up, the payer sends `private_payment_list_request`, and sends it again at most once in any 24 hours (the Paykit SDK's interval) while it still has no usable list; a request known to be undelivered counts as not sent, and goes again at the payer's next receive. If the client reports terminal unknown delivery, the payer follows Deliveries of unknown outcome above and makes no automatic replacement ask. No request goes while the payer's copy of the deny list holds the payee or isn't known: a request that went and didn't reach the payee's inbox goes again at the payer's next receive once that ends, if that is within 24 hours of the request, and otherwise at its next payment; one held unsent goes at its next payment. A list has expired at its `expires_at` or 30 days after the payer's inbox accepted it, whichever comes first (Section 5). The payee can't be forced to answer, and doesn't answer past its bounds (Answering a list request, above), so the payer waits for the payee's next list, such as its renewal (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.13), and tells the user it is waiting for the payee's list. It may offer to pay to the payee's public endpoints instead, if there are any, but pays there only when the user chooses to. A list paused as divergent (Section 6, Divergent records) is neither missing, expired nor used up, and these pause rules apply only to a list whose generation the check below admits, since a list of a higher generation neither pauses a payment nor is paid: when every Private Payment List of the payee that would otherwise be usable is paused, a payment by hand waits for its user's confirmation or a choice of public endpoints, and the payer asks no publishing app whose list is paused for a newer one meanwhile, since the upload may hold one. A list of an app that the payee's `identity.json` doesn't list in `apps`, or lists as removed, plays no part, paused or not, nor does a list of a higher generation than that copy (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.13): the payer judges that on a fresh copy, as the destination check takes it, read before it judges any list paused, and a read of that copy that fails refuses the payment, the list taken neither as a listed app's nor as a removed app's. A list of an app that `apps` lists, paused as divergent, pauses the payment whatever certificate its held list was signed under, since the upload may hold one signed under the certificate the app is listed with now.

#### 8.4.1 Reserving Private Payment List versions

**Required Switch support.** The origin slots, lineage proofs and related service and client requirements throughout this section describe Switch revision `f0bbddd1` (`spec/record-origins.md`, Sections 1-3 and 5-8.1, `spec/record-lineage.md`, spec Section 10.3 and wire 6.9). The current pin supplies neither those contracts nor their proofs. Publication under this section MUST remain unavailable until an implementing Switch revision is adopted with the required verification support. Ordinary zero-version `record.put`, a preflight absence read, a signed current value or a host confirmation cannot substitute. The separate protected-record holds of Section 6 remain unchanged. The publication record, prepared-message facility and runtime cases below also require Paykit implementation; this specification claims none of them already conformant. `paykit-lib` implements the pair's format and ordering (wire 4.13), not the check of its generation against the publisher's verified identity document.

**One counter per recipient.** A publisher reserves each new list version through one ordinary non-delete `record.put` at its own service, using the fixed `private_list_publications` record for its App ID, recipient identity and actual verified identity generation (wire 5.8). The first reservation writes counter 1; each continuation writes the current counter plus 1 with checked arithmetic. Reservations consume numbers even when their messages never go. An unchanged retry keeps the original pair and exact signed bytes. A new UUID, changed expiry, renewal, changed list or newly constructed withdrawal is a new reservation. Other recipients' publications never affect this counter; no shared app sequence or service time is disclosed as its value. Exhaustion holds; it never wraps or advances the identity generation locally.

The original is a standalone protected origin with `origin.child: null`, strict apply-time absence, zero ordinary expected version and policy-bound birth generation equal to both the signed operation and its actual first application generation. Later writes repeat its complete policy and require both its exact current ordinary version and its exact current lineage/application condition. The app verifies current account/history, identity/generation, key, policy, writer, original binding and application evidence. The publisher MUST sign its original and every new counter write with its own App ID. To read current counter authority, it verifies the current counter operation's signature by that App ID, with certificate standing at the operation's authenticated original application time, together with the complete clean protected-origin, key, policy, birth, binding, current-lineage and application evidence and every retained high-water or hold. The slot proves original admission and continuity, not an independently retained creator signature. The reader verifies every node the generic bundle requires; it does not require signatures of a superseded original or intermediate operation that the generic contract permits pruning. Such permitted pruning is not a missing required node. Current wrong-writer evidence, any retained contradictory writer or continuity evidence, and every existing missing-history or uncertainty condition still hold publication; a later own-signed value alone clears none of them. Another own app holding the scope can deny access by occupying or changing the key; its write never becomes this publisher's authority. The generic policy does not restrict writes exclusively to one App ID.

A current delete, retired/unknown slot, missing value at an allocated slot, unreadable key/value, wrong writer or binding, recovered/truncated/non-clean continuity, divergent candidates, inconsistent evidence, a value below a retained high-water or an unknown same-generation outcome holds publication, including withdrawal. It never supplies counter zero. Paykit performs no same-generation counter repair from a backup or a purported complete predecessor. It never deletes these records to reclaim their counter. Missing authority can therefore hold this namespace for the remainder of its generation.

**Host decisions and prepared bytes.** Every known restore, verified history boundary, host withdrawal or other stop first durably invalidates the affected nonempty host authorization and records withdrawal owed. The same effective barrier suppresses earlier private-list deliveries through outbox, kept-batch materialization, delayed jobs, offer-history reconstruction and explicit resend. A failed barrier commit holds delivery; receipts, proofs and other kinds keep their own rules. One publisher serializes local decisions and sends. Devices sharing its App ID must synchronize this local state as Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.1 requires; CAS does not synchronize arbitrary host choices.

An explicit host decision to publish nonempty endpoints creates a fresh random per-recipient `grant_id`, durably bound to the exact permitted endpoint set and current verified generation. It establishes its base from fresh acceptable current counter authority or the successful original path. A reservation under that grant may extend only that exact base or a current value already bearing the same grant. Another grant or a null-grant withdrawal superseding the base makes it ineligible: a conflict never silently rebases old nonempty intent over a stop. Changed endpoint authorization requires a new host decision and grant. After restore the host re-applies its withdrawals before re-enabling a list.

For each publication the app reads and validates current own identity and counter authority. A cached verified predecessor may prepare an ordinary CAS, but successful application to that exact predecessor is indispensable; a backup's counter is never allocation authority. A complete authenticated conflict view can supply the current read. The early-recovery update below requires a fresh view, not a cached one.

Before any service submission, the app durably commits one publication intent: recipient, pair, grant or withdrawal cause, local intent revision, exact predecessor/origin evidence, endpoint reservations, unique operation/batch identity, exact encrypted operation, and one complete immutable unsigned message with its hash. The message fixes UUID v4, actual `created_at`, sender, recipient, kind, refs, body and expiry. Its writer uses a fixed compact JSON representation and retains those exact bytes; retry neither reconstructs nor reserializes them. Local room is reserved first under the existing message, pending-batch and local-write bounds; refusal makes no service call and leaves the duty owed.

**Reserve, then sign and queue.** Submit the prepared non-delete record put under the normal current-generation check. Only its verified exact applied outcome, or a fresh verified clean current value matching that exact reservation and its application evidence, advances the intent to reserved. A no-op, bare counter equality, unsigned answer or local assumption does not. Commit that receipt/reference durably. A reservation without matching durable prepared bytes is burned, never repurposed.

Before signing and again before enabling delivery, check current generation, certificate/scopes, stop status, host intent and restore/supersession barrier. Superseded reservations stay consumed and their messages stay suppressed. Otherwise sign only the prepared bytes. The guarded App facility verifies sender/app binding, complete body, stored hash and current authority; accepting arbitrary caller bytes cannot bypass policy. Core's exact-JSON signing and deterministic Ed25519 suffice: an uncommitted signature computation may be recomputed only to those same bytes; every persisted signature is reused. Persist the signed plaintext, reservation receipt, intent revision and obligation to queue together before the signature can leave the process.

Queue materialization and its durable consumed-intent marker are one idempotent local commit, or a durable recovery job with equivalent behavior. A crash before materialization retries the same plaintext; a crash afterward cannot prepare another message at that pair. The present `App::sign_message`, isolated `queue_signed` transaction and pre-signed `OpBatch::queue` are not this combined contract. No generic deliverable outbox accepts the message before verified reservation. Two instances completing the same prepared intent preserve its exact representation; two different preparations cannot use one winning reservation. No remote write is required merely to mark local queueing.

| Interruption or outcome | Required disposition |
|---|---|
| Before durable intent | No service write/message; retain the host decision or owed duty. |
| After intent, before submission | Retry the same eligible preparation, or cancel locally without sending. |
| The write may have applied, reply missing | Keep the exact batch and unknown status; resolve exact application/current evidence. Do not sign, reset, decrement or infer non-application from absence. |
| Definite CAS conflict | Validate current authority, discard this preparation and re-evaluate intent. An owed withdrawal may reserve anew; an ineligible old grant may not. |
| Current counter exceeds a pending reservation | Suppress its unsent preparation without clearing unknown-write accounting. |
| Crash after reservation, signature or queue persistence | Recheck eligibility and resume only its exact prepared/signed bytes, with the original replay deadline. |
| Delivery expires or is given up | Never requeue the given-up message. Retain any owed work. If delivery is unknown, reconcile it under Deliveries of unknown outcome. A separately authorized new publication reserves a new pair. |
| Generation changes | Stop old materialization/delivery, retain uncertainty and endpoint reservations, owe withdrawal and use the new verified generation after its required registration. |

Local per-recipient intent revisions order host decisions and withdrawal duties. An eligible fresh withdrawal is attempted immediately and stays owed until acceptance, permanent refusal or a later explicit host decision after restored changes have been re-applied. Persist that disposition: timeout, enqueue success and a lost reply do not clear the duty. A later host grant suppresses automatic withdrawal retries from the superseded revision; failed/superseded grants never revive older ones. Restore creates a fresh duty even when the backup marks its carried withdrawal queued or accepted.

**Restore and generation boundaries.** Clean and uncertain restores both suppress all carried Private Payment Lists, empty and nonempty, before any delivery path is released. Required current-identity, certificate and restore first-read guards still run. For every recipient known from backup, retained local state, carried list jobs or authenticated current publisher records, the app owes withdrawal. At the same generation it reserves the next current protected counter, overtaking every publication already reserved for that recipient. Repeating the same restore repeats that CAS. A recipient discovered later by bounded listing or an authenticated eligible request receives the same treatment while restored-withdrawal mode applies. Invalid, unrelated, unauthenticated or otherwise inadmissible requests allocate no record. A wholly lost recipient mapping cannot be proactively notified; an empty inventory proves no all-recipient completion.

A cooperative move, in-place key change or authorized provider-loss recovery permits a new namespace only when it adopts a genuinely greater signed identity generation. Clear inherited nonempty grants and owe withdrawals at that adoption boundary. `(g+1,1)` exceeds every legal `(g,c)` without recovering old counters first. If another instance already created the current-generation namespace, continue its actual clean counter. Repeated restore within it cannot reset its slot. A newer document version without greater generation gives no reset.

Under the client contract of Switch revision `f0bbddd1`, the durable `HistoryBoundaryObserved { generation, recovery_from, identity_hash }` notification is recorded atomically with verified boundary adoption and delivered before post-boundary protocol actions, before the later `provider_lost` callback and without waiting for Upload/Lists. Paykit durably records suppression and owed work before acknowledging it. Failure keeps notification/action release pending; restart repeats it. This notification neither completes recovery nor provides counter authority.

After verified actual current-generation registration, the generic narrow path permits exactly one signed ordinary non-delete `record.put`, with no explicit or implicitly attached cursor and no other operation, before strictly older-generation uploads, listing and retained batches finish. The original must have current birth generation and null child. A later update needs fresh authenticated clean, non-retired current-birth origin evidence, its unchanged policy, ordinary expected version and exact-current lineage condition. At most one such new batch is unresolved per instance. Same-generation or unclassified pending mutations, unknown registration and same-key uncertainty keep their normal gates. This path changes no recovery/completeness bit, cursor, old batch, reservation or signer-removal duty, and releases no payment. Earlier-generation absence uncertainty is excluded only under the generic birth-policy proof; evidence already retained at the key is held until classified. An uploaded claimed birth-g original actually first applied before g is invalid and cannot allocate restrictive state or reset that namespace.

An authenticated reserved empty-list intent carries a durable message-specific decision to omit its own conversation-entry mutation. All direct/queued/reconstructed/resend paths honor that decision and the current suppression barrier. A kind-wide exemption is insufficient.
