Recipient identity delivery, routes, directory/keys, sealing, certificates/scopes, recipient admission/deny, quotas and retry limits remain; no invite is added automatically. Its own blocked/unknown deny copy does not hold this valid stopping message, but remains unknown and continues holding nonempty lists and ordinary requests. No contact, list completeness, identity-equivocation check or global recovery state is fabricated. Pending removals of other apps and a signer publication-resume clearance are not prerequisites. Actual revocation, missing required keys/registration, service failure, local durability, quota/rate refusal, same-generation authority loss and recipient admission can still hold delivery, with withdrawal owed and reported.

**Bounds and privacy.** Fixed derivation and padding are wire 5.8. The counter exposes no other recipient's publication gaps, but the service observes opaque key, writer, policy/generation, epoch and timing; an active operator can correlate traffic, and authorized own apps with naming/read keys can identify recipients. The encrypted current value replaces no bounded offer evidence, endpoint reservation, payer journal or consumed mark. Requests' held versions remain hints, not allocation authority. Proof lookup, asked-version/daily-answer state and every consumed key retain the full pair and recipient binding (wire 4.10, 4.14, 5.3 and 5.4).

Each recipient/generation consumes one non-recycling origin slot, even for a withdrawal. The generic fixed 100,000-slot account-history budget reserves 577 logical bytes per slot (57,700,000 bytes, excluding indexes/overhead) and is shared with every protected-origin user. A standalone slot with birth generation encodes at most 289 payload bytes but still reserves 577. Ordinary default 100,000 live keys and 64 MiB record ciphertext also apply; these values each cost 528 ciphertext bytes, so 100,000 cost 52,800,000 bytes before other records/evidence. Deletion frees no origin slot. At the cap a clean existing-key update needs no new slot, but a new recipient/generation cannot initialize, even for urgent withdrawal. Growth-free updates are not refused for ciphertext growth; all other limits remain. Clean transfer preserves slots/count. Each generic origin export part has a 2,048-byte bound (1,645 bytes for its maximal canonical form), with at most 100,000 such parts; lineage parts and the complete manifest also count against the receiving service's total import limit, 4 GiB by default. Export/import can refuse a maximal account without dropping slots.

Keep intent, recipient inventory, dirty/suppression state and offer history within accounted durable local bounds. A full queue reports refusal and retains the suppression/duty; it cannot erase an uncertain endpoint reservation. Counter writes, including originals, remain ordinary operations under Switch wire 9.4: normally one `POST /ops` batch, default 3,000 batches per app per minute, outside the 60/minute repeatable-control budget. Ordinary operation permits at most eight distinct record writes in one batch; early recovery permits one. Failed fresh CAS attempts can consume ordinary rate capacity; exact retained-batch retries do not reserve again. Recipient appends remain subject to the default 120 per minute per sender identity and per external sender app at each recipient account, and notifying appends to the separate default 20 per minute budget. There is no new issuance-time guarantee or reserved urgent capacity.

### 8.5 Two payer apps on one identity

Both receive the request and are notified. The other app learns about the payment through the feed, from the request's claim, whether a wallet or an Allowance executor paid it, and shows:

- "Being handled by your other wallet" after `attempt`;
- "Paid" only after SETTLED.

Once the claim's entries are stubs in the feed (Switch wire 7.5), the other app learns SETTLED by reading the claim again (Switch wire 7.4.8), or from the payer's activity record, which carries the proof.

## 9. Delivery and re-sends

Paykit's messages are delivered as the Switch delivers every message (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11). Paykit adds a delivery state:

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Paid | For the payer: the obligation is SETTLED. For the payee: it has verified the proof |

- **Sender retention:** the sender keeps the signed plaintext and `accepted_at` of each message it sent for at least 30 days from its first acceptance, to answer `resend_request`; it may keep them longer, but answers a `resend_request` only with messages first accepted in the last 30 days, the time recipients keep processed IDs, so every copy it re-sends is still deduplicated. A re-send doesn't restart this: the sender keeps one entry per `msg_id`, with its first acceptance time and the later ones, and a request naming any of those times selects it. A re-sent message is sealed to the current receive keys and keeps its `msg_id` and signed plaintext.
- **Deduplication of re-sent requests:**
  - A re-sent request, even after that window, has the same obligation ID, so its claim shows whether it was already handled; once a claim without a deadline is gone, 400 days after its last entry, the payer's record of the payment does (Sections 6 and 7.2).
- **Recovery request:** `resend_request` asks the counterparty to re-send messages; answering is best effort. Sending an ask and re-sending its answer both require `paykit_contacts` and a known deny copy that permits the counterparty (Section 3.1, Outbound authorization); receiving an ask needs only a Paykit scope. The counterparty re-sends only kinds that the requesting app's certificate allows it to receive (Section 3.3), and a payer's replies only to an app that would take them under their label (Section 3.4). It limits what it re-sends: one message to the same requester identity at most once an hour, and at most a number of messages per requester identity a day, which it chooses (the prototype: 500). It leaves the rest out, and the requester may ask again later, subject to the unknown-delivery rule above.
- **Messages dropped for a bound:** every bound on the received messages an app keeps drops a message as one never processed and records a bounded recovery obligation for the drop. This guarantees the required pair and mark changes, not delivery of an ask or recovery of the payload. The families so bounded are the replies, period details and Allowance answers kept before their record, and the stops kept for a record the app doesn't hold (Section 8.1, Replies before their record and Messages that cross; Section 8.3, Ends reach the executor), with the extension messages not yet taken (Section 3.3) and messages the client drops while waiting for protocol admission (Section 8.1, Waiting for admission), which count in those same dropped-reply marks; the messages kept over a divergent record (Section 8.1, Stored entries); the entries dropped unverified, and those the client drops while they wait for a scope key (Section 8.1, Stored entries); and, dropped for the user's block rather than a bound, the messages whose marks `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1 keeps, which follow the same rule. For an admitted message, in the commit that drops it the app forgets its pair (Pubky Switch spec, Section 11), or, for the user's block, once the read is done, so a re-sent copy is taken, and counts it in its sender's mark of its family. A client admission-custody drop instead commits the client's durable notice with the custody removal, and the app acknowledges that notice only with its required dropped-reply mark and host-notice changes in one local transaction (Section 8.1, Waiting for admission); it does not forget an independently protected pair or effect. An incoming ordinary victim is counted without ever gaining a processed pair. Each family keeps one mark per sender identity, holding the earliest acceptance time among the messages it counts, which a re-send asks from, and the latest, which the mark expires by. The mark asks that sender to re-send what its inbox accepted from its earliest time (`resend_request` with `accepted_from`, and `accepted_until` at its latest time where its family's section says so, `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.12) as soon as sends to that sender aren't held (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties), whatever records the app holds, and again at most once in any 24 hours by its own clock while it lives; a mark of messages that waited for a scope key asks only once the app holds that key, since a copy sent before would wait for it again, and a mark of the user's block also waits for the conditions `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1 gives. A missing `paykit_contacts` scope leaves the ask unsent, reports `MissingScope` to the host and keeps its mark in the ordinary bounded pool, subject to the same expiry and eviction rule below. It is not a deny-held sender and gets no bound exemption. Until the scope is explicitly granted, no ask is signed or queued; once it is granted, the app first obtains a complete deny copy and asks only if that copy permits the sender. Missing authorization never creates another pending-mark family or holds feed intake. An ask still in the outbox awaits its delivery outcome. A restored mark or outbox entry cannot establish whether a prior ask for its window was given up after the backup. If that outcome is unknown, the app holds automatic repeat asks, including asks triggered by the restore, and reconciles with the host. If the client gives up the ask as possibly delivered, Paykit keeps that outcome under Deliveries of unknown outcome above. It counts as neither a delivered ask for retiring a mark nor a failed ask to replace automatically, including on the next scheduled retry. A request known to be undelivered permits another ask at most once in any 24 hours, and none is queued while one to that sender is still in the outbox; the 24 hours between delivered asks count from the last one the outbox delivered. For mark retirement, an unresolved terminal ask is an ask the app cannot send. Retiring the mark does not resolve the possibly delivered effect. A mark goes 30 days after its latest time; before that, it goes for a bound on marks only once a `resend_request` covering its latest time was delivered, except a block mark only a restore that isn't clean or a `missed` item made, which goes first, the host told (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties). A mark that waits only because its sender's sends are held is one per family and sender the deny list holds, and counts toward no bound on marks: the deny list's own limit bounds them (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3, Storage rules; Switch wire 9.1: `max_deny`, 100,000 by default). Past a bound on marks with none whose ask was delivered, the mark whose latest time is earliest among those whose ask was refused or can't be sent yet goes, the host told once (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties), so a bound never holds an entry. When a mark expires or is evicted before an ask covering its latest time was delivered, the app tells the host once per sender and window, with no message content (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties). This notice reports the retired recovery obligation; it neither retains nor reconstructs the dropped payload. Missing authorization, an unknown deny copy, a block, refused delivery or a full mark pool may leave an obligation undelivered until retirement. Marks do not extend the sender's retention, which starts at the message's first acceptance, and a delivered ask is not proof that a message returned. Recovery remains best effort under the sender's retention and re-send budgets, and a dropped payload may be lost permanently. No ordinary family admits a counted payload above its bound. A required earlier victim whose pair or effect remains protected is retained, and only the incoming admission waits under Section 8.1, Waiting for admission. A payload whose required mark cannot be committed is not dropped, and a real local storage failure leaves the incoming entry unprocessed rather than committing an over-bound pool or a cursor-only success. The same atomic intake rule applies to extensions (Section 3.3). Immediate stops and protected first receipts keep their stated obligations and counting rules. These requirements remain subject to the runtime prerequisites above. Where v0.1 refused a message past its bound and left it in its sender's outbox, pruning nothing but superseded lists, intake from other counterparties unaffected (`specs/paykit-sdk.md`, "StorageAdapter"), v0.2 records recovery for a bound-selected drop, and uses the client's bounded waiting custody or durable drop notice when a protected earlier victim prevents admission, without a capacity decision stopping feed intake.
- **What an app asks for:** the service writes the unopenable, unreadable and `missed` items that lead an app to ask for re-sends, and the app can't check them, so it bounds what it asks on their word. For an entry it can't open, a `missed` item and an unreadable item, it never asks for a time before the time of its cursor, less the clock tolerance; for an unreadable item, also only after a verified departure, for an entry accepted at or before it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2); and never for a time that doesn't parse. A `resend_request` adds no conversation entry (Pubky Switch spec, Section 7).

## 10. Encodings

Paykit's documents use the encodings of the Switch (Pubky Switch spec, Section 13). Paykit's own signature and derivations:

| Object | Signed by | Label | Fields |
|---|---|---|---|
| Executor grant | App | `paykit/v1/executor-grant` | identity, counterparty identity, `allowance_id`, terms hash, `u64(confirmed_generation)`, `u64(generation)` and `u64(seq)` of the Allowance record version its write builds on (0 for a new record) |

| Value | Derivation |
|---|---|
| Request hash | `BLAKE3(request message JSON bytes)` |
| Terms hash | `BLAKE3(lp(proposal message JSON bytes, acceptance message JSON bytes))` |
| Obligation ID, used as the claim key | Of a request: `derive("paykit/v1/obligation", "request", blind(payee identity), blind(request hash), u64(start of the Billing Period in Unix seconds, or 0 for a one-time request))`. Of a payment without a request: `derive("paykit/v1/obligation", "payment", attempt ID)`, without the payee, so a former holder of the naming key can't test guesses of the payee against it |
| Natural keys | `payer_activity` and `payee_activity`: `lp(payee identity, request hash)` for a request record, and `lp(payee identity, request hash, u64(period start))` for a period record; for a payment without a request, `lp(identity, attempt_id)` on the payer's side and `lp(Payment Endpoint Identifier, settlement ID)` on the payee's, the settlement ID being the bytes the payment method defines to name one settlement, the same in every proof of it, such as a Lightning payment hash or an on-chain transaction ID, in the one spelling `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 1.5 gives each proof type. `payment_lists`: `lp(payee identity, publishing app ID)`. `allowances` and `allowance_ledgers`: `lp(counterparty identity, allowance_id)` |

## 11. Threat model

The adversaries, what they can and can't do, and the protections are those of the Pubky Switch spec (Pubky Switch spec, Section 14), whose table states what each can do to payments. Paykit adds:

- **Rejections and cancellations aren't claim states, by choice.** A rejection or cancellation lives in the request record, so one a compromised own app or a lost write removes from the record before an app that never saw it reads the record leaves that app free to pay the request, within an Allowance's limits if it pays automatically. Apps that saw it keep it (Section 8.2; `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.3, Seen roles), and a payee's cancellation an app verified is a stop it keeps in its own state (Section 8.1, Messages that cross).
- **Limits of the switch's copy:** a payer finds the payee's switch through the Homeserver's document, possibly an old one. After a cooperative move a stale copy names the old switch, which, frozen, answers with its last document and the freeze naming the new one, so the payer follows it to the newer document (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 4.3 and 12.5); it does so for the identity's record age limit, after which no record from before the move is payable. This doesn't help after a provider loss, where the old switch is gone or hostile. One that is gone doesn't answer, so the payer pays only on the user's explicit confirmation, and never automatically. A hostile one can answer with its last document and no freeze, which the payer can't tell from a current answer, so the old document's own limits apply (the record age limit, Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2). Otherwise a switch that doesn't answer, or a malicious one, can only fail to help: it can't serve a newer document than the signer made, nor lower the one the payer uses. Someone flooding the read can't switch the lookup off silently either: reads are limited per network source, answers may be cached, and a payment the switch couldn't confirm needs the user (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3). Anchoring `identity.json` in PKARR would close the provider-loss case too, at the cost of PKARR space and of coupling every `identity.json` update to it; v0.2 doesn't anchor it (Pubky Switch spec, Section 19, decision 23).

## 12. Privacy

What each party learns is in the Pubky Switch spec (Pubky Switch spec, Section 15). Paykit adds:

| Party | Learns |
|---|---|
| A payee's switch, from payers | An anonymous lookup of the payee's `identity.json` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3) shortly before a payer without a fresh copy pays a public endpoint: the reader's network address and the time, so that someone is about to pay the identity, but not who. After a cooperative move the old service, frozen, sees the lookups of payers holding the old document for as long as it answers them (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.5). A cache in front of the service sees what the service would |
| A counterparty | The label of a request a labelled app sent (Section 3.1), which is why a label should be opaque |
| A labelled selling app, of the identity's other payee apps | From `settlements` (Section 3.1), whose every bucket write carries its writer's signed operation and certificate: how many settlements the identity has credited, when, and by which own app, whose certificate names its label; and, for a settlement it can name, such as an on-chain payment to a public endpoint, whether it was credited, and to a one-time request, a Billing Period or a payment without a request, the occurrence itself only as a tag. Never a counterparty, an amount, or a settlement it can't name |
| A selling app, a hosted or labelled one included | Through `paykit_contacts`, which every payee app holds (Section 3.1): the identity's contacts, conversation entries, deny list and the senders the service flags, so whom the identity knows and has blocked |

## 13. Guarantees

- **No duplicate payment for one obligation**, under these conditions:
  - *Holds for:* the same obligation, with compliant apps and a consistent switch: one that follows Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.5, so that every item it acknowledged is durable and it never serves a state behind what it signed.
  - *Does not cover:*
    - a user who confirms an outcome (`reconcile`, `unknown` or `failed`), or a manual payment marked "may already be paid";
    - a malicious or non-compliant switch, including one that shows different apps different histories after any shared prefix, or loses a write it acknowledged and serves on (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 6.5 and 10.1); on such a split a recurring obligation can be paid once per branch in every Billing Period;
    - a payer that carries out an authorized attempt more than once, for example on two devices of one app (rail-level idempotency is the wallet's responsibility);
    - a payment method that reports a final failure and then completes the payment anyway, or whose declared idempotency or history (Section 7.2) doesn't hold;
    - a payee that issues two obligations for one purpose, or misdates a re-sent request during provider loss;
    - an old provider that keeps authorizing attempts after a provider loss (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6, residual risk).
- **One credit per settlement:** a settlement is credited to one occurrence at most, by every own payee app, labelled ones included, and for every counterparty (Sections 6, Settlement marks, and 8.1 step 4), with compliant apps and a consistent switch. *Does not cover:* a faulty or compromised own app holding `paykit_settlements` that drops a mark from its bucket, which an app that never saw it can't tell from an absent one, or a bucket delete whose last holders are removed or lose their state before publishing restored marks or a protective boundary, after which the Switch may forget the delete, or that writes a mark no credit made, which stops that settlement's credit; and a credit the user confirms by hand, shown that it may already be credited, where the authenticated creation, incarnation and deletion checks permit that credit, such as one in a full, unreadable or divergent bucket or after a provider loss. A bucket deleted, or put back with `lost_to_delete`, keeps the guarantee only with the publication-time absence and exact-incarnation checks and authenticated reader provenance of wire 5.7, and while a current participating reader retains its restriction through applied shared publication; that reader cannot acknowledge the delete first, even under quota pressure (wire 5.7). If every holder of an unpublished restriction is removed or loses its state first, the Switch's existing forgetting rule can leave a later app with no discoverable restriction; that case remains outside the guarantee (Pubky Switch spec, Section 10.3; Switch wire 9.1). Applied publication can use a boundary-only bucket and needs no seen mark. The cost of the retained boundary is: a settlement in it that an app has seen no mark of is credited only when the closed evidence list clears every retained and pending deletion boundary (Section 6, Settlement marks), and never under an `unknown` boundary, never on its user's confirmation; any other, a payment to a reusable endpoint by a method that gives no such bound among them, is never credited: a denial, never a second credit. So a faulty or compromised own app holding `paykit_settlements` that deletes every bucket ends the credit of every earlier settlement this evidence does not clear. More than 128 distinct retained deletes in one bucket saturate its boundary to `unknown`, permanently withholding every new credit in that bucket, including later payments. No confirmation, rewrite, backup restore or provider-loss recovery removes that denial. A reader lacking proof of a complete deleted predecessor may retain a delete that another reader could prove fully restored; that conservative restriction also remains once shared. A known hash whose actual application or required order is unproved also withholds credit until adequate authenticated evidence is available; a correctly signed prepublished hash can therefore deny credit but cannot authorize it. No amount of user confirmation supplies that evidence. Missing creation or incarnation evidence holds every new credit, including a by-hand credit, until that evidence is authenticated; a rewrite, restore or recovery does not substitute for it, and the hold may be indefinite. The pinned Switch checks absence at application but lacks the required lineage evidence for later readers, so the required settlement behavior cannot be enabled before that support and its Paykit integration exist.
- **Allowance executors** are responsible for their Allowances' limits.
- **Confidentiality, forward secrecy, integrity, isolation, idempotent processing and availability** are the Switch's guarantees (Pubky Switch spec, Section 16), which Paykit inherits.

## 14. Performance targets

| Action | Target |
|---|---|
| Send a message other than a newly constructed Private Payment List | 1 round trip: the append to the recipient's inbox and the shared record write at the sender's own switch, in parallel; 0.3-0.7 s. A first message to a new counterparty adds one round for the directory read, made in parallel with delivering the sender's `identity.json` and writing its conversation entry |
| Reserve and publish a Private Payment List | Current own-record read when no acceptable view is held, then one ordinary CAS batch before signing/queueing, then existing recipient delivery calls; an authenticated conflict view may supply the read. Exact retries retain their reservation. No service-time call is added for ordering (Section 8.4.1) |
| Receive a message | Delivered on the open long-poll; the shared record batch makes it durable in 1 round trip |
| Recipient sees it | Under 1 s in the foreground; 1-5 s through push |
| Pay a request | Critical path before money moves: 1 claim write (~0.2-0.3 s), with the payer's own `identity.json` revalidated in parallel; paid from a single-use endpoint of a Private Payment List, this needs an already-fresh copy of the payee's `identity.json` (Section 7.2, Reserve a single-use endpoint). The acceptance, `done`, the record update and the proof follow in the background, plus wallet time |
| Save a contact | 1 operation: allowlist entry |
| Directory lookup | 1 request when a counterparty is new; afterwards cached while the recipient's `identity.json` names the same switch and generation, and refreshed in the background when an append shows a newer `directory_version` |
| Receive key rotation | 1 request, about once per active day per app by default, after a feed read; never on a user-facing step. A sender whose cached directory predates the rotation pays one extra round (`stale_key`), unless it refreshed the directory in the background (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.1) |
| Add or revoke an app | One operation each for the service, `identity.json` and key bundles, whatever the contact count. A revocation also has own apps deliver the new `identity.json`, in the background, to the switches they wrote to in the last 30 days (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.2) |
| New app catch-up | 1 list call per collection, then the feed |
| Idle | Foreground: one long-poll every ~25 s. Background: none (push) |

**Homeserver calls outside the critical path,** allowed by the design rule: revalidating the app's own `identity.json` in parallel with an `attempt` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3); the event stream subscription that watches its own and its counterparties' `identity.json`, held while the app runs in the foreground; and fetching a counterparty's `identity.json` in the background when an event names it, or when a copy it doesn't watch is more than 10 minutes old, to route messages and to check the apps it hears from and pays to.

**Paying a public endpoint:** the lookup of the payee's `identity.json` at its switch runs in parallel with the Homeserver record read that follows the Homeserver's `identity.json`, and the payer waits for it at most a short grace after that read, so it adds no request in sequence. If it hasn't answered when money is about to move, for example because a lost request is noticed only after the client's timeout, the payment never waits longer, and is handled as Section 7.2 step 1 (check the destination) says for a lookup that hasn't answered (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3). The end-to-end latency test checks this on every sample: either the lookup answered before money moved, or the payment went ahead only on its user's explicit confirmation and no automatic payment was made, and with and without the lookup the requests in sequence before money moves are the same.

Targets are measured by counting requests per action, as the prototype does (Section 18). Latency is judged on a stated network profile for the user's own actions: sending a request, receiving one, tapping Pay until money can move, a first contact and a cold start. Background processing, such as the payer's follow-ups after paying, the payee's handling of an acceptance and a proof, and idle checks, and delivery on the open long-poll are reported separately.

## 15. Removed from v0.1

- **Shared state:**
  - the shared-state blob;
  - Homeserver locks;
  - v0.1's refusal of a concurrent replacement of the Paykit Profile: in v0.2 the later write wins (Section 4);
  - per-peer and per-app leases;
  - the single shared-state encryption key; shared data uses scoped, epoch-numbered Shared Data Keys (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.5).
- **Session crypto:**
  - Noise links, handshakes, snapshots and recovery markers;
  - relinking.
- **Discovery records:**
  - the App Registry;
  - the Noise Key Authorization;
  - Public Contact Markers.
- **Message format and delivery:**
  - the 1000-byte limit;
  - mandatory Delivery Confirmations;
  - Receipt Location and Receipt Access;
  - polling each contact.
- **Payment proof profiles:** the ERC-20 Payment Proof profile (`specs/erc20-payment-proofs.md`) doesn't apply as written, since it binds `paymentRequestId` and `conversionQuoteId`, which v0.2 replaces (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.2). A profile for v0.2 is decided later (Section 16).

## 16. Left to apps or later

What the Switch leaves to apps or decides later is in the Pubky Switch spec (Pubky Switch spec, Section 17). Paykit adds:

- **Up to each app:**
  - offline endpoint issuing;
  - deferring an occurrence for later reconsideration, kept in the executor's own state: it grants no authority to pay and binds no other app, as in v0.1 (`specs/allowances.md`, "Payment Request integration").
- **Decided later:**
  - a v0.2 ERC-20 Payment Proof profile (Section 15).
  - Allowances that pay requests priced in another asset, for example a subscription priced in usd paid from a btc Allowance. For v0.2 an Allowance pays only requests in its own asset, as in v0.1 (Section 8.3); a payee that wants automatic payment in another asset bills in that asset at its own rate. A later term can widen this without breaking older executors, which keep refusing.
- **No migration from v0.1.** v0.2 replaces v0.1: v0.1 and v0.2 apps can't pay each other, so the payer and the payee both update to v0.2, and a v0.2 app reads and writes no v0.1 state. What was open under v0.1 is set up again under v0.2:
  - each identity's Pubky grants of v0.1 are revoked or narrowed before its first v0.2 `identity.json` (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.4);
  - public Payment Endpoints are published in each app's v0.2 record (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.2);
  - Allowances and recurring requests don't carry over: the payee requests and the payer accepts them again under v0.2, and an Allowance's limits count from its v0.2 acceptance;
  - receipts and the rest of the v0.1 payment history stay where the v0.1 app keeps them, and v0.2 doesn't read them;
  - sellers on paykit-server: each seller approves it as a v0.2 app with three scopes, `paykit_request` or its shop's labelled selling scope `paykit_request:<label>`, and `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`, which either one requires (Section 3.1), and it sends its open requests again.

## 17. Open questions for the team

Questions 1 to 12 are deliberate trade-offs; 1 to 6, 8 and 9 are the switch's, given in Pubky Switch spec, Section 19. Questions 1 to 12 are decided: 1 to 9, 11 and 12 as their defaults, which this spec describes, with the metadata of 3 and 6 disclosed to users and push optional for each app, and question 10 is closed: shared contact details aren't part of Paykit. The team's decision list explains each one. The switch also has four open questions, given in Pubky Switch spec, Section 19: 25, an authenticated sign of erasure, 26, bounding `renewed` in `identity.json`, 29, uploads from a dishonest app after a provider loss, and 32, two identities against a third at the total bound on entries an app stores while they wait; until they are decided, their defaults apply, for 25 the 24-hour rule of Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.7. Paykit has five open questions too, 27, 28, 30, 36 and 37, below, and four decided since, 31, 33, 34 and 35, after them; the switch and Paykit share one set of numbers, which identify questions and don't order them, and 29 and 32 are the switch's; until they are decided, the rules this spec describes apply. Allowances across assets are deferred (Section 16).

7. **Limits.** 64 KiB messages, 16 KiB for the kinds activity records store, 256 KiB shared records, about 1,500 live ledger entries per Allowance (a 256 KiB ledger record), 32 apps per identity by default. Decided: kept.
10. **Shared contact details.** Closed: not part of Paykit. Contacts in Paykit are only the switch's allowlist and deny list (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). Names, pictures and notes that all of a person's apps share belong in an address book for every Pubky app, not in Paykit's records.
11. **Who may end an Allowance.** Any own app with `paykit_allowance` may end one, and an `executor_notice` makes sure the executor stops even if it was offline and the Allowance record was rewritten afterwards (Section 8.3). This lets the user cancel from any device, including when the executor's device is lost or off. The alternative is that only the executor may end it, which removes the notice but means cancelling elsewhere requires revoking the executor or handing it off first. Decided: any own app with `paykit_allowance` may end it.
12. **Paying while the Homeserver is unreachable.** Before each attempt, an app revalidates its own `identity.json` unless it did so in the last 60 s (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3), so payments stop while the identity's Homeserver can't be reached. This keeps an app from paying through a switch the identity has moved away from, which matters most right after a provider change (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.6). The alternative is a grace period on a cached copy, which keeps payments working through a Homeserver outage but widens that window. Decided: payments stop.

**Open questions.** Five questions are open. Each has a default, the rule this spec describes.

27. **Two notes on apps that hold the lists.** (Spam protection for payee apps is settled by Section 3.1: `paykit_request` and every labelled selling scope require `paykit_contacts`, so the spam rule binds every app that receives, as in v0.1, at the cost that a selling app, a hosted one included, reads the identity's contacts, conversation entries, deny list and the senders the service flags, and takes no new activity while it can't read them.) A dedicated outcome for a message deferred until the lists are known would be cleaner than the client's store-retry hold; it is a later client addition, and until then the store-retry hold (`Outcome::Retry`) stands in for it, so a host sees `StoreFailing` for this cause too. And an app whose lists are too large to fit one commit of its store can't refresh its copy, so it defers all new activity until they fit: at the default limits they fit, so this residual comes with raised limits. Open: until it is decided, the rule of `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 4.1 applies as written.

28. **Lifting the hold after a restore automatically.** After a restore from a backup that isn't clean, and after a `missed` item (Section 7.2, What a `missed` item loses), an app holds every open request and Allowance until the record shows a cancellation or end or the user confirms (Section 7.2, What a restore loses), since the re-sends a `resend_request` brings can't show that nothing more is coming. Lifting the hold without the user would need a `paykit.resend_answer` from each app that answers, saying that its selection was complete, with nothing left out by its limits or its retention; a MUST to answer; and a way for the requester to know which of the counterparty's apps must answer, since each app keeps only the messages it sent. Even then, a sender re-sends only messages first accepted in the last 30 days (Section 9), so a gap older than that stays uncovered. Skipping the hold after a clean restore is decided: the Switch reports the cursor it holds for an app with every feed page, a held cursor ahead of the backup's position shows lost progress, and a restore the report shows clean resumes without a hold (Section 7.2, What a restore loses; Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4). The question also covers a request first seen after the restore, which may be a re-send of one the backup missed. Candidates for ending its hold: a mark by its sender that it is a re-send, which a sender can't be relied on to set; the end of the 30-day re-send window after the restore (Section 9); or the user's confirmation of each request. Open: until it is decided, the hold follows every restore that isn't clean, covers requests first seen after it, and ends only by the record or the user's confirmation of each request. A `missed` item gives the last acceptance time of the entries it missed, so its hold covers a request first seen after it only when the request's signed `created_at` is earlier than that time plus the clock tolerance (Section 7.2, What a `missed` item loses).

30. **Growth of `used` in a `payment_lists` record.** A payer's `payment_lists` record keeps every `used` entry it has held, and readers ignore a write that drops one (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.4), so the record only grows, within the 256 KiB limit for a shared record value (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2), and paying one payee app's single-use endpoints for long enough fills it. A pruning rule would let an entry go once no own app may still pay from the list version it belongs to, for example once that version has expired, at its `expires_at` or 30 days after the inbox accepted it (Section 8.4); the question also covers what a payer does when the record is full before then. Open: until it is decided, entries are never dropped, and when the record can't take another entry, the `attempt` batch that carries the mark is refused whole (Section 7.2 step 1, Reserve a single-use endpoint), so no single-use payment happens unmarked: the payment fails closed, and the payee then publishes its lists from a new app, whose record starts empty.

36. **Writing over a refused write.** Section 6 governs a different signed write at the same held activity version. For other refused writes, when the switch shows as a record's current write one an own app refused, the reference client has the app build its next write on that write's version, from the content the app trusts, as it does for a write it doesn't take only for a missing date or for the certificate the switch shows with it; a write it ignores for another reason, such as a signature that doesn't verify or its own write, isn't built on, and the app builds on the version it holds (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). An app refuses a write for passing reasons too, such as a writer's certificate it can't place yet, not only for a faulty writer. Any write it makes there, a stop, a counterparty's message it verified or the record of a payment made included, drops what the refused write holds, and the app that wrote that ignores the new write, since it drops a message it saw. So a third own app that never saw the refused write can act on less than the record held. On the payee side: own app B credits a settlement to request R and writes R with the proof and its receipt; own app A refuses that write; a proof of the same settlement arrives naming R or another request R2, and A writes it over the refused write from its trusted content, so R loses the receipt; own app C, which never saw B's write, finds the settlement's mark in its bucket and credits nothing, so R only loses its receipt. On the payer side: the payee's period details, or a payment's record, placed over a refused write that holds another own app's rejection or a single-use endpoint's `used` mark, let a third own app pay a rejected request or pay that endpoint again. Candidate rules: (i) over a write it refused an app writes nothing: it keeps a stop as its own stop, keeps a counterparty's message it verified as a stored message, writes a payment's outcome on the claim alone, and makes no payment, acceptance or credit decision there, until a later write it accepts replaces the refused one or its user confirms after being shown it; or (ii) its write carries over every stored message of the refused write that verifies on its own, and every `used` entry and `manual_only` mark the refused write holds. Open: until it is decided, this exposure is an accepted risk.
37. **A reading app's check of a newer ledger version.** The reference client's check of a later write (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2) isn't handed the write's service time, which the ledger rules need (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.5): until it is, only the executor checks a newer version, before it builds on it.

**Decided since.** Four questions that were open are decided, as below.

31. **Crediting one settlement twice at once.** Decided: a credit adds the settlement's mark to its bucket in `settlements`, chosen by the settlement alone, in the batch that stores its receipt, writing the bucket at the version it read, so of two own payee apps that credit one settlement at once, to two occurrences of one counterparty or of two, only one batch applies, and the other app's record keeps the proof without a receipt (Sections 6, Settlement marks, and 8.1 step 4). Every payee app holds `paykit_settlements`, which `paykit_request` and every labelled selling scope require (Section 3.1), so a labelled app's credit and another label's or an unlabelled app's exclude each other too, while a labelled app learns of the other credits only what Section 12 says.
33. **A deleted `allowances` record.** Decided: an own app that holds a record whose content withholds or bounds a payment, an `allowances` record among them, and reads a delete of it never takes the delete as an end of what the record held: it writes the record back at once, over the delete, never as a state more permissive than the one deleted, so an Allowance whose deleted state the app can't show is put back ended, and a replayed or new proposal under its `allowance_id` is refused as conflicted (Section 6, Putting back a deleted record; Section 8.3, Ending). Every listed app must acknowledge the delete before the service may forget the value, and an app required to put it back withholds that acknowledgment until publication.

34. **The ledger version a new executor starts from.** Closed: Section 8.3 (Last ledger version and Handoff) says which ledger writes a new executor may build on.

35. **A proof shown again over a deleted record.** Decided: the settlement's mark outlives the record that credited it, no app deletes it, and every new credit must pass the current bucket read, authenticated incarnation, application-order and deletion-boundary rules (Section 8.1 step 4; wire 5.7), subject to Section 13's retention and fault limits. A proof shown for another occurrence does not bypass those rules. A later app reads an applied protective bucket; removal or loss of every unpublished witness before the service forgets a deletion remains outside the guarantee. The cost is one mark per credited settlement for the identity's life, 28 bytes in one of at most 1,024 bucket records, beside the record that credited it, which the payee app still keeps (Section 6, Settlement marks and Deleting finished activity). The fault and all-witnesses-lost limits remain those of Section 13. A delete with discoverable restrictions withholds credit until the required protection and authenticated application-order evidence permit it; a hash's first appearance alone never does.

**Further team decisions.** These aren't protocol fixes, and they are left to the team. They are numbered as in the team's decision list, after its closed decisions 13 and 14; that list gives each in full. All are decided or closed. Decisions 15, 23 and 24 are the switch's, given in Pubky Switch spec, Section 19. Two of the decisions add protocol: the labelled selling scopes of 22 (Sections 3.1, 3.3, 3.4, 3.5, 6 and 8.1) and the hand-over of 24 (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.8).

16. **Personal data: GDPR, retention and erasure.** Closed: the spec gives account erasure (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.7). The signer erases the identity's account at a service, which deletes everything it holds for the identity, so what Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3 keeps for good is kept for the account's life, and a user can delete their account. The rest, such as a privacy policy, records of processing and a data protection impact assessment, is each operator's own compliance work, not a protocol decision.
17. **Background payment on iOS.** Closed: whether an app pays while it is closed is each app's choice, within the key rules of Pubky Switch spec, Section 9, which describes the default in which the push extension only shows messages.
18. **Per-device or seed-derived app keys** (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.1). Random keys per device keep devices apart, and every restore needs an approval; seed-derived keys restore without one, but make the devices one app, unprotected from each other. Decided: seed-derived keys. Keeping the devices of one app from carrying out one attempt twice is the wallet's job (Section 13).
19. **The signer at launch** (Pubky Switch spec, Section 18). Decided: the wallet app is the signer for now; whether Ring becomes one too is decided later. One Rust signer core serves both of the wallet app's platforms, and Ring if it does.
20. **The SDK timeline.** Decided: the v0.2 SDK's surface is drafted and the host app's calls mapped onto it before the freeze, then a phased plan with owners, dates and the kill criteria of Section 18.
21. **The name of the service.** Decided: it is the Pubky Switch, a name that isn't Paykit-specific, since the switch isn't payment-specific. Its mailbox part, where senders' messages land and apps read them, keeps the name "inbox", as in `POST /v1/{id}/inbox` and "send to their inbox". Pubky already has the `pubky/inbox` ping service and the relay inbox of Pubky auth. For now the switch doesn't merge with `pubky/inbox`, which sends wake-up pings and leaves message bodies on the sender's Homeserver.
22. **How finely history is shared.** A scope's keys open all of that scope's records, so every app holding a scope sees its whole history: a hosted app holding `paykit_request`, such as paykit-server, reads all of the identity's payee history, including requests the person's own wallets sent (Pubky Switch spec, Section 8.2). Should history be shared by scope, or more finely, for example per app or per label? Options: keep it by scope; finer sharing, such as a labelled request scope with its own keys, so that a hosted app reads only what is written under its label. Decided: labelled selling scopes, so that a hosted app holding a label reads only that label's history, every label's settlement marks and the identity's lists (Section 12), and the identity's own wallets, holding `paykit_request`, still read all of it. They change the scope and kind tables (Sections 3.1 and 3.3), and Sections 3.1, 3.3, 3.4, 3.5, 6 and 8.1 specify them: payers see the label, plain `paykit_request` covers every label, and a certificate holds at most 8 labels.

## 18. Prototype and process

The reference prototype is shared separately, with its history (phase 1, the mechanisms the spec has since dropped: tickets, acks, leases and the claim lease; and the full lists of what phase 2 built and tested) and its full measurements.

**What the prototype covers**

- The reference prototype implements this spec, but the rules listed below under "Where the reference prototype differs", on a local Pubky testnet: the switch with its feed and chain, shared records, conditions, batches, conversation entries, app and receive key registration, key bundles and `identity.json` delivery; apps with shared activity records, and Allowances and recurring requests built only on claims and shared records; moving to another provider and provider loss; an in-process push relay; and a full payment over HTTP against a local Pubky testnet Homeserver.
- The same reference prototype supplies the Switch, signer and app behavior exercised by its recorded results; those results do not establish the additional contracts adopted at this specification's Switch baseline.
- `paykit-lib`, the public Paykit Library on this repository's `v0.2` branch, implements this spec's formats, with `pubky-switch-core` for the Switch's rules, with the exceptions and limits the Status paragraph at the top lists. Its public vector tests reproduce the stored values in `specs/v0.2/vectors/`, as that branch holds them, from that set's seeds (`paykit-lib/tests/vectors/` on that branch; the vectors `README.md`, How the files were generated); the Switch's own set is checked by `pubky-switch-core`'s tests at the dependency used by that branch, without establishing all contracts at this specification's newer baseline. Anyone can run `paykit-lib`'s in a checkout of the `v0.2` branch: `cargo test -p paykit-lib --test vectors` rebuilds and compares the stored expectations.
- Its tests cover every fault test, receive key test and conformance test of its history, and the shared test vectors but the cases the vectors READMEs list as generated by `paykit-lib` and `pubky-switch-core`: those in `specs/v0.2/vectors/` and [the Switch's](https://github.com/pubky/pubky-switch/tree/5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61/spec/vectors).
- An independent implementation, shared separately, written from the wire documents and the test vectors without the prototype's code, hasn't run this revision's vectors: Paykit's set, which `paykit-lib`'s public vector tests generate and check, nor the Switch's at this spec's pin. A Switch crate's vector checks cover its corresponding source revision. It uses the same Rust crypto crates and lockfile as the prototype, so it doesn't check those crates.
- A message protection study, shared separately, compared sealed messages, Noise IK, a Double Ratchet and MLS, and led to rotation after reads (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.6).

**Where the reference prototype differs.** It predates these rules, which this revision adds, so it doesn't enforce them, or can't be shown to; `paykit-lib` enforces each, and the vectors named check it, but where a rule below says its checks and vectors are to come:

- the adopted Switch ownership, callback, admission, duty/event, backup, storage, delivery, lifetime and wire contracts described under What this pin adopts govern the SDK dependency update. Required integration cases include an own certificate containing Paykit and another protocol through public join, load and restore, owner-isolated local state and duties, shared deny barriers, failed persistence and unchanged current authority. An unknown recovery ask must remain possibly delivered through restart and restore, without requeueing its signed bytes or automatically issuing another ask. A restored store lacking a give-up mark must not reconstruct a message from a shared record or flush a stale backed-up copy without current evidence that the original delivery remains pending. Existing peer-certificate vectors do not exercise these own-app lifecycle paths. The corresponding Paykit SDK tests must accompany the implementing code; this adoption adds no runtime or vector coverage;
- Allowance proposals carry `allower_generation` (wire 4.15): `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` parses it as a positive integer, and the stored proposal vectors carry it; the Paykit SDK on `v0.2` takes it, when proposing, from the Allower's identity document; its checks against that document belong to the acceptance mechanism (Section 8.3.1, specified with its implementation);
- Allowance acceptance is unavailable in this revision, and no app writes an acceptance that would newly fill an Allowance record's `response` (Section 8.3.1); the prototype accepts Allowances and pays under them, as its recorded results below include, and the Paykit SDK on `v0.2` refuses acceptance and holds every delivery of one: a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- Private Payment Lists carry the required positive generation/counter pair, compared generation first, then counter, across the list, request hint, proof, consumed endpoint and unrequested destination (wire 4.10, 4.13, 4.14, 5.3 and 5.4; vectors: `messages.json` `private_payment_list`, `private_payment_list_request` and `payment_proof_unrequested`, `sealed.json` `binding_accepted` and `activity_records`; `messages.json` `list_versions` refuses a version with no counter, a zero counter and an integer); a later `payment_lists` write holding another list of the same pair never replaces the held one, and its used entries are kept (wire 4.13, 5.4). When paying, the payer checks a list's generation against the payee's verified `identity.json` before any reservation (wire 4.13, Section 7.2);
- a publisher uses the fixed per-recipient/generation protected-origin record and ordinary record CAS, with current original/lineage/application authority, durable host grants and immutable prepared messages, reservation before signing, idempotent queue materialization, unknown-outcome retention and selective restore/supersession barriers (Section 8.4.1; wire 5.8). The origin and lineage contract these requirements use belongs to Switch revision `f0bbddd1` and is absent from the current pin; none of its positive paths may be enabled before an implementing revision is adopted with Paykit integration. Generated encoding/rejection cases come with the implementing library; crash, retry, authority, queue and scope behavior requires the runtime cases below;
- actual verified generation adoption durably notifies Paykit before post-boundary actions and before recovery uploads/lists finish, and a verified registered app may reserve through exactly one eligible current-birth ordinary record put while strictly older recovery work remains held; an eligible empty withdrawal skips only its own conversation mutation and remains subject to recipient admission and all actual authority checks (Section 8.4.1). Same-generation uncertainty, origin-cap exhaustion, local durability refusal or missing authority holds with the duty retained; no signer publication clearance or 60/minute control allocation is added. The client/SDK support and runtime cases remain to be implemented;
- an extension prefix holds a `-` (wire 3.1, 4.3, A14): the prototype takes a prefix without one, but `paykit`;
- a certificate holding another protocol's scopes beside Paykit's (Section 3.2, wire 3.1 "Several protocols"): its `permissions` hold every permission Paykit's scopes need and may hold more, Paykit's rules and bounds count only Paykit's scopes, and those scopes alone authorize Paykit's kinds; the prototype's verifiers require `permissions` to be exactly Paykit's list (`documents.json` `multi_protocol_certificates`, `messages.json` `multi_protocol_senders`);
- a `resend_request` from a Paykit App holding `paykit_contacts`, with any Paykit App permitted to receive it (Section 3.3, wire 4.1, 4.12): the contacts sender check and its positive and negative cases are to come;
- the checks of wire 1.4 on the identifiers and payloads of an app record's `paykit` member (wire 3.2; `documents.json` `app_records`, the last 4 refused);
- an app republishes its record on the Homeserver at a renewal only once the service accepts it, and while a publication fails, or the Homeserver copy differs from the newest record the service accepted, it publishes that newest record again, never an older one and never over a Homeserver copy with a later `updated_at`, until it deletes that record to leave, or leaves (wire 3.2, Renewal): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a reader that holds the version a write was built on ignores and flags a write that drops a listed period without a `claim_step` condition on that period's claim among the conditions its operation signed (wire 5.3): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a cancellation goes to the other role than its sender's, on receipt, on sending and in a resend answer (Sections 3.3, 3.4; wire 4.1, 4.3, 4.12; `messages.json` `cancellation_roles`);
- with conversion terms, a request's asset and its rates' assets follow the conversion convention, and each rate matches an endpoint the request names (wire 4.4; `messages.json` `conversion_terms`);
- period details carry rates only for a request with `per_period` conversion terms, and their rates are cross-asset only, bare or rail-qualified, follow the same convention and match an endpoint the details or the request name (wire 4.9; `messages.json` `conversion_terms`);
- the amount to an endpoint is the request's amount times the asset-rail rate, else the asset-wide one, else 1 for an endpoint of the requested asset, rounded up once to the endpoint's smallest unit, and none for an endpoint of another asset that no rate prices (Section 7.2): `paykit-lib` computes it (`amount_for_endpoint`) and `messages.json` `conversion_amounts` checks it; that the payer pays that amount, and treats such an endpoint as unusable under conversion terms, is a rule of an app's run;
- a reader of a period record reads the period details it holds against the request record's request, ignores any invalid against it and pays by none (wire 4.9, 5.3): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a payer's read of a payee's public endpoints reports one app's failure as that app's and keeps the other apps' usable endpoints, within 256 endpoints and 4 MiB of payloads, and reports an app that doesn't fit as over the bound (Section 4): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a payment priced by a rate pays the amount its user confirmed, by the rates shown, and period details that arrive after the confirmation don't change it (Section 7.2): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a recurrence's `ends_at` is present, a time or `null` (wire 4.4);
- period limits are duplicates only when spelled the same, and carry both their limits (wire 4.15; `allowance_usage.json` `period_limit_spellings`, `period_limit_members`);
- a stored message passes the sender's side of check 8 when it is read (wire 5.2; `sealed.json` `activity_records`);
- an activity record carries `request_hash`, `billing_period`, `attempt_id`, `unrequested` and `allowance_id` only where its binding and side allow (wire 5.3; `sealed.json` `activity_records`);
- a record's `msg_ids` names each ID once, the 16 newest distinct others, and readers read it in that form (wire 5.3, 5.4; `sealed.json` `msg_ids_bounds`);
- a proof's members follow whether it proves a request: `amount` only without one, its Billing Period with the period details it used, and no request context without a request (wire 4.10; `messages.json` `proof_members` checks the first and the last, and no vector refuses a proof that breaks the second);
- a receipt is of its proof's attempt and Billing Period, and names no period without a request (wire 4.11; `sealed.json` `activity_records` checks a receipt of another attempt, and no vector refuses one of another Billing Period or one naming a period without a request);
- an Allowance end names the stored acceptance, or is the proposal's withdrawal by its sender before any response; a rejected Allowance has no end; `executor_app_id` only in an acceptance from the allower (wire 4.16; `sealed.json` `allowance_records`);
- an unrequested destination's `list_version` is present exactly for a list, and is the proof's (wire 5.3);
- a newer Allowance ledger version holds an attempt that left into its totals in neither its entries nor its `totals.failed` (wire 5.5);
- period details as late as those held but with another hash replace nothing (wire 4.9, 5.3), and a later write of an `allowances` record keeps the proposal, response and end seen (wire 5.4);
- a later write of a `payment_lists` record holds no older list, no other list of the same `version`, and every `used` entry seen, whatever its list's `version` (wire 4.13, 5.4);
- a second, different proposal or response under one `allowance_id` makes the Allowance conflicted: the Allowance record holds it in `conflicted`, a proposal from either side with valid terms and another hash than `proposal`, or, beside the record's response, an acceptance or rejection from the proposal's recipient that refers to the proposal, with another hash than `response`; a conflicted record has no current grant, and a later write never drops or replaces it (Section 8.3, wire 5.4; `sealed.json` `allowance_records` checks the record's form, and no vector has a later write that drops it, which `paykit-lib` checks); keeping the second message as the stop, sending no response, end or grant for a conflicted Allowance, and an executor's attempts already open under it (Section 8.3, Conflicted) are rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an own app records its Allowance proposal, acceptance or rejection in the Allowance record, at the version it read, before it sends it, recording no acceptance of its own in this revision (Section 8.3, Recorded first; Section 8.3.1): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- Allowance terms take any positive `every`, and a period too long to evaluate makes the evaluation ineligible, not the terms invalid (wire 4.15);
- a receipt may carry the payee's `metadata`, and, for a payment without a request, a `payment_reference`; a receipt carrying a reference and a request hash is refused (wire 4.11, 1.1; `messages.json` `receipt_members`);
- period details' rates apply from their `created_at`, cut to whole seconds, through their `expires_at`, and each period detail stays valid until its own `expires_at` (wire 4.2, 4.9; `periods.json` `details_rates`);
- a period record keeps every period detail: `period_details` is the latest or the one the stored proof names, `earlier_details` the others, at most 7, and only the write that stores the proof may point `period_details` back at older details (wire 5.3; `sealed.json` `activity_records` checks the record's form, and its `later_writes` a write that drops details seen; no vector has a later write that points `period_details` back);
- a payer's request record of a recurring request holds `reassociations`, at most 16, strictly ascending, each `from_period` a Billing Period start of the stored request, by its second, and a reassociation is added only from a period later than every period in `attempted_periods` of the version its write builds on, which a reader holding that version checks (wire 5.3; `sealed.json` `activity_records`, with its `later_writes` and their `built_on_held`);
- a reassociation is added only after the user confirms it, to an accepted Allowance of the request's counterparty, neither ended nor conflicted, whose terms match the request, possibly not active yet, by a `record.put` carrying `server_time_in(null, from_period)` (Section 8.3, wire 5.3): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an executor accepts a subscription under an Allowance only while the executor's conditions hold and the terms are valid and in force, capacity being checked at each payment, and the request matches the terms; and its acceptance, conditioned on the Allowance record's version, pins the Allowance in `allowance_id` (Sections 8.2, 8.3; wire 5.3): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks; `paykit-lib` checks where `allowance_id` may appear (`sealed.json` `activity_records`) and that a later write never changes or drops one seen, which no vector does;
- a payer pays a Billing Period only of a subscription its request record shows accepted, and an executor pays one only under the Allowance the request record names, which for a recurring request only an executor's acceptance writes, never a period's payment, so a subscription accepted without an Allowance is never paid under one (Sections 8.2, 8.3; wire 5.3, 5.5): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an executor pays automatically only while every condition of Section 8.3, Executor, holds, the pauses of Sections 6, 7.1, 7.2 and 8.3 included; an ended or expired Allowance pays nothing more of a request, whose occurrences are then paid only by hand, a period or lifetime limit without room blocks only the period it affects, and a handoff keeps the Allowance; a payment priced by any rates isn't automatic; a ledger that can't take an entry after compaction, counted with an outcome in every entry, pays nothing automatically, and compaction is judged on the latest verified service time (Section 8.3): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an executor, or an app paying a period by its own policy without its user's confirmation, pays automatically only from the request's first Billing Period when the recorded time of the payer's acceptance precedes that period, else from the period containing that time, and each period only until the earlier of its attempt deadline, for a request with a payment deadline, and the next Billing Period's start, for every request, on the highest service time it has verified and judged again at the first dispatch, other periods being paid only by hand, each within its own payment deadline (Section 8.2, Paying periods): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an executor never attempts an obligation whose claim holds an attempt that its Allowance's ledger names neither in an entry nor in `totals.failed` (Section 8.3, Doubtful obligations): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an executor reads the Allowance record again immediately before an attempt's first dispatch and pays nothing unless it still holds every condition of Section 8.3, Executor, at the version the attempt's batch was conditioned on, a read that fails or a record that doesn't read paying nothing; a resume's repeat under an Allowance that a read made immediately before it shows ended or rejected, or whose end the executor keeps as a stop, or whose record that read fails or doesn't read, is held, never made, its ledger entry staying reserved and the attempt open (Section 8.3, Executor; Attempts already open): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an Allowance counts the amount the payment transfers in the requested asset, never less than the requested amount, for its reservation, its spending and every limit, and a ledger entry's `value` is that amount (Sections 8.2 and 8.3; wire 5.5): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an attempt its user started by hand writes `manual_only` on the occurrence's record in its own batch, the request record of a one-time request or the period record of a Billing Period, created if the period has none, at the version it read, so no executor, nor an app paying a period by its own policy, pays that occurrence automatically again (Section 8.3, Manual only; wire 5.3): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- the current executor writes each attempt's outcome into its ledger entry, in the batch that records the outcome when it records it itself and otherwise from the claim read again, `done` from a claim, `failed` only when the attempt's owner wrote it or the user released the amount with the never-dispatched evidence of Section 8.3, and a later `done` over a `failed`, a compacted failed attempt whose claim shows `done` moving into the totals once; a previous executor records its own attempts' outcomes on their claims alone (Section 8.3, Outcomes in the ledger, Handoff): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` at this revision nor a vector checks;
- a `failed` attempt counts as spent immediately, while its claim remains and after it is gone, whoever wrote the failure, whether the entry holds `failed` or reads it from the claim, and after compaction in `totals.failed`; it releases only on the executor's durable journal evidence that it ended before dispatch and can never dispatch, never on a user's confirmation alone or a restored journal, and a claim, ledger entry or proof showing it paid overrides that evidence; the executor writes `done` from its copy of the request's or Billing Period's payer record holding a proof of this attempt naming this Allowance, whoever made it, while no readable copy, no proof and a proof of another attempt or another or no Allowance release nothing (Section 8.3, `failed` isn't final; wire 5.5, Counting): the pure counting rules and generated `allowance_usage.json` cases are implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`, using already validated attempt evidence; the executor's durable journal, proof collection and ledger updates remain runtime work;
- an executor pays nothing under an Allowance while an answer to its proposal is kept or stored and not yet written into the record, or while its counterparty owes a re-send of replies dropped for the bounds (Section 8.3, Executor);
- an Allowance end is stored before it is sent; a withdrawal crossing the acceptance is answered by an end naming both, unless the Allowance is conflicted, and the messages that cross are reflected on either side; a proposal or answer whose record doesn't read is never written over it, and waits for the scope key the record is sealed under in the client's store, or is kept as a stored message; a counterparty's end is kept as the stop at once, dropped once the record holds an end or a rejection, and, for a record not held, bounded with the cancellations kept as stops and droppable after 30 days, never while a stored message of the Allowance waits to be written, when it counts outside the bounds (Section 8.3, Ends reach the executor, Ending): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- an `allowances` record is never deleted; an app that reads a delete of one puts the record back (the rules of the next item), and until it is back holds it as deleted, under which it pays nothing, writes no grant and sends no acceptance; it ignores a later write that brings the record back without a message it saw, or with another in its place, which `paykit-lib` checks for a later write of a record it is given the seen state of (wire 5.4), and no vector does; an executor pays nothing automatically under an Allowance whose current write it ignores until its user confirms; a proposal over a record held as deleted is taken as conflicted, an acceptance or rejection over one is kept as a reply before its record and an end as the stop for a record not held; and a record put back with `lost_to_delete` is ended for good (Section 8.3, Ending; Section 17, question 33): the rest, rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a `lost_to_delete` member that is present but isn't a valid value, `null` and a value of another type included, never clears a restriction, in any collection that carries it: the record reads at its least permissive state (wire 5, Deletion data that doesn't read); only an absent member means no restriction;
- the `lost_to_delete` member of activity, `payment_lists`, `allowances` and `block_floor` records: its delete item hash, allowed families, validation, preservation in later writes and least permissive states (Section 6; wire 5): this revision does not implement these format checks or the corresponding runtime behavior in `paykit-lib` or the Paykit SDK, and no vector checks them; the code and its positive and rejection vectors must land before release; the origin and lineage evidence specified at Switch revision `f0bbddd1` is absent from the current pin, and the complete protective-publication and service-cursor-hold implementation, Paykit family mapping and integration remain required before release;
- for a deletion of `payee_activity`, an exact predecessor containing an issued receipt, an older signed receipt and a missing copy each have the result in wire Section 5. Required SDK cases check writer attribution, a warning acknowledged before replay, both Billing Period boundaries, a later write at the key and a restart. Restoring a backup that predates the receipt on an installation that still holds it must retain the signed receipt and its credit restriction. Restoring the same backup on an empty installation cannot recover missing receipt bytes. A retained receipt for a settlement prevents crediting that settlement to another occurrence even if it names another payer. An unrelated settlement is judged on its own evidence. These cases check the displayed receipt and the effects on local state, shared records and credit. The public SDK and its end-to-end coverage for this observation are still outstanding;
- the permanent payer hold of an older-copy put-back (Section 6, Payment history lost): runtime checks and generated cases must reject payment through the marked record with an absent claim after 400-day retention and a request clock four minutes ahead, a future-dated or replayed request, an early pinned deadline, a present claim showing only failed attempts, a purged then recreated claim, partial recovery after provider loss despite user confirmation, and erasure followed by reenrollment; every Billing Period of a marked recurring request stays held, including one paid early by hand. Positive cases must preserve the proved exact-predecessor put-back path without adding a new loss marker. No runtime or vector currently checks this rule; its implementation and generated cases are required before release;
- a record whose content withholds or bounds a payment or a credit, of the families Section 6 lists, deleted by another app, is put back over the delete, naming the delete's version, before or atomically with the service cursor acknowledgment of that delete, a durable publication obligation holding acknowledgment while quota, the value limit or another refusal blocks the write: as the app's copy when that copy is the write the delete was built on, naming the deleting app, and otherwise as the family's least permissive state with `lost_to_delete` naming the delete by its item hash, naming no app, under which a payer's occurrence is paid never, by hand neither, once put back from an older copy, with no claim state or user confirmation lifting that hold, and a payee's occurrence is credited nothing; every older copy of a `payee_activity` or `payment_lists` record enters its protected family even without a receipt or `used` entry; a user's confirmation binds the write's item hash and advances only over authenticated exact predecessors, ending at the first delete, missed write, unproven predecessor or other value the app reads, and at a restore; a record held as deleted reads as that state; a write-back that loses to another takes it or writes the two merged over it; an app without a write scope only names the deleting app and tells its user; a deleting app is named once and the user told once per deleting app and once per delete an older copy answers; and a payer deletes a record holding `manual_only` before its request's deadline, or a request record listing a period, never (Section 6, Putting back a deleted record and Deleting finished activity; wire 5, Put back over a delete): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- when more than one accepted Allowance covers a request whose record names none, the executor chooses the one it accepts or pays it under, pays under that one alone, and its batch names it in the request record (Section 8.3, One Allowance per request); an executor that held a ledger version before it became the executor keeps it as its last ledger version (Section 8.3, Last ledger version); and no reader takes a missing ledger as empty (Section 8.3, A grant implies a ledger): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a later write of an Allowance record that drops a `ledger_handoff` its reader has seen, or replaces it by one for the same or an earlier grant, is ignored and flagged; a new executor pays nothing automatically until a valid handoff carrying the complete matching value or successful complete-authority repair establishes its accepted anchor under the current grant; a version the service shows alone or an earlier-term copy supplies none of those paths. The previous executor writes `ledger_handoff` only while the current ledger exactly matches its complete value and every ledger write it sent has a verified answer incorporated into that state; it writes none for a version a restore alone installed, for one it did not write or take by a `ledger_handoff` since its own grant, or for a grant its own app signs again (Section 8.3, Last ledger version and Handoff; wire 2 and 5.4; generated vectors of the `ledger_handoff` binding and signature are in `sealed.json` and `signatures.json`): `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` checks the first, a later write that drops or replaces a seen `ledger_handoff` (next item); the others are rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- handoff preservation and ordering (wire 5.4): required checks cover omission of parsed and unread handoffs, an earlier-grant replay, a changed handoff for the same grant, an unread handoff with a readable grant position and one that cannot be placed, a valid later handoff observed after skipped grant versions, and a later write dropping or replacing the current last grant. Exact preservation and a verified handoff for a strictly later grant are positive cases. A handoff naming no ledger write is signed over its own invalid ledger tuple so its signature verifies and only the version rule refuses it. These format and later-write checks are implemented by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2`; their generated handoff-binding and signature cases are copied here in `sealed.json` and `signatures.json`;
- an executor repairs a ledger it didn't accept only with its user's confirmation of the figures shown, writes exactly the ledger whose figures it showed, and refuses if anything it was built from changed; complete usage authority is required even for an empty inventory, and no complete version kept refuses instead of starting from zero; the merge keeps the version kept as it was, may count an attempt twice, never less, and refuses for a request with more Billing Periods whose claim may still be held than the repair reads (Section 8.3, After a confirmation; wire 5.5): `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` computes the merge of the version kept with the evidence (`merge_for_repair`), keeping that version and counting an attempt twice, never less, and no vector checks it yet; the confirmation, the complete usage authority, the refusals and the write are rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- complete usage authority and its bounded durable state (Section 8.3; wire 5.5) are required before repair and handoff. Required cases cover a lifetime-limited payment compacted into totals, its activity and claim evidence expiring, and provider loss removing the accepted ledger and journal: repair of an empty inventory refuses and a new obligation cannot reuse the lifetime capacity, even after confirmation or a new grant.
