Also required are partial surviving inventories, lost compacted counts and failed items, an older accepted ledger missing a later applied batch, unanswered first-grant, executor, terminal-outcome and compaction writes across failed local persistence and restart, a backup missing a later write, a hash-only or incomplete-source handoff, an older-copy put-back, another instance using the same executor identity, and provider loss showing an older matching copy after that instance's later write was lost. Positive cases retain cumulative usage through compaction, repair a current complete value after definitive non-application or incorporation of an applied pending write, and transfer the complete current value by a valid handoff. A concurrent writer must fail the repair's CAS. Neither deadline expiry nor elapsed usage windows repairs lost completeness. These runtime checks, durable state and generated cases are required with the implementing code before release; neither those checks nor a complete-inventory query exist yet;
- successful repair as an alternative to handoff (Section 8.3; wire 5.4 and 5.5): A hands a complete ledger to B, B writes nothing and goes offline, then A becomes executor again with its complete checkpoint still exactly current. Confirming and applying the conditioned repair durably establishes its result anchor and permits automatic payment when every other check passes. Unknown, refused and locally uncommitted repair results do not; a changed term requires its own authority. A late handoff is checked against the retained first repair base, preserving rejection of an earlier named write, another item hash at the same version, a later named write and a repair built on absence. The authenticated repair result and the base have separate purposes and survive reload. Required runtime and generated cases come with the implementation;
- repair amount reconstruction requires durable transfer-amount evidence, including rounding and excluding fees, for every included attempt; a nominal request or Billing Period amount, a claim, a request proof without an amount, current endpoint units or rates, and confirmation alone cannot supply it. Required rejection cases cover claim-only and proof-only reconstruction after a rounded payment, journals retaining only the request amount, missing evidence after claim expiry, restore or provider loss, and an unaccepted shown ledger that understates the transfer. The concrete request `0.000000001` BTC, transfer `0.00000001` BTC and lifetime limit `0.000000011` BTC must not allow a second `0.00000001` BTC transfer after repair. Positive cases use a retained accepted amount or a journaled exact transfer bound, preserve a larger accepted value, include a recurring period and exclude fees; confirmed failure without never-dispatched evidence stays spent. No current repair implementation or generated case verifies this complete rule; implementation and cases are required before release (Section 8.3, Amounts recovered by a repair; wire 5.5);
- the executor checks a newer ledger version against the last version it accepted and the terms of the `allowances` record it holds, at each service time from the version it accepted to the newer write's, before it builds on it; an app that only reads the ledger flags a newer version that counts less once the reference client hands that check the write's service time (Section 8.3, From one version to the next; wire 5.5): `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` implements the pure check of a newer version against a held one over that range of service times (`check_successor`), and `allowance_usage.json` `compaction` `successors` holds its generated cases; obtaining and authenticating the two service times, handing them to the check and acting on its verdict are rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- `manual_only` is set only on a payer's record, the request record of a one-time or a recurring request or the period record of a Billing Period, as `true` or absent (wire 5.3; `sealed.json` `activity_records`), and a later write never drops one seen, which `paykit-lib` checks and no vector does;
- a period with no period record yet is marked by the write creating it, built on no version, and a payer's period record may hold the mark and no message (Section 8.3, Manual only; wire 5.3): `paykit-lib` takes such a record, which no vector shows;
- the `block_floor` record, one per identity under `paykit_pay`: its value, its binding to the own identity, and a later write of a lower floor ignored (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.6), which `paykit-lib` checks and no vector does;
- recovery of a deleted `block_floor` (wire 5.6): the exact authenticated predecessor preserves its floor; an older copy uses `max(highest floor seen, authenticated applied-delete time + clock tolerance)` only with verified clock continuity covering every contributing floor writer, otherwise automatic one-time dispatch and repeat stay held and numeric put-back waits. Required generated cases include the unseen unblock floor `T + 300 seconds`, deletion at `T + 1`, and a request at `T + 400`: both the temporary deleted view and applied put-back must still require payment by hand, unlike a replacement of `T + 2`. Positive cases preserve the exact-copy floor without an extra raise and a higher floor already held. Further cases cover each unblock writer, restore and missed-item widening, repeated deletion, cooperative moves and in-place key changes preserving clock continuity, undated imported deletes, missing old-generation evidence after provider loss, old-backup restore, erasure and reenrollment, and checked overflow of both the replacement and its request cutoff. Missing evidence or overflow never releases the deleted-view or service-acknowledgment hold. No current runtime or vector checks this rule; its implementation and generated cases are required before release;
- the `settlements` bucket records under `paykit_settlements`: a settlement's bucket and tag and an occurrence's tag, and a bucket's marks, at most 9,000, in ascending order, each settlement once, each naming a one-time request, a Billing Period or a payment without a request, so a mark that names an occurrence no writer produces is invalid (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Sections 1.5 and 5.7), the canonical tagged deletion boundary (1 to 128 strictly ordered unique item hashes, or `unknown`), union with saturation, and refusal of malformed, empty, duplicate, unordered or overbound known lists; every already-retained boundary preserved by later writes, with `unknown` absorbing, and a write carrying a boundary merged over: `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` implements these format and pure merge/later-write checks, including boundary-only buckets. Generated cases are stored in `sealed.json` `settlement_marks` and its `boundaries` group, with derivations and the fixed policy in `identifiers.json`. Authenticated creation and continuity, delete application and publication, cursor holds, settlement-credit enforcement and host warnings remain runtime work;
- `paykit_request` and every labelled selling scope require `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`, so a payee app's certificate holds all three (Section 3.1, wire 3.1): `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` checks these requirements. The `documents.json` `certificates` entries for payee apps, including `hosted`, hold both required scopes; `documents.json` `payee_certificates` includes accepted plain and labelled selling certificates and rejection cases missing a required scope;
- a one-time request whose signed `created_at` is earlier than its sender's block mark's latest time, or the block floor, plus the clock tolerance, opens its payer record with `manual_only`, also when that record is written later, and once a read that opened it without the mark is done, the block floor being the identity's `block_floor` record, raised only as a blocked window closes, by each app once, or as a widening applies, never per message and never at the ask: by a Paykit App holding `paykit_pay` that removes a sender from the deny list, to the service time of its latest verified read plus the clock tolerance before it writes the unblock, which it doesn't write if the raise fails; by each app holding `paykit_pay`, in the commit that records an unblock it didn't write, as its read of the lists shows it, to the same time, before it takes any further entry of that sender or queues any ask, so a copy that comes back unasked after the unblock opens marked in every own app that saw the block, by a restore that isn't clean to the time of its first read of the lists and by a `missed` item to the earlier of its last acceptance time and the service time of the read that passes it, each in the commit that applies its widening, the sender's entries and asks held until the raise is applied and a widening applied only with its raise; an app without `paykit_pay` raising and holding nothing for the floor; every unblock moving every unpaid one-time request created before it to payment by hand; an own app that joins after an unblock no app holding `paykit_pay` raised the floor before writing, before any own app holding `paykit_pay` that saw the block raised it, isn't covered; a recurring one opens with no mark; no app pays a one-time request so dated automatically, whatever its record holds, judged by a read of the record made immediately before each automatic dispatch, a read that fails or doesn't read withholding the dispatch; and a floor an app can't read holds every one-time request (Section 8.1, Blocked counterparties): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- `paykit_allowance_execute` requires `paykit_contacts`, so an executor's certificate holds both (Section 3.1, wire 3.1; `documents.json` `executor_certificates`);
- `paykit_pay` requires `paykit_contacts`, so a paying app's certificate holds both (Section 3.1, wire 3.1; `documents.json` `payer_certificates`, and the certificates of `messages.json` `cancellation_roles` and `sealed.json` `allowance_records`);
- the record of a payment without a request keeps its amount in `unrequested.amount`, the stored proof's (wire 4.10, 5.3; `sealed.json` `activity_records`);
- a writer that writes a shared record again keeps every member it doesn't know, each object's as it was read (wire 5; `sealed.json` `activity_records`, the record of a payment without a request with members its reader doesn't know);
- the payee's lower bound on the verified payment time, which an own app that didn't record the request takes from the request's signed `created_at` (Section 7.1): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- after a `missed` item, an app holds every open request and Allowance as after a restore, makes no automatic attempt or acceptance and repeats no dispatch made before the item, counts no confirmation given before it, holds a request first seen after it only if its signed `created_at` is earlier than the item's last acceptance time plus the clock tolerance, with nothing releasing the hold automatically, and asks each counterparty of an open request or Allowance, and of its unfinished activity records, to re-send, never from before its cursor's time less the clock tolerance (Section 7.2, What a `missed` item loses): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a `missed` item marks every counterparty its deny list holds at its next read of the lists as blocked from the item's earliest to its last acceptance time, and asks it once it is unblocked, raising the block floor to the earlier of that window's last acceptance time and the service time of the read that passes the item in the commit that applies those marks (Section 7.2, What a `missed` item loses): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- after a provider loss, an obligation uncertain by Section 7.3 (a request its inbox accepted before the end of the residual window, or whose signed `created_at` is earlier than that end plus the clock tolerance; no Billing Period starting after the declaration plus the residual window) is in doubt at the new service until its claim there is SETTLED or the user states it wasn't paid before the loss, a statement a newer loss ends: nothing pays it automatically, and a payment by hand needs the user's confirmation that it may already be paid; one the app can't place is in doubt; the app keeps `declared_at_ms` and `uncertain_until_ms` for it: a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks (Section 7.3, After a provider loss);
- the prototype predates Section 6, Different writes at the same activity version. The Paykit SDK holds activity that depends on a different signed write at the held version. The Switch revision adopted here reports when its selected write differs from the signed write the app holds at the same version. Paykit conformance requires a payer and a payee to hold the payment, credit or record update that depends on a disputed request or Billing Period, including a period that depends on its disputed parent, while unrelated activity continues. A reload must preserve the hold, local stops and the outcome of a payment already made. A backup restore must read the service before acting and cannot reconstruct a missing comparison from the journal. When the original signed write is selected again or a successor is accepted, the app must recheck the current record and retry an eligible retained message once with its original ID. These are SDK runtime cases. The shared wire vectors and the Switch client's tests do not exercise the Paykit effects.

- bounded message eviction guarantees the atomic recording of the recovery obligation with the required payload and processed-pair changes, not eventual payload recovery. A permitted re-send within retention remains eligible under the pair-forgetting rule. The ordinary mark lifetime and retirement policy remain unchanged, so an evicted payload may be lost permanently (Sections 3.3, 8.1 and 9): required runtime cases, not existing library or vector checks;
- strict ordinary admission keeps each existing family within its stated count bounds and preserves its required victim: a protected earlier victim causes only the unadmitted input to use the generic client admission wait. - late recording of a payment already dispatched without the switch must be tested with authoritative success more than ten minutes after the first returning answer, reconciliation lasting longer than that, quota refusal and an expired first submission; each established uncreated claim can still be recorded under the original attempt ID without another payment call. Missing answers, an absent claim after provider loss or forgetting, expired batch evidence and non-authoritative or expired method history must not authorize a fresh attempt, a refreshed pin or a repeat dispatch; unknown outcomes remain accounted for (Section 8.4, Late recording; wire 5.3): required runtime tests, not library or vector checks;

The mark asks that sender to re-send as soon as sends to it aren't held, whatever records the app holds, a mark of key waits once the key is held, and again at most once in any 24 hours while it lives; a mark goes 30 days after its latest time, or for a bound on marks only once an ask covering its latest time was delivered, a block mark only a restore that isn't clean or a `missed` item made going first, and with none such the earliest whose ask was refused or can't be sent yet, the host told, so a bound never holds an entry; a mark waiting only on held sends counts toward no bound, the deny list's limit bounding those; an ask known to be undelivered permits another ask at most once in any 24 hours, a terminal unknown ask is reconciled under Section 9, none queued while one to that sender is in the outbox; a required victim whose drop cannot be committed is retained without admitting an incoming counted payload above the family's bound; a protected prior victim defers only the incoming admission, and an actual storage failure remains a failure; an undelivered obligation retired at expiry or for capacity is reported to the host, with no payload, and neither that notice nor an ask guarantees recovery, which remains best effort under the bounded mark lifecycle and sender retention (Section 9, Messages dropped for a bound): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a message of any recorded kind (wire 4.3), Allowance messages and period details included, whose effect is decided from whether the app holds a record of a collection the app's copy hasn't listed yet, from its join, after a restore, after a `missed` item until the app has listed again, or after a certificate that reads collections, or members of one, that the certificate it last listed under, or any it held since, didn't, or period details it would take with no effect from such a record, is held unprocessed and read again once it is listed; one about a record the copy holds builds its write on the version held, or over a refused write as question 36 says, and is built again on a newer one; one that needs a scope key the app doesn't hold yet waits for it as Section 8.1, Stored entries says (Section 8.1, Records not listed yet): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- every queued delivery and re-send is judged by the current per-message send rule before transmission, including retries, generic flush, payment resume, kept-batch resolution, reload and restore; blocked or unknown recipients retain the held kinds, including automatic Allowance ends for crossing withdrawals, while eligible user-initiated ends, other permitted stopping and payment-outcome messages and list withdrawals remain deliverable; a confirmed blocked subscription acceptance requires its exact durable confirmation and a fresh known deny decision for its original delivery only; durable origin evidence, role and effect, durable holds, failed hold updates, replay expiry and release after an unblock follow Section 8.1, Messages already queued. The required generic client support, SDK integration and conformance cases below remain unimplemented, and no client API or vector conformance is claimed for them;
- a list request naming the newest version sent gets a newer version, one naming an older version that hasn't expired gets the newest re-sent, answers are bounded per version named and per 24 hours, a version already answered gets the newest re-sent at most once an hour, a payer without a usable list asks again at most once in any 24 hours, and a blocked counterparty gets no list, renewal or answer (Section 8.4, Answering a list request, Blocked counterparties): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a publishing app holding `paykit_contacts` sends no list, renewal or answer while its copy of the deny list holds the counterparty or isn't known, keeps what is owed one it already sends its lists to until that ends and nothing else holds it, refuses a list the host chooses to send, and holds an answer or a proof's newer version past the daily bound until the bound allows, answered once; a payer sends no list request while its copy holds the payee or isn't known, and asks again once that ends within 24 hours of the request, otherwise at its next payment; a withdrawal goes at once whether sends are held or not, is sent again an hour after it at the earliest, then hourly for a day and daily, while a version the counterparty may hold can be paid, at once for a restore's withdrawal never queued, never after the counterparty's switch refused it for good; and after a restore, until the host sets its list, a withdrawal also goes to a counterparty the restored state doesn't know, every newly constructed withdrawal reserving a greater per-recipient pair through current protected authority (Sections 8.1, 8.4.1): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- the publishing app keeps what each version of its Private Payment Lists offered until 30 days after its `expires_at`, credits a proof naming such a version within that time only for a payload it offered that payer at that version, sends no receipt for one it didn't, and after that time checks such a proof by its payment method alone (Section 8.4, Only what it offered): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a publishing app gives a single-use payload back to its payment method only when no payer can pay it, never one of a version a restore brought back before it was seen delivered short of the app's removal, and owes a change of its list to every counterparty that gets it from the commit that makes it until its message is queued, a version that fails to go for another reason tried again hourly for a day, then daily (Section 8.4, Giving payloads back, Publishing): rules of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- two writes of the Paykit Profile at once leave the later one, and an app reads the Profile again just before it writes it whole (Section 4): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- the Paykit Profile grant covers `/pub/paykit/profile.json` and `/pub/paykit/blobs/` together or not at all, and an app without them offers no Profile editing and asks for them again only when its user asks to edit the Profile (Section 3.4): rules of the signer and of an app's run; `paykit-lib`'s signer tests check that both paths are offered, and no vector checks either;
- after a clean restore, one the cursor the service holds for the app shows to have passed no entry since the backup (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.4), an app holds nothing for the restore and asks nothing; after any other restore from a backup, an app holds every open request and Allowance, making no automatic attempt or acceptance on them, until the record shows a cancellation or end or the user confirms, and asks each counterparty to re-send from the backup's position (Section 7.2, What a restore loses): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib`, a stateless library, nor a vector checks; the conformance case `restore_stale_backup_cancellation_stop`, below, does;
- a restored publishing app whose backup holds its list or a list it sent sends no list but a withdrawal until its host sets its list again, tells its host so once, and removes every carried list delivery, empty or nonempty, before the host can send it, durably retaining its obligation to withdraw and suppressing any later materialization from a kept batch; it withdraws with a newly signed empty version reserved under Section 8.4.1, even after a clean restore, and sends the list again when its host sets it unless its host withdrew it since (Sections 7.2, What a restore loses, and 8.4, Withdrawing a list): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- a restore that isn't clean marks every counterparty its deny list holds at its next read of the lists as blocked, from the backup's cursor time less the clock tolerance, or the restore's bound on the times it restores where that is earlier, to that read, asks it once it is unblocked, and raises the block floor to that read's time in the commit that applies those marks; a sender blocked and unblocked again between the backup and that read is asked only by the restore's other re-sends, and a one-time request of it that comes back opens with `manual_only` (Section 7.2, What a restore loses): a rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks;
- after a restore, delivery from a carried outbox or stored signed plaintext requires current evidence that the exact original delivery remains pending. A backup taken before a give-up may contain the outbox entry and no unknown-delivery duty. Another backup may hold only the record's signed bytes after the terminal duty was acknowledged. Neither case permits automatic delivery or a replacement. The host reconciles the possible effect. A delivery proved still pending is subject to the original deadline, current send authority and the record checks of Section 7.2, What a restore loses; these runtime cases have no Paykit vector;
- the dispatch window (Section 7.2 step 1): `orphan_after` at least the payment method's longest time plus the allowance before the dispatch, a dispatch only within that allowance, and a repeat only while the method's longest time fits before the orphan age. A rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks. The Paykit SDK on the `v0.2` branch implements it; its flow tests `test_fence_attempt_past_its_pre_dispatch_budget_not_paid`, `test_orphan_after_below_the_floor_refused`, `test_default_config_takes_a_one_day_rail` and `test_repeat_too_close_to_the_orphan_age_not_made` cover it, and the conformance case `dispatch_window`, below, states it;
- the restored certificate (Section 7.2): a certificate Paykit's rules refuse is never installed by a restore or a join, and nothing is done under one taken later. A rule of an app's run, which neither `paykit-lib` nor a vector checks. The same SDK implements it; its flow tests `test_restore_certificate_paykit_refuses_writes_nothing`, `test_certificate_join_paykit_refuses_writes_and_sends_nothing`, `test_certificate_renewal_paykit_refuses_holds_entries_and_refuses_calls` and `test_certificate_restore_then_renewal_paykit_refuses_takes_no_action` cover it, and the conformance case `restored_certificate`, below, states it.

Appendix A of the wire format marks the rows where the prototype differs from a choice there: A2, A3, A5, A10, A14 and A16.

**Conformance cases.** Rules of an app's run that no vector can check are checked by these cases, which an implementation runs against its own app and a switch:

- **`private_list_reservations_are_recipient_local`** (Section 8.4.1; wire 4.13 and 5.8).
  - *Setup:* one app publishes to A and B at the same verified generation. Hold two different prepared A messages at one predecessor; also copy one identical prepared intent before signing.
  - *Steps:* reserve for B repeatedly between two A reservations; submit both A contenders; interrupt/restart the identical-intent instances at signing and queue materialization. Exercise first creation and ordinary continuation, and a requester naming a larger unknown pair.
  - *Required:* A advances only for A's reservations, including burned ones; exactly one distinct contender applies. The copied intent yields identical signed bytes and one queue obligation. No hint resets or fast-forwards authority. All proof, used and journal paths retain both pair members and recipient binding. First creation and updates consume ordinary batch capacity, not repeatable-control capacity.
- **`private_list_intents_survive_unknown_outcomes`** (Section 8.4.1).
  - *Setup:* retain nonempty grant G and an owed withdrawal, with reserved single-use payloads and an available current counter.
  - *Steps:* crash before/after each durable intent, service apply, receipt, signature and queue commit; lose the CAS reply; retry the exact batch; resolve by a verified current view; race G against a withdrawal and then against a genuinely later explicit host grant. Repeat after generation adoption and with an expired delivery.
  - *Required:* unknown never becomes unused; no message is signed or made deliverable without its exact applied reservation. Missing prepared bytes burn the number. A stale G cannot rebase over the withdrawal. A later explicit grant may resume and suppress old withdrawal retries, without reviving older grants. Every route honors suppression; endpoint reservations and old unknown batches remain accounted for. Retry of a pending delivery keeps its exact bytes and deadline. A given-up delivery is never requeued. A separately authorized new publication reserves a new pair.
- **`private_list_origins_do_not_reset`** (Section 8.4.1; wire 5.8).
  - *Setup:* a valid origin is followed by deletion/forgetting, removal of every witness, or a recovered/unreadable/divergent current value. Keep a backup below the actual latest counter.
  - *Steps:* attempt first creation and same-generation continuation from each state, including through restore; race two originals at one key and two keys for the last origin slot. Attempt publication by another own app holding the scope. Complete a cooperative transfer containing live, retired and unknown slots.
  - *Required:* a genuine first original succeeds exactly once; deleted/forgotten/unknown/recovered authority never resets. Wrong-writer evidence holds. Slots/count survive transfer without omission; partial import activates nothing. At capacity an existing clean-key update remains eligible under ordinary limits, while a new recipient/generation remains owed and refused. Missing registration/keys, full local storage, retained unknown effects and recipient refusal never erase the duty.
  - *Healthy ordinary pruning:* also create and advance a clean counter through ordinary updates until the generic contract permits pruning its original and intermediate operation bytes, then restore a backup predating original creation. With the dated publisher-signed current operation and complete clean slot/current-lineage evidence, the withdrawal reserves the next counter. Permitted pruning alone must not hold this healthy path; every missing required node, unknown/recovered origin or retained wrong-writer observation still does.
- **`private_list_withdrawal_precedes_recovery_completion`** (Section 8.4.1).
  - *Setup:* adopt a genuinely greater signed generation and verify own registration while an older witness never returns and older uploads, record listings, pending batches and other-app removals remain incomplete.
  - *Steps:* durably observe the boundary, reserve a current-birth original and subsequent update, and deliver an empty withdrawal; interrupt/restart before observer acknowledgment. Repeat with same-generation or unclassified pending work, unknown registration, mixed operations, an implicitly attached cursor and invalid late old-generation origin evidence.
  - *Required:* the eligible one-operation path and empty delivery succeed without completing older work or waiting for signer clearance; notification persists and precedes actions. Same-generation/unclassified cases refuse. No old reservation, batch, cursor hold or completeness bit is cleared. Late invalid evidence allocates nothing and cannot reset the new namespace. Empty delivery creates no own conversation entry/invite and still obeys recipient admission/deny; nonempty lists retain their normal gates.
- **`private_list_pair_receiver_and_bounds`** (Section 8.4.1; wire 4.13 and 5.8).
  - *Steps:* install `(g+1,1)` after `(g,9007199254740991)`; deliver lower and equal-pair/different-hash messages, an empty list, expired lists, and a list of a generation above the payee's verified `identity.json`, then let the verified document catch up without delivering that list again; on both sides of the catch-up, attempt a payment to an otherwise eligible, unexpired single-use endpoint of that list. Exercise proof/consumed lookup across recipients and generations; maximal values and retained unknown members; complete origin/view/transfer encodings and ordinary rate/key/byte/local limits.
  - *Required:* only a greater authenticated pair replaces; equivocation is rejected and reported; empty/expired ordering and consumed marks remain. A list of a generation above the verified document is installed by its pair before any payment, is refused while the document is behind, with no `used` entry written and no version consumed, and becomes payable unchanged, without redelivery, once the document reaches its generation; a lower pair of that app stays ignored throughout. Unavailable history holds. Empty/nonempty counter values are both 528 ciphertext bytes; JSON over 508 bytes is refused whole. Every generic cap is enforced, not satisfied by truncation. A refusal leaves duties and uncertain resources retained. Positive and rejection encoding vectors for the pair and publication record are generated with their implementing code.
- **`queued_sends_follow_current_deny_state`** (Sections 8.1 and 8.4).
  - *Setup:* an admitted counterparty has received an initial Private Payment List. A transient delivery failure leaves one changed nonempty list queued. Before the next attempted delivery, the app either blocks the recipient or fails to refresh an expired deny copy, which is now unknown. No changed list has reached the recipient at this point.
  - *Steps:* independently exercise direct retry, generic flush, payment resume, re-send, resolving a previously unanswered batch, reload followed by flush, and restore followed by flush. Repeat for each held kind in Section 8.1, including both roles of a cancellation, both reasons for an acceptance and both origins of an Allowance end. Include a failure to persist a hold and a crash after queueing but before a delivery attempt.
  - *Required:* no message required to wait reaches the counterparty through any route. Neither a clean restore nor a batch that later applies makes it eligible. A known, permitted recipient can receive an otherwise eligible queued message after an unblock; one still unknown cannot. Expired messages are given up, never delivered after the hold ends. A carried list removed on restore never reappears, whether it was nonempty or an empty withdrawal; the fresh restore withdrawal follows the case below, and only a newly permitted nonempty list after the host sets its endpoints may follow it.
- **`queued_stops_and_outcomes_still_go`** (Sections 7.2, 8.1, 8.3 and 8.4).
  - *Setup:* independently queue an empty newer list withdrawal, an owed receipt, a dispatched payment's acceptance and proof, a payee cancellation and an eligible user-initiated `allowance_end` under a transient delivery failure. Keep an unrelated nonempty list and list request to the same recipient queued beside each. Establish either a block or an unknown deny copy before delivery.
  - *Steps:* exercise the same delivery routes above, with the required restore first-read guard completed where applicable. For a positive restore case, retain current evidence that each exact original delivery is still pending. Also restore only an older outbox copy after the original store gave up the delivery with an unknown outcome. Separately try a payer cancellation, a subscription acceptance that reports no dispatched payment and an automatic Allowance end for a crossing withdrawal over a seeded accepted Allowance record (Section 8.3.1); also attempt to reconstruct a receipt from a shared record after restore while sends remain held.
  - *Required:* a stopping or payment-outcome message proved still owed reaches the counterparty while the unrelated held messages do not. Retries preserve the original message, except that restore selectively supersedes the carried list delivery with the newly signed empty version required by Sections 7.2 and 8.4. This replacement authorizes no duplicate payment, receipt, proof, acceptance, cancellation or Allowance end. The withdrawal leaves no usable list, including in the post-backup publication case below. The payer cancellation, subscription acceptance and automatic crossing end stay held. The automatic end remains recorded and its own-app notice still goes. The exception does not reconstruct a receipt forbidden by the restore rule, erase a recorded stop, or release all sends to the recipient. The stale outbox copy stays held for reconciliation. Missing evidence of a permitted role or effect keeps that message held rather than treating its kind as sufficient.

- **`queued_allowance_ends_follow_their_origin`** (Sections 8.1 and 8.3).
  - *Setup:* an app holding `paykit_allowance` and `paykit_contacts` independently queues, for the same counterparty, an eligible user-initiated end of one Allowance and an end generated automatically for a proposer's withdrawal crossing an acceptance of another, its accepted Allowance record seeded since acceptance is unavailable in this revision (Section 8.3.1). Both ends are recorded; a transient delivery failure or an unanswered batch leaves their counterparty messages owed. Establish either a block or an unknown deny copy before the next delivery.
  - *Steps:* exercise every delivery route in `queued_sends_follow_current_deny_state`, including later application of the kept batch, reload, and clean and uncertain restore with their ordinary guards completed and current evidence that each original delivery is still owed. Separately make the durable evidence of an end's origin unavailable. Also exercise the crossing withdrawal arriving while sends are already held, as in `crossing_end_recorded_while_held`.
  - *Required:* the user's eligible end reaches the counterparty without a new logical end, while the automatic crossing end stays held on every route. Both durable ends stand and their own-app notices remain eligible; the executor makes no new payment under either ended Allowance. An end with unknown origin stays held rather than gaining the exception from its kind, reference form, signature, recorded end or queued status. Once sends are permitted and all other restrictions allow it, the automatic end may go with its original bytes before its replay deadline. An expired message is given up under the existing rules, while the end remains in the record.

- **`restored_withdrawal_supersedes_unseen_list`** (Sections 7.2, 8.1 and 8.4; wire 4.13).
  - *Setup:* a transient failure leaves an empty withdrawal W queued at version v. Take backup K, then have the host resume sharing and deliver a nonempty list L at a greater version to the same counterparty. Restore K before the host re-applies those changes. Exercise both a clean and an uncertain restore verdict and both a block and an unknown deny copy. Keep an owed receipt and dispatched-payment proof beside W in K.
  - *Steps:* complete the ordinary certificate, current-identity and restore first-read guards. Attempt every delivery route in `queued_sends_follow_current_deny_state`, including a carried kept batch that later applies. Include a failed local update and a crash between suppressing W and delivering its replacement. Deliver replacement W1, explicitly re-enable and deliver L2, then restore the same K and deliver W2, while every verified time is equal. Repeat when the recipient first became known after K, and after a genuine generation change followed by two restores within that generation. In a separate control, retry W without restoring and without any later publication.
  - *Required:* the app never treats W's old signed version as satisfying the restored withdrawal. It suppresses that carried delivery and retains the obligation durably, then sends a newly signed empty version whose protected reservation pair is greater than L, without relying on a clock or burst bound. W2 exceeds L2 through the current recipient counter; at a greater generation counter 1 exceeds every earlier-generation counter, and the next restore continues that new namespace. The counterparty holds no usable list afterward. If the valid replacement cannot yet be published, the obligation waits and W is not released. The unrelated owed receipt and proof remain deliverable under their own rules, while nonempty lists and list requests remain held. The control without restore retries the original eligible W normally. Later retries of the fresh withdrawal do not extend any signed message's replay deadline.

- **`restore_stale_backup_cancellation_stop`** (Sections 7.2, What a restore loses, and 8.1 to 8.3).
  - *Setup:* payer identity A with one app P holding `paykit_pay` and `paykit_contacts` (a variant has two apps, both of whose record writes fail); payee identity B with an app S holding `paykit_request`, `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`. S sends R1, a one-time request without a deadline, R2, a recurring request that P accepts (a variant pays R2 under an Allowance with P as its executor, seeded, since acceptance is unavailable in this revision (Section 8.3.1), as an accepted Allowance record holding P's grant and grant history with P's accepted complete ledger and P's consent list holding it), and R3, another one-time request without a deadline. P records all three. P takes a layered backup K0 at cursor c0.
  - *Steps:* S sends a `payment_request_cancellation` C1 for R1 and C2 for R2. P verifies both, keeps the stops and passes the entries in that commit, at cursor c1 after c0. Then either (F) P's request record writes are refused for good, such as with an injected `403 permission_missing`, or (P) P stops before it sends them. Every target app's cursor has passed the entries, so the service deletes them. P is restored from K0.
  - *Expected:* P was restored, so it holds every open request and Allowance. Nothing in the chain shows that P passed anything after c0, since the entries were passed with cursor moves alone, so an implementation that relies on the chain to decide whether to hold fails this case. P makes no automatic attempt on R1, R3 or any Billing Period of R2, in the Allowance variant even after the user confirms the Allowance again (Section 8.3); a manual attempt on R1 or R3 needs the user's confirmation that the payee may have cancelled it; and P sends S a `resend_request` with `accepted_from` at the `accepted_at` of the entry at c0, less 5 minutes.
  - *Branches:* (i) S re-sends C1 and C2: P keeps both stops, makes no attempt on R1 or R2 even with the user's confirmation, and writes the request records; R3 stays held until the user confirms it. (ii) S never answers: the hold stays; once the user confirms a request, an attempt on it is allowed. (iii) P is restored instead from a backup K1 taken at c1: the kept stops come back and stop R1 and R2 for good, and no confirmation overrides them; the hold still covers every other open request and Allowance, so R3 is held until the user confirms it. (iv) Another own app's record write succeeded: the record shows the cancellations, which stop R1 and R2 for good.

- **`dispatch_window`** (Section 7.2 step 1).
  - *Setup:* payer identity A with one app P holding `paykit_pay` and `paykit_contacts`, paying through a method that declares its longest time L and isn't idempotent; payee identity B with an app S holding `paykit_request`, `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`. S sends R, a one-time request.
  - *Steps:* P writes the `attempt` for R. (a) The dispatch is delayed, after the `attempt`'s answer, until P's allowance before the dispatch has passed. (b) P is configured with an `orphan_after` below L plus that allowance.
  - *Expected:* (a) P submits nothing and writes `failed`, and S sees no acceptance. (b) P refuses the configuration before it writes any `attempt`.
- **`multi_protocol_own_lifecycle`** (Section 3.2; What this pin adopts).
  - *Setup:* an own certificate with valid Paykit scopes and another protocol's scope, and a host protocol set registering both owners with disjoint collections and local state.
  - *Steps:* use the public Paykit lifecycle to join, persist and load, then take and restore a layered backup. At each path receive a valid kind for each owner, inspect and acknowledge their duties, and apply a shared deny-list change while one owner cannot persist its callback. Repeat with a missing owner and a certificate missing a permission Paykit requires.
  - *Required:* the valid mixed certificate works through all three public paths without removing the other owner or bypassing Paykit validation; each owner handles only its kinds and local writes, and Paykit never acknowledges another owner's duty. The deny barrier holds until every owner durably takes it, including after reload. Missing owners and invalid Paykit authority refuse without partial lifecycle installation. These are required SDK cases, not existing vector coverage.
- **`restored_certificate`** (Section 7.2, The restored certificate).
  - *Setup:* identity A with app P holding `paykit_pay` and `paykit_contacts`; its certificate passes Paykit's rules. P takes a layered backup K0. A's signer then renews P's certificate with one switch permission that P's Paykit scopes don't derive, which the Switch takes and Paykit refuses, and lists it in `identity.json`.
  - *Steps:* (a) A backup K1 carrying the refused certificate, made by hand since P takes no backup under a certificate it refuses, is restored. (b) P is restored from K0, then reads its feed, and a counterparty sends it a `payment_request`.
  - *Expected:* (a) The restore is refused and writes nothing. (b) The restore succeeds; the first read takes the renewed certificate; P then sends nothing, pays nothing and holds the request unprocessed. Once A renews the certificate again with one that passes, P processes the request.
- **`crossing_end_recorded_while_held`** (Section 8.3, Ending and Blocked counterparties).
  - *Setup:* allower identity A with two apps holding `paykit_contacts`: W, where the user answers Allowance proposals, and X, its executor; allowee identity B with an app S. Seed, since acceptance is unavailable in this revision (Section 8.3.1), A's Allowance record holding S's proposal, W's acceptance, X's grant and grant history, with X's accepted complete ledger and X's consent list, kept durably, holding the Allowance, while S holds only its proposal; S withdraws the proposal before it has the acceptance, so the withdrawal crosses the acceptance. A's user blocks B before W reads the withdrawal.
  - *Steps:* (a) W reads the withdrawal while sends to B are held. (b) A's user unblocks B, and X runs before W runs again; S has an open request the Allowance would cover, which X would otherwise pay automatically.
  - *Expected:* (a) the Allowance record holds the end naming the proposal and the acceptance, X is sent the `executor_notice`, and nothing reaches S. (b) X pays nothing under the Allowance: it finds the end in the record, and a batch it built on the record's version before the end fails. At W's next run the end's message reaches S once.
- **`settlement_credited_once`** (Sections 6, Settlement marks, and 8.1 step 4).
  - *Setup:* payee identity B with two apps, W holding `paykit_request`, `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`, and H holding `paykit_request:shop1`, `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts`; payer identity A. W has sent A a one-time request R1, and H a one-time request R2 under `shop1`, for the same amount, both payable to one reusable endpoint of B. A pays R1 by one settlement S, and sends a proof naming S for R1 and another for R2, as a payer reusing one settlement would.
  - *Steps:* (a) W and H verify their proofs and submit their credit batches at once. (b) A new app J of B holding `paykit_request`, `paykit_settlements` and `paykit_contacts` joins, and A sends a proof naming S for a new request R3 that J sent. (c) A faulty app of B deletes S's bucket, and A sends the proof for R3 again.
  - *Expected:* (a) one batch applies; the other app's record keeps its proof and no receipt, it sends A no receipt, and its user is shown that S was credited to another occurrence; H learns of R1 only what Section 12 says. (b) J reads S's bucket, finds its mark, and credits the proof for R3 to nothing. (c) W and J, which saw the mark, credit nothing, show the delete with its writer and put the bucket back at once (`specs/v0.2/wire-format.md` Section 5.7, Put back over a delete), S's mark included, so an app that joins afterwards finds S's mark; an app that reads the bucket while it is deleted credits S neither automatically nor by hand, whatever its user confirms, since S predates the delete and no evidence of Section 6, Settlement marks, clears it, and tells its host (`CreditHeldForDelete`).


- **`settlement_delete_boundaries`** (Section 6, Settlement marks; wire 5.7). These are normative requirements for the settlement runtime and its end-to-end tests, which remain to come. The bucket formats and pure merge/later-write rules are already checked by `paykit-lib` on `v0.2` and the generated `sealed.json` `settlement_marks` cases; those checks do not establish the runtime guarantees below.
  - *A hash published before its delete applies:* M correctly signs D with the expected version of a later bucket credit and computes its item hash before submitting D. An ordinary authenticated update W preserves all prior marks and boundaries and adds that hash. A reader sees W before D applies and records a method height, a time or a fresh-destination issuance. None clears the hash: its actual application and the required later order are unproved. After a credit and D have applied and an exact-current-delete protective put-back has restored W's partial copy, another reader still cannot reuse W's earlier observations to credit that settlement again. Valid signatures, union, incarnation continuity and publication before acknowledgment do not turn W into a later carrier. The correctly signed D and valid ordinary W must be used in the eventual executable conformance case.
  - *A carrier proved later than the applied delete:* the reader authenticates D's application to this bucket and a subsequent carrier C, including their causal order. A method height observed after verifying C, C's authenticated application time with the required tolerances, or a guaranteed fresh destination issued and offered after that verification may supply the corresponding conservative bound. A settlement satisfying that bound and all other retained boundaries may be credited. One at the numeric time or height bound is not beyond it; one that predates D is withheld. A carrier with only a valid signature, a proof for another bucket or delete, and an order not established across generations do not qualify.
  - *Retained observations after restore or repeated deletion:* keep a prepublication observation, restore it or recover its carrier, and later learn D's valid application proof. The earlier observation remains earlier. A later deletion context naming the same hash cannot reuse evidence of an earlier application without proving that it also covers the later one. A recovered D1-only copy never clears retained D2; valid later evidence covering both permits a genuinely later settlement, while missing or incomparable order holds it. Bounded evidence insufficient to prove the order causes a hold, not lifetime-history growth or a weaker cutoff.
  - *An absent read races with a credit and delete:* W reads the bucket absent and prepares a credit of S. H then credits S to another occurrence, and a faulty own app deletes that bucket before W's batch applies. The publication-time absence check refuses W's entire batch, because zero cannot match the retained delete. W emits no second receipt and decides again under the deletion rules. Extra pre-create reads do not replace this check. A genuine absent-key creation with the required applied evidence remains permitted. The same refusal applies to a receipt-only by-hand credit whose bucket write is omitted: the missing bucket write does not remove the publication-time guard.
  - *An exact-copy put-back races with a later delete:* W proves D1's complete predecessor and prepares its put-back. Another put-back lands, S is credited, and D2 deletes its mark before W applies its prepared D1 restoration. The exact-delete check refuses W's stale put-back; proof of D1's predecessor does not authorize it over D2. W reads and retains D2 as required, and S gets no second credit. A put-back applied while the proved D1 is still current remains permitted.
  - *Unmarked recreation and later readers:* a faulty app recreates a deleted bucket without a boundary, then writes a further signed version. A fresh app, an app with no seen marks and an old-backup restore each read only that latest value. None credits a proof until it verifies absence-only creation or protective put-back and the required exact-predecessor continuation. A signature, a nonzero or increasing version and a current value without a boundary are insufficient. User confirmation and by-hand repair do not grant credit authority. An authenticated valid continuation remains usable under every retained boundary.
  - *Provenance across restart and recovery:* keep an unresolved incarnation hold and retained deletion restrictions, then retry, reload, restore an older backup or upload an older value after provider loss. None resets the origin or clears the hold. Missing predecessor evidence and two recovered values reusing a version do not prove continuity. Credit resumes only when the required evidence is reauthenticated and every deletion boundary independently permits that settlement. Conservative boundary publication still produces no credit by itself.
  - *A delete first seen without marks:* W holds another selling label than the app that credited S, and has never seen S's mark or its bucket's predecessor. W reads the current delete D and durably retains it. W publishes `{ "v": 1, "marks": "", "lost_to_delete": { "kind": "known", "deletes": [D] } }` in canonical encoding before, or atomically with, the service cursor acknowledgment of D. The empty marks string is valid only with a valid boundary; an empty unrestricted bucket remains invalid. No fictitious mark is added. A later app J that cannot see the original receipt lists the live boundary-only bucket, withholds S and sends no receipt. A later mark merges into that same bucket without removing D.
  - *Publication refused, delayed or interrupted:* the same write hits a quota/value limit, lacks a key, loses its answer or is interrupted by a crash after local retention. W's local receive position may advance, but no deferred flush, unrelated batch, restart, restore or other device sharing W's cursor acknowledges D at the service until the protective write is verified applied. While W remains current, the Switch cannot forget D. Once publication succeeds, later apps see the restriction even after the old delete's chain history is compacted. Removing or losing every unpublished witness first is the explicit limitation of Section 13, never a successful publication test.
  - *Boundary-only unknown and recovery:* malformed readable boundary data with no recoverable marks, known-lost local deletion state, and a recovery with only retained restrictions all produce and publish a valid `unknown` or known boundary-only bucket as their evidence requires. No branch skips publication for an empty mark set. `unknown` stays absorbing across repair, restore, recovery and later attempts to add marks; no new credit or receipt follows. A backup or recovered write without the restriction cannot replace the publication obligation or release its service-cursor hold.
  - *Repeated deletion and recovery:* a bucket has retained delete D1. Settlement S becomes final after D1, is credited, and its mark is then removed by delete D2. App W retains D2 but never saw S's mark. A provider loss exposes a recovered write carrying D1 alone. W keeps the union of D1 and D2 and rewrites it when possible. S's evidence clearing D1 does not clear D2: W credits nothing and sends no receipt, even if its user confirms, the recovered write is otherwise valid, or a backup containing only D1 is restored over W's retained state. A distinct settlement whose verified evidence clears both may be credited, its mark written, with both boundaries kept.
  - *Complete restoration and its absence:* W holds the authenticated complete predecessor of D and proves an applied put-back preserves every mark and retained boundary of it and of W. W may resolve that pending D without adding a boundary and credit a genuinely uncredited settlement that predates D, subject to every previously retained boundary. Reader J holds only part of that predecessor; a current rewrite containing all of J's seen marks is insufficient proof. J retains D and applies the evidence rule. Neither reader removes D once it appears in a retained boundary. Equal versions of uploaded values, a superseded delete stub and a user confirmation never substitute for the predecessor proof.
  - *Concurrent and repeated merges:* apps retain different unresolved deletes of one bucket, including D2 and D3 while a current write still carries D1. Put-backs, conflict retries, repairs and uploads in every order produce the canonical union of D1, D2 and D3; repeated merges change nothing. A settlement must clear all three, even if it clears the current write's hash. A later delete adds its own hash; no hash replaces another by byte order, arrival order or record version.
  - *Saturation and persistence:* two individually valid known lists of at most 128 hashes whose union has 129 distinct hashes merge to `unknown`, even beside all 9,000 marks. No hash is truncated and no overbound known value is written. Every merge of `unknown` with an absent or known boundary remains `unknown`, in either order and across repeated writes, restart, restore and provider loss. Evidence after every individually known delete and every user confirmation still permit no new credit or receipt; seen marks and own receipts still show prior credits. The host reports the unknown boundary. Malformed, empty, duplicate, unordered and overbound known lists are invalid; a readable malformed boundary retains `unknown` rather than enabling a by-hand credit.

**Not covered yet:** a real phone with APNs or FCM, the Pubky staging Homeserver, a real network (the profiles below are simulated), persistent storage on disk, per-app Pubky grants (apps write through their identity's session), and histories of days or weeks.

**Tests** (counts as of 2026-11-08; update them here when the suites change):

- The reference prototype's suite reports 681 passing tests, 0 failing and 3 ignored (the scale run, the soak and the vector generator, run on demand). 4 of the 681, the end-to-end tests, need PostgreSQL for the local Pubky testnet; in the run reported here it was available, so all 681 ran, but without it those 4 print `SKIPPED` and still report a pass.
- With `PAYKIT_E2E_REQUIRED=1`, a missing database makes those 4 tests fail instead. CI's test job sets it.
- The independent implementation has 20 vector tests of its own, one per file of the two sets, separate from the 681; all 20 pass on the vectors it was last run on, before those cases were added.

**Results**

- **Request counts (measured):** requests in sequence per action, in a release build: send 1, tap Pay 1, receive 2, first contact 5 (the prototype runs first contact's steps one after another; Section 14 needs two rounds). The counts are the same under every network profile, and they are what Section 14's targets count.
- **Timings on localhost (measured):** every action's warm p95 at most 25 ms.
- **Timings on a simulated network (with retries):** in release, three seeds per profile (2026-11-04), given as the median of the three runs' p95 with the highest in brackets: tap Pay 29 ms (30 ms) on home Wi-Fi (20 ms round trip, no loss) and 106 ms (107 ms) on mobile 4G (80 ms, 1% loss); 331 ms on poor mobile (250 ms, 3% loss), from one earlier run. On mobile 4G, over 20 warm cycles and 30 samples for first contact and cold start, the user's own actions' p95 were: first contact 1,377 ms (1,397 ms), receive 192 ms (1,158 ms), send 114 ms (1,075 ms), tap Pay 106 ms (107 ms) and cold start 204 ms (255 ms). A run's p95 is its second slowest sample, so two samples that hit a loss put it near 1.1 s, hence the higher brackets. The background steps after paying took 306 ms (1,274 ms) and the payee's processing of acceptance and proof 412 ms (1,358 ms). Paying a public endpoint without a fresh `identity.json` took 853 ms (1,329 ms) until money could move; 1 of the 60 such payments on 4G went ahead before the payee's switch answered, flagged as unconfirmed (Pubky Switch spec, Section 4.3). Every action succeeded, through retries of the identical request.
- **Delivery to an open long-poll, simulated mobile 4G:** p95 109 ms over 30 samples, against Section 14's target of under 1 s in the foreground. An earlier run reported 2,990 ms, the slowest of only 10 samples, in which the simulation noticed a lost request only 1 s after the long-poll's wait, later than TCP would retransmit it. It is reported apart from the actions above.
- **Scale (one identity for 10 minutes):** 10,000 contacts; 1,000 inbound messages per minute; no refusals. After the performance hardening, the service's CPU stays flat at about 6% of a core and the end-to-end p95 at 9 ms.
- **Soak (one switch for 75 minutes):** 1,000 identities with 2,500 apps, about 18,400 payments, including requests paid from two wallets at once, Allowance payments and payments without a request, with receive key rotations and apps going offline throughout. Every invariant held: no obligation paid twice, every outbox empty, every journal entry closed, every paid request SETTLED, and no inbox entry, open attempt or live claim left.
- **Correctness:** no duplicate payment outside Section 13's exclusions, and no stuck state without a shown way to resolve it.
- **What it changed:** building the prototype produced 175 changes, listed with the prototype: the design ones are made in this spec, and the wire details are settled in `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md`. The independent implementation found 129 gaps and 28 mismatches in the documents, all settled.

**Limits of these results**

- The service keeps its state in memory, and the apps save into an in-memory `Storage`, so no durable commit or fsync cost is included. Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.5's durability rule is therefore stated, not exercised; the prototype models only the key's mark: a restore fails stop, or models a non-compliant operator that serves on.
- The network is simulated: delay, jitter and loss per request, without bandwidth, TCP retransmission or connection setup.
- There is no real phone, APNs or FCM, and no Pubky staging Homeserver.
- The end-to-end latency test enforces the latency criterion below on the Wi-Fi and 4G profiles, with 30 samples for first contact, cold start and long-poll delivery, and checks that every action completes and that payments are made once; the poor-mobile profile is reported only. It needs PostgreSQL.
- These are prototype timings, not production measurements. Section 14 gives the targets.
- The scale run and the soak ran on an earlier version of the prototype and weren't run again. The end-to-end latency test ran again in release on 2026-11-04, on the Wi-Fi and 4G profiles with three seeds each: the request counts are the same; the poor-mobile timings are older. Since then it ran again in a debug build: the request counts are the same, and the timings above weren't measured again.

**Kill criteria**

- **Fail:**
  - warm p95 latency above 2 s for a user's own actions, on a mobile 4G profile (80 ms round trip, 1% loss);
  - any duplicate payment outside the exclusions of Section 13;
  - any stuck state that isn't shown to the user with a way to resolve it;
  - iOS push p95 above 10 s.
- **Pass:** a paused "may be paid" state that is shown, with a way to resolve it.
- **Network profile:** latency criteria are judged on a stated network profile. The user's own actions are first contact, send, receive, tap Pay and a cold start (Section 14). Background processing, such as the steps after paying and a payee handling an acceptance and a proof, is reported separately, and so is delivery to an open long-poll, which Section 14's "Recipient sees it" target covers. Under the prototype's poor-mobile profile the user's own actions stayed under 1.5 s at p95 except a cold start at 2.3 s, and the payee's background processing exceeded 2 s; the criterion is judged on mobile 4G.
- **Results:** on the simulated mobile 4G profile the latency criterion passes in every run, with the user's own actions under 1.5 s at p95 (first contact the slowest, at 1.40 s at most over three seeds); the duplicate payment and stuck state criteria pass. These hold for the prototype, within the limits above. iOS push p95 isn't measured yet; it needs a real phone (Before freeze).

**Before freeze:**

- **Still open:**
  - a last check of the two retention rules (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 6.3 and 8.3);
  - team approval of this spec, including the defaults of the open questions, and the decisions that gate the freeze: the labelled selling scopes and the signer hand-over decided in Section 17 (decisions 22 and 24);
  - the same measurements against the Pubky staging Homeserver, with storage on disk, and on real phones and real networks, including iOS push p95 with APNs;
  - persistent storage in a production switch;
  - an independent human crypto review.
- **Done,** each implemented and tested in the prototype: schemas and safe encodings in the defined portions of `specs/v0.2/wire-format.md`, excluding the incomplete protected-record contract and the rules Section 18 lists as added since; the certificate check order and renewal (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 3.2 and 3.4); the retention and quota table (Pubky Switch spec, Section 6.3), with chain retention that follows cursors rather than reads, and batch IDs kept with their chain stubs and compared by apps within 30 days of the chain (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 8.3 and 13.5); the resumable provider export, which carries every stub the old service keeps (Pubky Switch spec, Section 12.5); reclaiming deleted record keys (Pubky Switch spec, Sections 6.3 and 10.3); the conformance tests; and the shared test vectors, Paykit's generated by `paykit-lib`, the Switch set's [`export.json`](https://github.com/pubky/pubky-switch/blob/5f3209aae28d1b538a31c8ce43506b39df828b61/spec/vectors/export.json) now a complete export that activates. The reference prototype's history describes each one in full.

**Production plan.** Work for production, not for the prototype. Each item needs an owner before the SDK and the switch ship.

- **Secrets:** secret types that aren't `Copy`, zeroize on drop, print nothing in `Debug` (already so in the prototype) and derive no `Serialize`; keys in platform keystores (Pubky Switch spec, Section 3.1); deletion by crypto-shredding; and a test that a deleted key's bytes are gone from every file the app persists.
- **Supply chain:** a required cargo-deny job (advisories with expiring ignores, bans on duplicate crypto crates, sources, licenses) with scheduled runs and a Dependabot entry for cargo; the TLS crates kept current; the Android build's linker settings reviewed; one version of each direct crypto crate, one entry point for randomness and a getrandom backend per target; no patched or vendored pubky in any shipped crate, with a `cargo package` check in CI and a scoped conditional-write API asked of Pubky upstream.
- **A client and server crate split:** the mobile bindings must not link a web server, and the client gets TLS through an explicit feature, not through feature unification with pubky's dependencies.
- **A formal model:** state the at-most-once invariant and its assumptions in Section 13 (deadlines derived exactly, a restored journal never read as "not started", transitions durable before they are signed, service time that never goes back, `failed` only on authoritative evidence); model claims, the journal and recovery in Quint or TLA+; and export the model's traces as conformance tests.
- **Conformance:** freeze the vectors, so that a regenerated diff counts as a wire change; add boundary vectors for rules pinned only by text; build a second implementation on a crypto stack other than Rust's; run Wycheproof and the Ed25519 speccheck cases; fuzz the prototype against the independent implementation; and build a rejection corpus by mutating valid vectors, first the security bindings, the UUID form and the integer bound, then the rest, negative `stale_key` cases included. Releases are gated on it.
- **Fail-closed code:** no `unwrap`, `expect`, indexing or unchecked arithmetic in the protocol crates, enforced by Clippy, and fuzz targets for the parsers, times and Billing Period arithmetic.
- **Faults against real parts:** crash and failover tests against real storage, an approval end to end through Pubky auth, per-app grants enforced by a testnet Homeserver, and an on-device test of the iOS notification extension, with its choice of entry to show, a feed view of entries only and a fixed generic relay payload.
- **Bindings:** only the Rust core parses and verifies signed bodies; Swift and Kotlin never parse them again. CI builds every binding (`build.sh all`) and checks the bindings' diff.
- **The external crypto audit's scope:** the subset of hpke 0.14 that Paykit uses, strict Ed25519 verification with ed25519-dalek 3 and curve25519-dalek 5, and the approval's companion-claim channel.
- **CI:** the test job runs a PostgreSQL 16 service and sets `PAYKIT_E2E_REQUIRED=1` (2026-11-03), so the end-to-end tests run there and fail rather than skip if the database is missing.

**Then:**

1. Freeze the spec.
2. Make the shared test vectors mandatory for every release.
3. SDK latency and success metrics from day one.
